===Original===
> Joshua fit d’ battle ob Jericho

 And d’ walls come tumblin’ down﻿—

===Chinese===
> Joshua fit d’ battle ob Jericho（约书亚打了耶利哥那一仗）

 And d’ walls come tumblin’ down﻿—（城墙便轰然倒塌了）

===Original===
For Glendora﻿—

> May her laugh be silver,

 like her hair

===Chinese===
献给格伦多拉（Glendora）——

> 愿她的笑声如银，

 一如她的头发。

## Jericho / 耶利哥

### I / 一

===Original===
Despite the objections of the dickties, who prefer to ignore the existence of so-called rats, it is of interest to consider Henry Patmore’s Pool Parlor on Fifth Avenue in New York.

The truth about Fifth Avenue has only half been told, that it harbors an aristocracy of residence already yielding to an aristocracy of commerce. Has any New Yorker confessed to the rest﻿—that when aristocratic Fifth Avenue crosses 110th Street, leaving Central Park behind, it leaves its aristocracy behind as well? Here are bargain-stores, babble, and kids, dinginess, odors, thick speech. Fallen from splendor and doubtless ashamed, the Avenue burrows into the ground﻿—plunges beneath a park which hides it from 116th to 125th Street. Here it emerges moving uncertainly northward a few more blocks; and now﻿—irony of ironies﻿—finds itself in Negro Harlem.

You can see the Avenue change expression﻿—blankness, horror, conviction. You can almost see it wag its head in self-commiseration. Not just because this is Harlem﻿—there are proud streets in Harlem: Seventh Avenue of a Sunday afternoon, Strivers’ Row, and The Hill. Fifth Avenue’s shame lies in having missed these so-called dickty sections, in having poked its head out into the dark kingdom’s backwoods. A city jungle this, if ever there was one, peopled largely by untamed creatures that live and die for the moment only. Accordingly, here strides melodrama, naked and unashamed.

Patmore’s Pool Parlor occupied the remodeled ground floor of a once elegant apartment-house: two long low adjacent rooms, with a smaller one in the rear. You could enter either of the larger two from the street, and a doorway joined them within. There were no pretenses about these two rooms: one was a pool room, its stolid, green-covered tables extending from front to back in a long squat row; the other was a saloon, with a mahogany bar counter, a great wall mirror, a shining foot rail and brass spittoons. In the saloon you could get any drink you had courage and cash enough to order; in the pool room you could play for any stake and use any language you had the ingenuity to devise. The third room was off the pool room and behind the saloon; this gave itself over to that triad of swift exchange, poker, blackjack, and dice.

Such was Pat’s standing in the community that you might at any time find in this little rear room a policeman sitting in a card game, his coat on the back of his chair, his cap on the back of his head. For men, Pat’s was supremely the neighborhood’s social center, where you met real regular guys and rubbed elbows with authority. Henry Patmore was no piker, no sir, not by a damn sight.

---

===Chinese===
尽管那些体面黑人（dickty）对此大不以为然——他们宁可装作世上根本没有所谓「穷哥儿」（rat）这号人——纽约第五大道上亨利·帕特莫尔（Henry Patmore）开的那家台球厅，还是值得一提的。

关于第五大道，世人只说出了半截真相：这地方养着一派居所的贵族气，而这气派已然要让位给买卖的贵族气。可有哪个纽约人肯向大家伙儿招认——气派十足的第五大道在跨过110街、把中央公园甩在身后之后，也把自家的气派一并甩在了身后？这里满眼是廉价铺子、吵嚷和野孩子，灰头土脸，气味冲鼻，口音黏稠。从显赫里跌落下来，想必羞愧难当，大道一头钻进地下——潜到一座公园底下躲着，从116街一直藏到125街。再冒头时，它犹犹豫豫又朝北挪了几条街；这下——讽刺里的讽刺——它发现自己竟到了黑人的哈莱姆。

你能眼看着这条大道变脸色——先是茫然，继而惊骇，末了是铁了心的确信。你几乎能看见它直摇头，顾影自怜。倒不全因为这里是哈莱姆——哈莱姆也有自家拿得出手的傲街：礼拜天下午的第七大道、奋进者排屋（Strivers' Row），还有山岗区（The Hill）。第五大道的耻辱在于一步迈岔，没够着这些所谓的体面地段，倒把脑袋探进了这黑暗王国的穷乡僻壤。要说都市丛林，这里便是不折不扣的一座，住的大半是些没驯化过的生灵，活只活眼前，死也只死当下。于是情节剧就在这儿迈开大步，赤条条来去，不知羞耻为何物。

帕特莫尔台球厅占着一幢昔日体面的公寓楼翻修过的底层：两间相连的长而低的大屋，后头还带一小间。大的两间各自临街开门，屋里另有门洞相通。这两间屋子毫不装模作样：一间是台球室，绿呢铺面的笨重球台从前到后排成矮趴趴的一长列；另一间是酒馆，配着红木吧台、一面顶大的墙镜、锃亮的踏脚横杆，还有黄铜痰盂。在酒馆里，只要胆量和现钱够得着，点什么酒都有；在台球室里，赌多大随你，什么话骂得出口、编得出巧，也随你。第三间屋子开在台球室旁边、酒馆后头，专营那门出手要快的三合一营生：扑克、二十一点，加骰子。

帕特在这一带的身面有多大？大到随便什么时候，你都可能在后头那间小屋里撞见一个警察正坐在牌局上，外套搭在椅背上，帽子扣在后脑勺上。对男人们说来，帕特这儿是本地头一份的社交中心，在这儿既能结识真正够意思的汉子，又能跟官面上的人勾肩搭背。亨利·帕特莫尔可不是小家子气的人，绝没有的事儿，差得远着呢。

---

===Original===
In Patmore’s the discussion concerned a possible riot in Harlem, a popular topic among these men who loved battle.

Jinx Jenkins and Bubber Brown led the argument on opposite sides, reinforced by continuous expressions of vague but hearty agreement from their partisans:

“Tell ’im ’bout it!”

“That’s the time, papa!”

“There now﻿—shake that one off yo’ butt!”

===Chinese===
这天在帕特莫尔店里，大伙儿争论的是哈莱姆兴许要闹乱子的事——这话题在一帮嗜斗的汉子中间向来抢手。

金克斯·詹金斯（Jinx Jenkins）和布伯·布朗（Bubber Brown）各执一端领头吵，两边的拥趸不断送上含糊却热烈的声援：

「跟他说个明白！」

「这就对了，老哥！」

「得，这一招你屁股上可抖落不掉喽！」

===Original===
Jinx and Bubber worked at the same job every day, moving furniture. At this they got along tolerably, but after hours they were chronic enemies and were absolutely unable to agree upon anything.

Jinx was thin and elongated, habitually stooped in bearing, lean and sinewy, with freckled skin of a slick deep yellow and a chronically querulous voice.

“Fays got better sense,” said he. “Never will be no riot no mo’ ’round hyeh.”

Bubber was as different from Jinx as any man could be, short, round and bulging, with a complexion bordering on the invisible.

“ ’Tain’t due to be ’round hyeh,” he corrected. “It’s way over Court Avenue way. Darkey’s gonna move in there tomorrer and fays jes’ ain’t gon’ stand fo’ it.” Bubber spoke with a loose-lipped lisp, perfected by the absence of upper incisors.

===Chinese===
金克斯和布伯天天干同一份活——搬运家具。干起活来两人还过得去，一下了工，便成了宿敌，任什么小事也拧不到一块儿。

金克斯细长一条，平日弓背塌腰，精瘦有筋，皮肉是油亮的深黄色、撒着雀斑，嗓音里常年带着股抱怨劲儿。

「白佬们脑子清楚着呢，」他说，「这一带往后再也闹不起乱子喽。」

布伯跟金克斯差得不能再远：矮、圆、浑身鼓胀，那肤色黑得快要看不见。

「要闹也不在这一带闹，」他纠正道，「是在老远老远的法院大道（Court Avenue）那边。明天有个黑人要搬进那地方，白佬们死也咽不下这口气。」布伯说起话来咬舌漏风——上门牙一掉，这口音便练到家了。

===Original===
“Who he?” Jinx inquired.

“Some lawyer ’n other name’ Merrit.”

“The one got Pat in that mess with d’ government?”

“Nobody else,” said Bubber.

“Well ef he’s a lawyer he sho’ mus’ know what he’s doin’.”

“Don’ matter what he is,” argued Bubber. “Ef he move in that neighborhood, fays’ll start sump’m sho’﻿—and sho’ as they start it, d’ boogies’ll finish it. Won’t make no difference ’bout this Merrit man﻿—he’ll jes’ be d’ excuse﻿—Man, you know that. Every sence d’ war, d’ boogies is had guns and ammunition they stole from d’ army, and they jes’ dyin’ fo’ a chance to try ’em out. I know where they’s two machine guns myself, and they mus’ be a hund’ed mo’ in Harlem.”

===Chinese===
「他是谁？」金克斯问。

「一个律师来着，姓梅里特（Merrit）。」

「就是把帕特坑进跟政府那桩麻烦里的那位？」

「不是他还是谁，」布伯说。

「那他要真是个律师，准知道自己是在干啥。」

「他是干啥的不打紧，」布伯辩道，「他只要搬进那片街坊，白佬们笃定要先动手——他们一开头，黑哥们笃定来收尾。这位梅里特先生是龙是凤都不打紧——他就是根引线——老兄，这你清楚。自打大战那阵子起，黑哥们手里就攥着从军队偷来的枪支弹药，一个个巴望着有机会开开荤。我自个儿就知道哪儿撂着两挺机关枪，全哈莱姆少说还得再有一百挺。」

===Original===
“Yea,” said Jinx. “I’ve heard ’bout that, too. But I don’t think no shine’s got no business bustin’ into no fay neighborhood.”

“He got business bustin’ in any place he want to go. Only way for him to git anywhere is to bust in﻿—ain’ nobody gon’ *invite* him in.”

“Aw, man, whut you talkin’ ’bout? Hyeh’s a dickty tryin’ his damnedest to be fay﻿—like all d’ other dickties. When they git in hot water they all come cryin’ to you and me fo’ help.”

“And they git help, what I mean. Any time dickties start fightin’, d’ rest of us start fightin’ too. Got to. Dickties can’t fight.”

“Jes’ ’cause they can’t fight ain’ no reason how come we got to fight fo’ em.”

===Chinese===
「可不，」金克斯说，「这话我也听说了。可我看，随便哪个黑人也不该硬往白人街里闯。」

「他想去哪儿，哪儿就闯得。黑人想挣出个名堂，只有硬闯一条路——反正没有人会*请*他进去。」

「嗐，老兄，你说什么呢？这不过是个体面人拼了命要当白佬——天下的体面人都这德行。惹了祸，一个个又哭着喊着来找你我求救。」

「救是有人救的，我就是这个意思。体面人什么时候开了打，咱们余下的人也得跟着打。不打不行。体面人不会打架。」

「他们不会打架，不等于就轮到咱们替他们打。」

===Original===
“ ’Tain’ nothin’ else. Fays don’ see no difference ’tween dickty shines and any other kind o’ shines. One jig in danger is ev’y jig in danger. They’d lick them and come on down on *us*. Then we’d have to fight anyhow. What’s use o’ waitin’?”

“Damn if you’d ever go out o’ yo’ way to fight f’ no dickties,” Jinx taunted.

“Don’ know﻿—I might,” Bubber said.

“Huh!” discredited Jinx. “You wouldn’ go out o’ yo’ way to fight f’ yo’ own damn self﻿—and you far from a dickty.”

“Right,” cheerfully agreed Bubber. “I’m far from a dickty, no lie. But I ain’ so far from a rat.” Jinx missed the meaning of this, so Bubber gilded up close to him and drove it home. “Fact I’m right next to one.”

===Chinese===
「没二话。在白佬眼里，体面的黑人和别的黑人半点分别没有。一个黑人遭了殃，就是个个黑人遭了殃。他们撂倒那几个，掉头就得来收拾*咱们*。横竖是一打，等个什么劲？」

「鬼才信你会绕这个远路替体面人打架，」金克斯激他。

「说不准——兴许会，」布伯说。

「哼！」金克斯一脸的不信。「你连替你自己这倒霉身子都懒得绕一步打架——再说你离体面人也差着十万八千里呢。」

「没错，」布伯痛快地认账，「我离体面人差着十万八千里，不掺假。可我离穷哥儿不远。」金克斯没咂摸出这话的味儿，布伯便贴到他跟前，一锤定音：「其实我就紧挨着一个穷哥儿。」

===Original===
Encircling grins improved Jinx’s understanding. “Next to nuthin’!” exploded he, giving the other a rough push.

“Next to nuthin’, then,” acquiesced Bubber, caroming off. “You know what you is lots better’n I do.” Whereupon he did a triumphant little buck and wing step, which ended in a single loud, dust-raising stamp. Dry dust and drier laughter floated irritatingly into Jinx’s face. Jinx was long and limber but his restraint was short and brittle. Derision snapped it in two.

“So’s yo’ whole damn family nuthin’!” he glowered, heedless of the disproportion between the trivial provocation and so violent a reaction. For it is the gravest of insults, this so-called “slipping in the dozens.” To disparage a man himself is one thing; to disparage his family is another. “Slipping” is a challenge holding all the potentialities of battle. The present example of it brought Bubber up short and promptly withdrew the bystanders’ attention from their gin.

The bystanders began “agitatin’ ”﻿—uttering comments deliberately intended to urge the two into action. The agitators concealed their grins far up their sleeves, presenting countenances grave with apprehension and speaking in tones resigned to the inevitability of battle.

===Chinese===
四周一圈咧开的笑脸，帮金克斯开了窍。「挨着个屁！」他炸了，狠狠搡开对方。

「挨着个屁就挨着个屁，」布伯就势弹开，坦然认输，「你是个啥，你自己心里比我有数得多。」说完他得意洋洋来了一段踢踏舞，末了一记跺脚，「咚」地跺起一股干土。干土和更干的哄笑直往金克斯脸上飘，飘得人火起。金克斯的身条又长又韧，忍性却又短又脆，这一嘲笑，把它「咔」地撅成了两截。

「你全家也屁都不是！」他恶狠狠道，全然不顾这芝麻大的由头配不上这么大的火气。要知道，这所谓的「骂娘游戏」（slipping in the dozens）是天底下顶顶犯忌的侮辱。糟践一个人是一回事，糟践他的家人是另一回事。「骂娘」就是下战书，字字都埋着开打的引线。眼下这一句骂得布伯猛地煞住脚，也立时把看客们的心思从各自的杜松子酒上勾了过来。

看客们开始「煽风」——你一言我一语，存心拱两位动起手来。煽风的人们把笑意深深掖进袖筒，脸上挂满忧心如焚的凝重，话音里透着「这一架是逃不掉喽」的泰然。

===Original===
“Uh-uh! Sho’ mus’ know each other well!”

“Wha’ I come fum, dey fights fo’ less ’n dat.”

“Ef y’ can’t stand kiddin’, don’ kid, I say.”

“I don’ b’lieve he’s gon’ hit ’im, though.”

“I know what *I’d* do ’f anybody said that ’bout *my* family.”

===Chinese===
「啧啧！这俩交情可不浅！」

「俺老家那儿，为比这小的茬儿都开打。」

「我说，经不起逗，就别逗人。」

「不过我看他未必敢真动手。」

「换了谁敢那么讲*我*家里人，我知道*我*会怎么收拾他。」

===Original===
As a matter of fact, the habitual dissension between these two was the symptom of a deep affection which neither, on question, would have admitted. Neither Jinx and Bubber nor any of their associates had ever heard of Damon and Pythias, and frank regard between two men would have been considered questionable to say the least. Their fellows would neither have understood nor tolerated it; would have killed it by derisions, conjectures, suggestions, comments banishing the association to some realm beyond normal manhood. Accordingly their own expression of this affection had to take an ironic turn. They themselves must deride it first, must hide their mutual inclination in a garment of constant ridicule and contention, the irritation of which rose into their consciousness as hostility. Words and gestures which in a different order of life would have required no suppression became with them necessarily inverted, found issue only by assuming a precisely opposite aspect, concealed a profound attachment by exposing an extravagant enmity. And this was a distortion of behavior so completely imposed upon them by their traditions and society that even they themselves did not know they were masquerading.

Bubber, his round face gone ominously blank, drew slowly closer to Jinx, who, face thrust forward a little and scowling, stood with his back to the bar counter, on which both elbows rested.

“Mean﻿—*my* family?” inquired Bubber.

===Chinese===
其实呢，这两人经年累月的别扭，不过是一副药引，引的是底下那份深情厚谊——只是这份情谊，你要当面问起来，两人谁也死不认账。金克斯和布伯，连他们那一帮伙伴，谁也没听说过达蒙和皮西厄斯（Damon and Pythias）；两个大男人之间敞敞亮亮的敬重，搁他们那儿，往轻里说也要叫人起疑。同伴们既不懂它，也容不下它，只会拿嘲笑、猜度、影射、闲话把它掐死，把这交情轰到正常男子汉之外的什么地界去。于是这份情谊要露头，就只有走反路。他们得自己抢先讥笑它，把彼此的好感裹进一件笑骂与抬杠连成的外衣，外衣磨出的硌痒升到心上，就成了敌意。那些换一种活法本不必压着的言语和举动，到了他们身上只得翻个面，非装出一副恰恰相反的嘴脸才出得了口，非亮出一场夸张的仇怨才罩得住一份深厚的情分。而这一种行为上的扭曲，又是他们的老规矩和社会整个儿扣在他们头上的，扣得连他们自己都不知道自己在做戏。

布伯那张圆脸阴沉沉地板成一片空白，慢慢朝金克斯挪近。金克斯把脸微微探前，眉头拧着，背靠吧台站着，两条胳膊肘都拄在台面上。

「你说的是——*我*家里人？」布伯问。

===Original===
Jinx dared not recant. “All the way back to the apes,” he assured him “﻿—and that ain’t so awful far back.”

“The apes in yo’ family is still livin’,” said Bubber, “but they’s go’n’ be one daid in a minute.”

“Stay where you at, you little black balloon, or I’ll stick a pin in you, you hear?”

By this time Bubber was almost within range and an initial blow was imminent. Absorbed in the impending clash, no one had noticed the arrival of a newcomer. But now this newcomer spoke and his words, soft and low though they were, commanded immediate attention.

“Winner belongs to me.”

===Chinese===
金克斯不敢改口。「从你祖宗一直数到猿猴，」他一口咬定，「——那也没多远。」

「你家的猿猴倒还个个活着，」布伯说，「不过眼瞅着就要死一只了。」

「你给我在原地待着，小黑气球，不然我拿别针扎爆你，听见没？」

这时布伯眼看就要够着拳脚，头一记眼看就要落下。众人全副心神都吊在这一场好戏上，谁也没留神进来了个生人。偏这生人开了腔，话虽又轻又低，却立时镇住了全场。

「赢家归我。」

===Original===
Everybody looked﻿—spectators holding their drinks, Bubber with his blank black face, Jinx with his murderous scowl. They saw a man at one end of the bar counter, one foot raised upon the brass rail, one elbow resting on the mahogany ledge; a young man so tall that, though he bent forward from the hips in a posture of easy nonchalance, he could still see over every intervening head between himself and the two opponents, and yet so broad that his height was not of itself noticeable; a supremely tranquil young Titan, with a face of bronze, hard, metallic, lustrous, profoundly serene. He repeated his remark in paraphrase:

“I am askin’ fo’ the winner. I am very humbly requestin’ a share in his hind-parts.”

It was apparent that the bristling antagonists bristled no longer, had limply lost interest in their quarrel.

“Aw, man,” mumbled Jinx, “what you talkin’ ’bout?”

“You know what I’m talkin’ ’bout you freckle-face giraffe, and so does ’at baby hippopotamus in front of you. We got that Court Avenue job in the mornin’, and if I got to break in one rooky on it, I might as well break in two.” The voice, too, was like bronze, heavy, rich in tone, uncompromisingly solid, with a surface shadowy and smooth as velvet save for an occasional ironic glint.

===Chinese===
人人都望过去——看客们端着酒，布伯黑着脸一片木然，金克斯拧着满脸杀气。只见吧台一端立着一个人，一只脚踩在铜杆上，一只胳膊肘支在红木台沿：好一个年轻人，高到这步田地——虽说打胯上就懒洋洋地俯着身、一副闲散姿态，隔着自家和那两位之间的一颗颗脑袋仍旧一览无余；却又宽厚壮实，叫人单看竟显不出他高来。好一位安之若素的年轻泰坦，一张青铜色面孔，坚硬、泛着金属光、沉静极了。他把方才那话换个说法又交代一遍：

「俺要的是那个赢的。俺毕恭毕敬地恳请，分他屁股上的一份。」

一眼便看得出，两位炸了毛的对手已然不炸了，蔫头耷脑地对这场争吵失了兴致。

「嗐，老兄，」金克斯咕哝，「你说什么呢？」

「你知道我说什么，你个雀斑脸长颈鹿——你前头那头小河马也知道。明儿一早咱们有法院大道的活，反正我得往里带一个生手，索性带上俩。」那嗓音也如青铜，沉，醇，瓷实得没商量，表面幽暗平滑如天鹅绒，只是偶尔闪过一星讥诮的光。

===Original===
“This boogy,” explained Bubber, “thinks he’s bad. Come slippin’ me ’bout my family. He knows I don’t play nuthin’ like that.”

“Needn’t git uppity ’bout it,” mumbled Jinx sullenly.

“Ain’ gittin’ uppity. Jes’ natchly don’ like it, thass all. Keep yo’ thick lips off my family ef y’ know what’s good fo’ y’.”

He who had interrupted queried blandly, “Ain’t there gonna be no fight?”

Jinx said to Bubber﻿—“Aw go ’haid, drabble-tail. Ain’ nobody studyin’ yo’ family.”

And this questionable apology Bubber chose to accept. “Oh,” said he. “Oh﻿—aw right, then. Thass different.”

===Chinese===
「这黑哥们，」布伯解释道，「自认为了不得。跑来骂我的家里人。他知道我从不来这一套。」

「这事就别翘尾巴了，」金克斯沉着脸咕哝。

「谁翘尾巴了。天生不爱这口，就这样。识相就把你那厚嘴唇从我家人身上挪开。」

打岔的那位淡淡地问：「这架是不打了？」

金克斯对布伯道：「得了吧，脏尾巴。没人惦记你家里人。」

这句成色可疑的赔罪，布伯决定收下。「哦，」他说，「哦——那敢情好。这就两说了。」

===Original===
The atmosphere cleared, attention returned to gin and jest, and Bubber approached the giant, who now was grinning.

“Certainly am sorry th’ ain’ go’n’ be no hostilities,” sighed the latter. “Been wantin’ to spank yo’ little black bottom ev’y sence you broke that rope this mornin’.”

“Aw go ’haid, Shine. That boogy’s shoutin’ ’cause you was hyeh to protect ’im. I’m go’n’ ketch ’im one these days when you ain’ ’round, and I’m go’n’ turn ’im ev’y way but loose.”

===Chinese===
气氛云开雾散，众人的心思重新回到杜松子酒和玩笑上。布伯凑到那大个子跟前，大个子这会儿正咧着嘴呢。

「打不成真是可惜，」大个子叹道，「打今儿早上你崩断那根绳子起，我就一直想揍你那小黑屁股。」

「去你的，夏因（Shine）。这黑哥们敢咋呼，还不是仗着你在这儿给他撑腰。哪天你不在跟前，叫我逮着他，非把他拧得七荤八素不撒手。」

===Original===
“Don’t let ’im surprise y’. He kin wrastle the hell out of a piano.”

“Piano don’t fight back.”

“Don’t it? Well﻿—neither will you if he get the same hold on y’.”

“Humph. Who the hell’s scared o’ that﻿—freckle-face giraffe?”

===Chinese===
「别叫他偷袭着你。他跟钢琴较起劲来可不是闹着玩的。」

「钢琴不会还手。」

「不会吗？哼——他要照样一把揪住你，你也一样还不了手。」

「哼。谁他妈怕那玩意儿——雀斑脸长颈鹿？」

### II / 二

===Original===
Patmore, the proprietor, appeared, a large, powerful man with a broad, hard face, a bright display of gold teeth, and the complexion of a guinea hen’s egg. He wore a loose brown suit, of which the coat was large and boxy and the ample trousers sharply creased but so long that they broke about his ankles in cubistic planes and angles. Smoke and the caustic vapors of rum had rendered his voice rough and husky, and when he spoke you had an irresistible impulse to clear your throat.

Pat addressed Bubber. “You and Long-Boy still at it, huh?”

“Aw﻿—’at string-bean’s crazy. I’m gon’ snap ’im in two and string ’im one these times.”

“Know what I’m go’n’ do with you two?”

“Whut?”

===Chinese===
帕特莫尔——店主人出场了：高大魁伟的一条汉子，阔脸硬朗，一口金牙明晃晃，肤色活像几内亚母鸡下的蛋。他穿一身宽松的棕色西装，上衣又大又方，肥大的裤子熨得裤线笔挺，只是裤脚太长，在脚踝上堆出一个个立体的方块和棱角。烟气和朗姆酒的苛辣熏坏了他的嗓子，说起话来沙哑粗粝，听的人喉咙也不由自主地想清一清。

帕特冲布伯开口：「你跟长条儿还没闹完呢？」

「嗐——那根豆芽菜疯着呢。赶哪一回，我非把他掰成两截，再拿线穿成串儿。」

「知道我打算怎么处置你们俩吗？」

「咋？」

===Original===
“See that door over there?”

“Yea.”

“That’s the cellar door, see? Next time y’all start anything in hyeh, I’m go’n’ send the two of you down there and let you settle it once and for all. Best man come out﻿—other one drug out. See?”

“Any rats down there?”

“Yea﻿—and y’all’ll make two more.”

“Well,” grinned Bubber, “when I walk out, them rats’ll have some bones to gnaw on anyhow,” and he moved off toward the pool room.

===Chinese===
「瞧见那边那扇门没有？」

「瞧见了。」

「那是下地窖的门，懂吧？下回你们俩再在这儿动手，我就把你们俩都送下去，让你们把这账一次算清。谁赢谁走出来——另一个拖出来。明白？」

「底下耗子多不多？」

「有——你们俩一下去，就又添俩。」

「嘿，」布伯咧嘴一笑，「等我走出来，那些耗子好歹有骨头啃了。」说着便往台球室那边蹭去了。

===Original===
Ignoring Pat’s attempt to play the genial host, Shine had already returned to his drink with an indifference hardly short of insult. He now replenished his glass from a pint bottle in his hand, and slipped the bottle into his own hip pocket.

Pat’s green eyes narrowed. “That’ll be only three bucks to you, Shine.”

Shine looked up. “What?”

“Anybody else﻿—four.”

“This,” said Shine, “is *good* licker.”

“ ’Course ’tis. All my licker is good.”

===Chinese===
帕特本想摆出一副殷勤主人的做派，夏因却不领情，早已回去喝他的酒，那份冷淡几乎就是无礼。这时他又从手里那瓶一品脱的小瓶里给自己满上一杯，随手把瓶子揣进了自己后裤兜。

帕特的绿眼睛眯了起来。「算你三块钱，夏因。」

夏因抬起眼。「什么？」

「换了别人——四块。」

「这可是，」夏因道，「*好*酒。」

「那可不。我的酒哪有不好的。」

===Original===
“This ain’ never been yourn﻿—’scription licker.” Shine sampled his glass with an odd mingling of relish and unconcern, the one unmistakably for his drink, the other for his company.

Pat feigned incredulity. “Mean that’s *your* licker?”

“ ’Tain’t my brother’s.”

“Mean﻿—” Pat’s unbelief mounted “﻿—mean﻿—you buy licker somewhere else and bring it in my place to drink?”

Shine tossed off the rest of the glass, set it down on the bar counter, and looked upon Pat, who was almost as tall as himself, with a wearily tolerant smile.

===Chinese===
「这瓶可从来不是你的货——处方酒（'scription licker）。」夏因呷了一口，有滋有味与心不在焉古怪地掺在一起——一样明明白白是给杯中物的，另一样是给眼前这位的。

帕特装出一脸的不敢相信：「你是说，这是*你自个儿*的酒？」

「总不是我哥的。」

「你是说——」帕特的不信步步高升「——你是说——你从别处买了酒，带进我的店里来喝？」

夏因把杯底一口倒尽，杯子往吧台上一放，打量着帕特——帕特个头几乎与他一般高——脸上挂一个懒得计较的笑。

===Original===
“Sho’ takes you a long time to see a thing,” he remarked. “You hear me say it’s ’scription. You ain’t runnin’ no drugstore, are y’? You see me drink it. You ain’t blind, are y’? Yea, I bought it. Yea, I brought it here. Yea, I’m drinkin’ it. Now what the hell ’bout it?”

A smaller man equally “bad,” equally convinced of the necessity of being hard, but aware of physical odds against him, would have said this with sneers and sarcasm, thus bolstering his courage against his handicap. Shine however had never found it necessary to be nasty as well as bad. He had spoken with an air of amusement, and there was but a touch of challenge in his terminal remark.

Pat stood silent a moment. Eventually he said, “Nothin’ ’bout it, big boy. Nothin’. Jes’ askin’ f’ information, that’s all.” And rather too abruptly he walked away.

===Chinese===
「你瞧明白一件事可真得费工夫，」他说。「你听我说了这是处方酒。你又不是开药房的，对吧？你眼看着我喝下去。你又不瞎，对吧？对，是我买的。对，是我带来的。对，我正喝着呢。这他妈又怎么着了？」

换个矮小些的男人，同样「横」，同样笃信做人就得硬，却自知力气上吃亏，说这话时便少不了撇嘴带刺，好给自个儿的短处壮胆。夏因却从来不觉得横之外还非得刻薄。他说这话时一脸的有趣，末尾那句里也不过带了一丝挑衅。

帕特闷站了半晌，末了道：「不怎么着，大个子。不怎么着。就是打听打听，没别的。」说罢转身走开，走得未免太急了些。

===Original===
---

Shine stared long into his third glass of ’scription liquor before he lifted it to his lips. Good whiskey is not like gin. Gin makes you forget, good whiskey makes you remember. Perhaps it was at the memories in this, his third glass of good whiskey, that Shine now stared.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===
---

夏因盯着自己第三杯处方酒，看了许久才举到唇边。好威士忌不比杜松子酒。杜松子酒叫人忘事，好威士忌叫人记事。夏因这会儿盯着瞧的，多半就是这第三杯好威士忌里泡着的旧事。……

---

===Original===
A boy, overgrown, bigger by far than his fellow orphan asylumites, so much bigger that they never challenged him to do battle as they frequently challenged the others. As big, almost, as the superintendent, about whom the smallest thing was his pebble of a heart. They were all at work in the truck garden, Shrimpie, Frankfurter, Jellybean, and the others, as well as this overgrown one whose name was then Joshua Jones. They were picking tomatoes, mostly green ones, to be taken to the kitchen and made into “pickalilly.” They were seeing who could pick most in the hour allotted to them for the work.

And Shrimpie, unaware that they were being watched from the window of the nearest cottage, suddenly stopped, staring in surprise and delight at a big, red, prematurely ripe tomato in his hand.

“Y’all kin work fast as you please,” Shrimpie declared. “I’m gon’ stop and eat this hyeh one.”

Three bites out of the luscious thing﻿—and the superintendent’s hand was on Shrimpie’s shoulder. Three cruelly vehement shakes of Shrimpie’s little body﻿—and a hard green tomato sped through space and broke on the super’s cheek.

The red face became redder, the super dropped Shrimpie and turned toward the big boy, enraged. Made for him﻿—dodged another tomato﻿—came on. Grappled, scuffled, slipped, fell, and found the boy astride him. Pounding on his head﻿—pounding﻿—gone quite crazy, pounding. The super was stunned less by the pounding than by the fact that the boy kept doing it. Even after he was shaken off, the boy kept fighting aggressively. Without a rod it wasn’t so easy to tame as overgrown sixteen-year-old devil. When they both let up, it was at least spiritually Joshua Jones’s round.

===Chinese===
一个男孩，个子窜过了头，比孤儿院里一帮同伴高出一大截，高到他们从来不撺掇他打架——别的孩子倒常被撺掇。几乎跟院长一般高大了；而院长这个人，浑身上下最小的一样东西，就是他那颗石子儿般的心。那会儿大伙儿全在菜园里干活，小虾米（Shrimpie）、法兰克福（Frankfurter）、糖豆（Jellybean），还有旁的孩子，连同这个头窜得太高的——他那时候的名字是约书亚·琼斯（Joshua Jones）。他们在摘番茄，多半是青的，好送去厨房做成「酸甜腌菜」。摘一个钟头，看谁摘得多。

小虾米不晓得最近那间小屋的窗户里正有人盯着他们，忽然停下手，又惊又喜地端详手里一只又大又红、熟得太早的番茄。

「你们爱多快就多快，」小虾米宣布，「我可要停下来，把这只给吃了。」

这馋人的东西才咬了三口——院长的手已经搭上了小虾米的肩膀。小虾米的小身板被狠狠抖了三下——一只硬青番茄破空飞去，正砸在院长的腮帮子上开了花。

红脸涨得更红，院长撂下小虾米，憋着怒气朝大男孩转过身来。扑上去——又躲过一只番茄——再扑上来。揪住，扭打，脚下一滑，摔倒，睁眼一看，男孩已经骑在他身上。拳头往他头上砸——一下一下地砸——人完全疯了似的砸。院长发懵，倒不在于挨的砸，而在于这男孩砸起来没个完。连被甩脱了，男孩还咄咄逼人地缠着打。手里没根棍子，要驯服这么个疯长起来的十六岁小魔王，可就不那么容易了。等两人各自罢手，至少在气性上，这一回合是约书亚·琼斯的。

===Original===
---

A bigger boy now, almost a man; well over six feet tall, but still ribby and hungry-looking. Eighteen now. Shining shoes in front of a Lenox Avenue barbershop. Making nine, ten, sometimes twelve dollars a week.

The head barber liked to stand in the doorway and kid the boy about being so big.

“Great big husky﻿—” he would draw out the “great” till it was as long as Joshua himself﻿—“great big husky like you﻿—it’s a shame. You oughter be movin’ pianos ’stead o’ whippin’ shoe-leather. Benny, come ’eh. Look at dis boy. When he stoop over his heels is higher ’n his head.”

Joshua Jones took it good naturedly, grinned occasionally, said little. “Shine?” was the most he ever uttered, and from this the men dubbed him Shine.

===Chinese===
---

这会儿男孩更大了，差不多是个人了：六英尺开外，可还是瘦得肋骨根根、一脸饥色。十八岁了。在勒诺克斯大道（Lenox Avenue）一家理发店门前擦皮鞋。一礼拜挣九块、十块，运气好十二块。

掌柜的理发师爱站在门口，拿这孩子的大个子打趣。

「好——一个大块头——」他把「好」字拖得老长，长得跟约书亚本人一个尺寸——「好——一个大块头，长成你这样——真叫可惜。你该去搬钢琴，不该在这儿磨鞋皮。班尼（Benny），过来。瞧瞧这孩子。他一弯腰，脚后跟比脑袋还高。」

约书亚·琼斯由着他们说，时不时咧嘴一笑，话不多。「擦一擦？」这是他至多的一句话，众人便由此给他起了诨号：夏因（Shine）。

===Original===
Nobody called him Shine, however, but Negroes. A fay patron, with no other intent than to be genial, once repeated the name Shine after hearing the head barber use it. “How do you get to the subway from here, Shine, my boy?” he asked, paying his bill.

Shine looked him up and down, and after a moment inquired, “How’d you know my name was Shine?”

“Guessed it,” smiled the patron.

“Guess how to get to the subway, then.”

The patron stared, gaped and departed mystified at so sudden hostility.

But the head barber, looking on, grinned and approved. “Tight kid,” he commented. “What I mean, *tight*.”

===Chinese===
不过除了黑人，谁也不叫他夏因。有位白人主顾，别无他意、纯属套近乎，听了掌柜的这么称呼，也跟着叫了一声。结账时问道：「去地铁打这儿怎么走，夏因，我的孩子？」

夏因把他上上下下打量了一遍，半晌才问：「你咋知道我叫夏因？」

「猜的，」主顾笑道。

「那你也猜猜地铁打哪儿走吧。」

主顾愣了愣，张着嘴走了——好端端忽然挨了呛，丈二金刚摸不着头脑。

旁观的掌柜理发师却咧着嘴直点头。「这小子有种，」他评道，「真真儿地，*有种*。」

===Original===
---

A tight kid makes a hard man﻿—two hundred and twenty pounds of hardness in this case, wrestling daily with pianos; pianos equally hard and four times as heavy; two hundred and twenty pounds of strength; not the mere strength of stevedores hooking cotton bales on a wharf; you can’t hurt a bale of cotton﻿—it can’t hurt you; tumble it, hook it, kick it﻿—what the hell? But pianos﻿—even swaddled in quilting﻿—pianos must be handled like glass. Not mere strength do they require, but delicacy and strength; not muscles driving out or yanking in with abandoned force, but muscles held taut, precisely controlled under however great tension, released or restrained at will. You are protecting not only the instrument but yourself and your partner at the other end. The soft edge of a cotton-bale won’t hurt a fellow’s foot﻿—the hard one of a piano will break it.

A piano is a malicious thing; it loves to slip out of your grip and snap at your toes, with an evil chuckle inside. Push up its lip and see it sneer; touch it and hear it rumble or whine. Ponderous, spiteful, treacherous live thing﻿—a single spirit in a thousand bodies, one of which will crush you soon or late.

A malicious thing. Only today they were putting a piano into a third-story window of a house on a busy street. They had used hooks over the cornice, and the cheap rotten cement crumbled. Cornices aren’t supposed to bear weight﻿—an inferior mixture will do. One hook came through just as Shine was reaching out of the window to catch hold of the suspended instrument and guide it through the frame. He heard the crackle of broken cement above, saw the instrument sag a little while over it showered crumbs of broken cornice. With the hand already extended he grabbed the nearest leg of the upright and pulled it part way through the window just before the other hook lost its hold above. The greater part of the piano however was still unsupported outside the window﻿—the longer arm of a lever that all but broke even Shine’s tremendous strength. Straining back with all the power of his back and arms, his knees braced against the lower edge of the window-frame, he held the instrument there slipping on the sill till Jinx and Bubber reached him. Someone must have been hurt in the crash that would surely have come otherwise.

===Chinese===
---

有种的孩子熬成硬汉子——这一位，是二百二十磅的硬，天天跟钢琴摔跤；钢琴也一样硬，还重上四倍；二百二十磅的力气——可不是码头工拿钩子搭棉花包的那种力气；棉花包砸不坏你——你也砸不坏它；翻它、搭它、踢它——有啥了不得？可钢琴——哪怕裹进了棉被——钢琴得当玻璃伺候。它要的不光是力气，是巧劲加力气；不是使蛮劲往外送、往里拽的肌肉，是绷得紧紧、任凭吃多大力气也收放得恰到好处的肌肉。你护的不光是琴，还有你自己和对过儿的伙计。棉花包的软边硌不疼谁的脚——钢琴的硬棱却能把脚砸断。

钢琴是个促狭东西；最爱从你手里滑脱，咬你的脚趾头，肚子里咯咯直笑。掀起它的上唇，看它冷笑；碰它一下，听它呜噜呜噜发牢骚。一个沉甸甸、记仇、使坏的活物——一副魂灵住着一千个身子，早晚有一个来压碎你。

促狭东西。就说今儿个，他们正往一条闹市街上一户人家的三楼窗户里送一架钢琴。挂钩搭在檐口上，那偷工减料的烂水泥酥了。檐口本就不该承重——料次一点也凑合。夏因正探身出窗去接那吊着的琴、好领它进窗框，一只钩子恰恰在这时松了脱。他听见头顶水泥酥裂的脆响，看见琴身往下一沉，碎檐口的渣子雨点般洒下来。他探出去的那只手顺势抓住立式琴最近的一条琴腿，赶在上头另一只钩子松脱之前，把它半拖进了窗。可琴的大半截还悬在窗外没有着落——一副杠杆的长臂，差一点拗断了连夏因那一身神力。他背和胳膊攒足了劲往后拽，膝盖死死抵住窗框下沿，硬是把琴抵在窗台上直打滑，直到金克斯和布伯赶到。不然那一下砸下去，非伤着人不可。

===Original===
“Thing nearly pulled me out the winder,” he remarked when the piano was again under control.

“Why the hell didn’ y’ let it go, then?”

Shine looked rather blank. “Damn ’f I thought of it,” he said and grinned at his own stupidity.

===Chinese===
「这玩意儿差点把我整个人拽出窗户外头去，」琴重新归置好了之后，他说。

「那你他妈当时咋不撒手？」

夏因一脸茫然。「妈的，压根儿没想到，」他说，随即为自己这份憨咧嘴笑了。

### III / 三

===Original===
Joshua Jones, whom his fellows called Shine, came out of his reverie, to observe the return of Jinx and Bubber, arm in arm and quite happily drunk.

“This yeh freckle-face giraffe, he’s a good boogy,” Bubber declared. “Good boogy﻿—yassuh. He’s my boy. Ain’t you my boy, biggy?”

“No lie,” Jinx agreed. “Tell ’im ’bout that licker we ruint.”

“Try some good licker,” Shine invited, turning the rest of his pint over to them. “Go ’head﻿—I got enough.”

“Jes’ had some good licker, I tell y’﻿—Pat saw us go﻿—”

===Chinese===
约书亚·琼斯——伙伴们都叫他夏因——从遐想里回过神来，正瞧见金克斯和布伯回来，勾肩搭背，醉得快快活活。

「这个雀斑脸长颈鹿，是个好黑哥们，」布伯宣布。「好黑哥们——没错儿。他是我的好兄弟。是不是我的好兄弟，大个子？」

「不假，」金克斯附和。「跟他说说咱们糟蹋的那瓶好酒。」

「来点好酒，」夏因招呼着，把剩下的半瓶递过去。「喝吧——我够喝了。」

「刚喝过好酒，跟你说——帕特眼看着咱们拿的——」

===Original===
“Y’all drink,” Shine ordered, “and let me do the talkin’.”

“Talk, then﻿—talk. Don’ nobody have to listen jes’ ’cause you talk. Talk.”

“I told y’all ’bout that Court Avenue job in the mornin’.”

“What d’ hell you so worried ’bout that job for?”

“Might have to get me some extra hands. Boss told me find somebody.”

===Chinese===
「你们喝，」夏因吩咐，「话让我来说。」

「说呀——说呀。你说话又不兴谁非听不可。说吧。」

「跟你们说过明儿一早法院大道那趟活了。」

「那活你他妈操哪门子心？」

「兴许得多添几双手。老板叫我找人。」

===Original===
There was quick and sober resentment on the part of Jinx and Bubber. “Extra hands﻿—fo’ whut? Ain’ no job too big fo’ us three.”

“Trouble, maybe,” Shine explained. “You know what’s happened already. Guy tried to move in on 149th Street, this winter and they dared ’em to take the stuff out of the van. Jes’ las’ month, four blocks from where we go tomorrow, somebody put dynamite under a shine that moved in on his hardness. Well, boss is making this dickty pay for risk this time, and we get a bonus, see? But we got to get the stuff in safe, else﻿—no bonus. And we got to keep our eyes open, or we may leave some of our hips right up there on that Court Avenue asphalt.”

“Won’t leave none o’ Jinx’s,” Bubber prophesied.

“How come?” challenged Jinx.

“ ’Cause you ain’ got none to leave, you doggone eel.”

===Chinese===
金克斯和布伯立时酒也醒了，脸上挂不住：「添人手——添来干啥？天底下还没有咱哥仨搬不动的活。」

「兴许有麻烦，」夏因解释。「以前的事你们知道。今年冬天有人想搬上149街，人家当面撂话，敢把东西卸下车试试。就在上个月，离咱们明儿去的地方四条街，有人往一个硬闯进去的黑人底下埋了炸药。这回老板跟这位体面人把风险钱算足了，咱们有一份外快，懂吧？可东西得平平安安搬进去——不然，外快没有。再说咱们得把眼睛放亮些，搞不好就得把谁的半拉胯骨撂在那条法院大道的柏油路上。」

「金克斯的胯骨保准撂不下，」布伯预言。

「怎么讲？」金克斯不服。

「因为你压根儿没有胯骨可撂，你这条死泥鳅。」

===Original===
“So be ready for anything,” Shine said. “Five bucks extra apiece if the junk gets in OK.”

“Well﻿—” Bubber was uncertain, “﻿—five bucks is five bucks, but they’s a lot mo’ five buckses loose in the world ’n they is hips. Look yeh.” He exhibited his own hips by drawing his coat in tight around the waist. “See them? It’s took me twenty-five years to git them. And you talkin’ ’bout lettin’ somebody throw dynamite at ’em fo’ five bucks. Huh. Man down at Coney Island once offered me *ten* bucks a day jes’ to let ’em throw baseballs at my haid﻿—and baseballs don’t explode.”

“Furthermo’,” Jinx added, “you could spare that haid. ’Twouldn’ be no loss whatsoever.”

“Point is,” said Shine, “five bucks or nothin’, I’m jes’ tellin’ y’ to be ready, see? If anybody bother us jes’ up and knock hell out ’n ’em, that’s all.”

“You a pretty hard boogy, Mr. Shine,” Bubber observed, “but I ain’t never see you knock the hell out ’n no dynamite.”

===Chinese===
「所以有个准备，」夏因说，「五块不五块的且不说，我就是叫你们有个数，懂吧？谁敢来搅和，上去揍他个半死，就这么回事。」

「这个——」布伯拿不定主意，「——五块是五块，可这世道上散着的五块，比胯骨多多喽。你瞧。」他把衣裳往腰里一紧，亮出自己的胯。「瞧见没？这两块胯，长了我整整二十五年。你倒好，说让人家拿炸药炸它，就为五块钱。哼。科尼岛（Coney Island）有个人，出*十*块一天，雇我站着让他们朝我脑袋扔棒球——棒球可不会炸。」

「再说，」金克斯补一句，「你那脑袋丢了也不心疼。横竖不算什么损失。」

「要紧的是，」夏因说，「别管五块不五块，我就是嘱咐你们有备无患，明白？有人来找茬，劈头就揍，揍扁为止，完事。」

「你倒是个够硬的黑哥们，夏因先生，」布伯说，「可我从来没见你把炸药揍扁过。」

===Original===
“Far as I’m concerned,” contributed Jinx, “I’m ready now﻿—to run. I been haulin’ furniture, and I been haulin’ pianos; but when they starts plantin’ dynamite, this baby’s gonna start haulin’ hind-parts!”

“Be the first honest haulin’ you ever done, too,” commented Bubber.

To Shine this banter was merely pledge of allegiance in case of crisis, assurance that the hiring of extra hands would in no event be necessary. Beneath the jests, the avowed fear, the merriment, was a characteristic irony, a typical disavowal of fact and repudiation of reality, a markedly racial tendency to make light of what actually was grave﻿—a tendency stressed in Jinx and Bubber by the habitual perversion of their own conduct toward each other. Members of another race might have said simply:

“What the hell do you think we are﻿—quitters?”

So between them they killed the rest of the pint and mourned its death with laughter.

===Chinese===
「要我说，」金克斯表态，「我现在就备好了——备好撒腿跑。我是搬过家具，也搬过钢琴；可等他们埋起炸药来，这位爷就要改行搬屁股喽！」

「那也该是你头一回干正经搬运，」布伯评道。

在夏因听来，这一番打趣不过是危难当头时的效忠保证，是「绝用不着添人手」的定心丸。玩笑底下、叫嚷害怕底下、哄笑底下，是一种特有的反话，一种典型的嘴上不认账、心里不认现实，一种把天大的事当笑话讲的种族脾性——这脾性在金克斯和布伯身上，又叫他们俩彼此之间惯常的反着来推到了十分。换个旁的种族，多半就直截了当地说：

「你把我们当什么人了——怂包？」

就这样，三人把剩下的酒分而食之，又笑着给它吊了丧。

===Original===
---

Patmore returned, grinning.

“You two,” he directed Bubber and Jinx, “catch air. I got a bug to put in the big boy’s ear.” And when they had eventually obeyed, he went on to Shine: “Jes’ to show y’ they’s no hard feelin’s, I got a scheme that means bucks, and if you got two good eyes, you kin see how to make some of ’em.”

“They’s mo’ guys in jail for schemin’ than they is for bein’ blind.”

===Chinese===
---

帕特莫尔回来了，咧着嘴。

「你们俩，」他冲布伯和金克斯一努嘴，「起驾吧。我跟大个子咬咬耳朵。」两人磨蹭了半天才依了他，他便对夏因接着说：「为了表明没有芥蒂，我这儿有个财路，你只要长着俩好眼睛，就看得见钱怎么挣。」

「蹲大牢的，搞鬼的比瞎眼的多。」

===Original===
“Listen. You don’t specially like no dickties, do y’?”

“I ain’t none too fond o’ rats.”

“But dickties give you a very special pain, don’t they?”

“Lot’s o’ places. No lie.”

“Me too. Now that’s where you and I are alike, see?”

===Chinese===
「听着。你不怎么待见体面人，对吧？」

「我对穷哥儿也说不上多疼。」

「可体面人特别硌你的心，是不是？」

「硌的地方多了。不假。」

「我也是。瞧，这就是你我心连心的地方，懂吧？」

===Original===
Shine’s silence admitted nothing; but Pat went on:

“Heard you say sumpin’ ’bout movin’ this dickty Merrit.”

“Did?”

“Yea. Now there’s a guy I can’t see with field glasses.”

“No?”

“No.﻿—Tell you sumpm’.” Pat looked about to be sure of privacy, leaned closer to the bar counter. “If Merrit died tomorrer﻿—I wouldn’ send ’im no flowers.”

===Chinese===
夏因不吭声，等于什么也没认；帕特却接着说：

「听见你说要给那位体面人梅里特搬家了。」

「听见了？」

「可不。这家伙，我拿望远镜也瞧不上。」

「哦？」

「瞧不上。——跟你透个底儿。」帕特四下扫了一眼，确信没人，往吧台上凑近了些。「梅里特要是明天死了——我一朵花也不会送。」

===Original===
“What you got ’gainst ’im?”

“Plenty.” There came a characteristic confidential twist to one side of Pat’s mouth. “He put me in some time back, see? Damage suit﻿—ten thousand berries. Hit a guy crossin’ the speedway﻿—knocked him f’ a gool, the dumbbell. Well﻿—it was pay off or see jail, and naturally I wasn’ go’n’ see jail. Coulda’ got out cheaper may be, on’y this bird Merrit wouldn’t listen to reason. Claim’ he was go’n’ bring in my occupation and lots o’ other stuff if I didn’ come clean﻿—forcin’ my hand, see? Knew I had cash and knew he could make me pay off by threatenin’ to squeal. I ought to ’a’ crowned him then, but he was too wise﻿—knew where to meet me and when. So all I could do was pay off. Ten thousand bucks to stay out o’ jail.”

“Ten thousand bucks wasted,” Shine said.

Pat misunderstood. “Yea,” he agreed. “Nothin’ to show for it. Know what I could ’a’ done with that much at that time?”

“What?”

===Chinese===
「你跟他什么仇？」

「多了去了。」帕特的半边嘴角扭出一个特有的、递情报的弧度。「他把我弄进去过一回，懂吧？索赔官司——一万块钱。有个傻瓜蛋横穿快车道撞我车上——撞出去老远，这蠢货。得——不舍钱就蹲大牢，我自然不肯蹲大牢。本来兴许能少花点，可梅里特这鸟人油盐不进。扬言我不痛快掏钱，就把我的营生连同别的乱七八糟全抖出来——这是逼我上套，懂吧？知道我有现钱，也知道他一嚷嚷我就得掏。当时真该照他脑门来一下，可惜他精得很——约我见面的地方、时候都挑得死。我只好掏。一万块，换了个不蹲大牢。」

「一万块打了水漂，」夏因说。

帕特听拧了。「可不，」他应道，「连个响都没听见。知道那一万块搁那时候我能干什么吗？」

「干什么？」

===Original===
“I could ’a’ bought in a fay neighborhood and held on for a price. I could ’a’ made fifteen thousand on that ten. Same as he’s doin’ now.”

“So now you figger on a comeback?”

Pat was almost reproachful. “ ’Course not﻿—that ain’t the kind o’ bird I am. Hell, I ain’t evil, Shine. Anyway th’ ain’ nothin’ I kin do ’bout it now anyhow, is they?”

“How’n hell do I know?”

“All I want is his trade, man. Bygones kin be bygones, far as I give a damn. Gittin’ even is woman’s stuff﻿—man don’ hold no grudges. But if I kin sell him and his friends licker regular, it’ll mean a lot to me, see?”

“Unscheme yo’ scheme, boogy.”

===Chinese===
「我能在白人街区买进一套，攥着等涨价。一万本钱能挣一万五。跟他这会儿干的一样。」

「所以你现在琢磨着东山再起？」

帕特几乎委屈起来。「哪能呢——我不是那号鸟人。得了，我又不恶毒，夏因。反正事到如今我也拿他没辙，不是吗？」

「我他妈怎么知道？」

「我要的只是他的买卖，老兄。既往不咎，老子才懒得计较。找后账是娘们儿干的事——爷们儿不记仇。可我要是能长期卖酒给他和他那帮朋友，对我可不是小数，懂吧？」

「你那财路还是自己收着吧，老哥。」

===Original===
“Listen, I’m handlin’ a Canadian Club that’ll sell itself, no stuff. If I kin git him to sample it, he’ll take it﻿—order it for himself and recommend it to his friends. It’s bound to go big, see? But hyeh’s the thing: if he knows I sent it he’ll figure I’m tryin’ to poison him and be scared to touch it, see? Now I got half a case on hand he kin have and I got ten bucks you kin have if you deliver it along with his things in the mornin’.”

Shine’s brows lifted. “Yea?”

“Yea. You’re my agent, see? Only don’t tell him﻿—let him think you’re handlin’ it y’self. They’ll be more later﻿—not only to him but his friends, and you kin collect ev’y time. How ’bout it?”

Shine’s answer did not come promptly.

“Fact,” Pat pursued, “with yo’ job, you could work up a wonderful delivery service for me﻿—no suspicion attached to it, see? Hyeh’s yo’ chance, man﻿—start out as my agent.”

===Chinese===
「听着，我手上这批加拿大俱乐部（Canadian Club），货自己会说话，不掺假。我只要能请他尝一口，他就跑不了——自己订，还荐给他朋友。保准能打开局面，懂吧？可事儿在这儿：他要知道是我送的，准以为我要药死他，碰都不敢碰，懂吧？我手头正好有半箱，可以给他；再拿出十块给你——明儿早上你随他的家什一道送到，就归你。」

夏因眉毛一挑。「是吗？」

「是。你当我的掮客，懂吧？只是别告诉他——让他以为是你自己的货。往后还有——不光给他，还有他朋友，你回回都能抽头。怎么样？」

夏因没有马上答话。

「实话，」帕特追着说，「有你这份差事，你能给我张罗一条顶好的送酒路子——谁也不会起疑，懂吧？机会来了，老兄——从我的掮客干起。」

===Original===
“Agent yo’ hiney,” said Shine. “Listen. Ain’t you heard ’bout me?”

“ ’Bout you?”

“Sho’, man. I done started already.”

“Bootleggin’?”

“No lie. I got a regular business. Ain’ but two people in it, though, the bootlegger and the customer. I’m both of ’em.”

===Chinese===
「掮客你个头，」夏因说。「听着。你没听说过我的事？」

「你的什么事？」

「当然，老兄。我早干起来了。」

「贩私酒？」

「不假。我这买卖正规得很。就是里头统共俩人：私酒贩子，加顾客。俩人都是我。」

===Original===
Once again Pat eyed Shine in silent frustration and, after an angry moment, turned away wordless.

Watching him go, Shine grinned, then frowned and muttered to himself:

“Wise guy. Aimin’ to choke Merrit and throw the blame on me. Jes’ ’cause I bring my own licker in and pass up his kerosene. Can y’ beat it﻿—? Wonder how many kinds of a jackass that bozo thinks I am?”

===Chinese===
帕特又一次憋着火打量夏因，干瞪了片刻，一言不发扭头走了。

瞧着他走远，夏因咧嘴一笑，随即皱起眉，自言自语：

「精明鬼。想药倒梅里特，再把罪名扣我头上。就为我自带酒、不喝他那些煤油。见过没有——也不掂掂，那货当我是几种货色的蠢驴？」

### IV / 四

===Original===
By way of contrast, it is of further interest to drop in on a little group of dickties, superiorly self-named the Litter Rats, who were assembled informally this evening in the dwelling of one J. Pennington Potter, their current president.

This particular meeting of the Litter Rats’ Club had been set apart for the discussion of The Negro’s Contribution to Art and The Lost Sciences of Ethiopia. But when Fred Merrit announced that he had bought a house on Court Avenue, most exclusive of the residential streets adjacent to Negro Harlem, scheduled discussions were for the moment forgotten; and when he added that he intended to live in the house, and to do so whether a riot resulted or not, the dozen men about the room came promptly to the edges of their chairs.

“Preposterous!” said J. Pennington Potter, a plump little sausage of a man, whose skin seemed stuffed to the limit with the importance of what it contained.

“Why so?” inquired Merrit.

===Chinese===
作为对照，不妨再顺便去看看一小撮体面人——他们自我感觉良好，自封「垃圾鼠俱乐部（Litter Rats）」——这天晚上，这伙人正不拘礼数地聚在他们本届主席J·彭宁顿·波特（J. Pennington Potter）的宅子里。

垃圾鼠俱乐部的这一场聚会，本来专候着讨论《黑人对艺术的贡献》和《埃塞俄比亚失传的科学》。可弗雷德·梅里特（Fred Merrit）一宣布自己在法院大道——紧邻黑人哈莱姆的住宅街里顶顶排外的一条——买了一所房子，排定的议题当下就忘到了脑后；待他又补上一句，说自己打算住进去，而且不管闹不闹乱子都要住，满屋十来个男人齐刷刷挪到了椅子边上。

「荒唐透顶！」J·彭宁顿·波特说。他是根圆滚滚的小香肠，那张皮仿佛被里头盛着的重要感撑到了极限。

「怎么就荒唐了？」梅里特问。

===Original===
“This colony,” Potter pronounced, “should extend itself naturally and gradually﻿—not by violence and bloodshed.”

“The extension of territory by violence and bloodshed strikes me as natural enough,” Merrit grinned. “I haven’t much of a memory, but I seem to recall one or two instances﻿—”

“Progress is by evolution, not revolution,” expostulated J. Pennington Potter. “And you may be sure that race progress is no exception.”

“Who the deuce said anything about race progress or about extending the colony?” asked Tod Bruce, the young and far from fundamentalist rector of St. Augustine’s. “Why is it that a shine can never do anything except as a shine?”

“Well,” commented Langdon, an innocent looking youngster who was at heart a prime rascal and who compensated by writing poetry, “if Fred will just keep his hat on, none of his neighbors will know he’s a shine.”

===Chinese===
「咱们这块聚居地，」波特断言，「应当自然而然、循序渐进地扩展——不该靠暴力和流血。」

「靠暴力和流血扩地盘，在我看来自然得很，」梅里特咧嘴一笑。「我这人记性不济，可好像总还记得那么一两件——」

「进步靠进化，不靠革命，」J·彭宁顿·波特劝诫道。「种族的进步，你大可放心，也决不例外。」

「谁他妈提种族进步、提扩展聚居地了？」圣奥古斯丁教堂（St. Augustine's）年轻、远非基要派的牧师托德·布鲁斯（Tod Bruce）发问。「怎么一个黑人干什么事，就永远非得顶着黑人的名头干？」

「这个嘛，」兰登（Langdon）搭腔——这小子一脸无辜，骨子里是个头等泼皮，靠写诗将功折罪——「弗雷德只要帽子不摘，左邻右舍就认不出他是黑人。」

===Original===
“Or he could try Stay-Straight for those kinks﻿—” someone suggested.

“My point,” said Bruce, “is that Fred probably isn’t concerned primarily with the racial aspect of the thing﻿—”

“He ought to be!” exploded Potter.

“I am,” said Merrit coolly. “All of you know where I stand on things racial﻿—I’m downright rabid. And even though, as Tod suggests, I’d enjoy this house, if they let me alone, purely as an individual, just the same I’m entering it as a Negro. I hate fays. Always have. Always will. Chief joy in life is making them uncomfortable. And if this doesn’t do it﻿—I’ll quit the bar.”

“Well, Fred,” said Langdon, “don’t forget the revenue. They’ll pay you double the value of the place just to get you out, you know.”

===Chinese===
「要不他那头卷毛试试直发剂（Stay-Straight）——」有人出主意。

「我的意思是，」布鲁斯说，「弗雷德首先操心的，多半不是这件事的种族一面——」

「他就该操心！」波特炸了。

「我操着呢，」梅里特淡淡地说。「种族的事我什么立场，你们全知道——我是个不折不扣的疯狗。不过诚如托德所说，就算人家不来搅扰，我纯粹作为一个人也会喜欢这所房子；可我进去住，就是作为黑人进去。我恨白人。一向恨。往后也恨。这辈子头一桩乐事，就是叫他们不痛快。这要是还不奏效——我就退出律师界。」

「对了，弗雷德，」兰登说，「别忘了那笔进项。他们为了请你搬家，肯出双倍房价呢，你知道。」

===Original===
“I had that in mind, but hell﻿—what’s money? They won’t pay me what I’ll ask anyhow, and I won’t sell for less.”

There was a certain grimness about Merrit, for all his rosy cheeks and cherubic grin. He was anomalous in certain important particulars. Fair as the northernmost Nordic, his sandy hair was yet as kinky as that of any pure blooded African; and not the blackest of Negroes could have hated the dominant race more thoroughly.

“You know,” he said, “I especially wanted my mother to live there. How she would have queened it﻿—it would have been part compensation﻿—”

It was another of Merrit’s anomalies that, though he hated his lineage in general, he had been especially devoted to his mother. She had always seemed to him a symbol of sexual martyrdom, a bearer of the cross, as he put it, which fair manhood universally placed on dark womanhood’s shoulders. Of all those whom he blamed and cursed for his own mongrel heritage, she was the one exception; for her, the only one he had actually known, he had only racial pity and filial devotion. She had recently died, late enough in her own life, but too early in his, to enjoy the luxuries he had just become able to give her. And so in addition to what she already represented, she had now become a symbol of motherhood unrewarded, idealized in memory far beyond what had been true in life.

“I think,” he added, “my housekeeper’ll give the neighbors enough of a shock, though. She’s as colored as they come.”

===Chinese===
「我掂量过，可是见鬼——钱算什么？反正他们出不到我要的价，我也绝不贱卖。」

梅里特两颊红润、一脸娃娃笑，骨子里却透着一股狠。他有几处要紧的地方生得反常：明明白得像最北头的北欧人，一头沙色头发却卷得跟纯血非洲人一个样；而恨起主宰的种族来，最黑的黑人也不见得恨得更彻底。

「你们知道，」他说，「我原本顶想让母亲住到那儿去。她该多像个女王——这也算一份补偿——」

梅里特的另一桩反常是：他虽恨透了自己的血统，对母亲却孝得入骨。在他眼里，她始终是一个性祭坛上的殉道者，用他的话说，一个背十字架的人——那十字架是白种男人普遍搁到黑种女人肩上的。凡是被他又怨又咒、怪他们给了他这副混血身世的人里，她是唯一的例外；对这个他唯一真正亲识过的人，他只有种族的悲悯和儿子的孝心。她新近过世了：论她自己的年岁，走得不算早；论他的，却太早了——她没能享上他刚刚挣得起的那份福。于是，在她原本代表的一切之上，她又添了一重身分：一个不曾得报的母亲，在记忆里被捧得远远高过了生前的真身。

「不过我想，」他又说，「我家管家就够街坊们受的了。她黑得不能再黑。」

===Original===
“There’ll be a riot!” exclaimed J. Pennington Potter.

“Good!” grinned the cherubic Fred.

“They’ll set fire to the place﻿—they’ll blow it up﻿—the way they did Morris and Peters.”

“Insurance is a marvelous invention, isn’t it?”

“Uncalled for distress. I thoroughly disapprove of deliberate, intentional havoc. It’s just what we’re trying to prevent.” This was to be expected of the extremely proper J. Pennington Potter, a “social worker” with a windy, pompous voice and a deep devotion to convention.

===Chinese===
「要出乱子的！」J·彭宁顿·波特喊起来。

「好啊！」娃娃脸的弗雷德笑嘻嘻。

「他们会放火烧房子——会把它炸上天——跟对付莫里斯（Morris）和彼得斯（Peters）一个样。」

「保险可是一项了不起的发明，不是吗？」

「无谓的灾殃。我彻底反对蓄意的、存心的破坏。这正是我们要全力防止的。」这话出自规矩到极点的J·彭宁顿·波特之口，倒也不出人意料：他是个「社会工作者」，嗓门空洞浮夸，对陈规的虔敬深入骨髓。

===Original===
“Well,” moderated Bruce, “Harlem began its growth by riots. I remember when I was a youngster, I used to be scared to stay out after dark. It was pretty bad then﻿—either a crowd of fay boys would catch a jig and beat him up or else a crowd of jigs would get a fay boy and teach him the fear of the Lord. In either case the thing would be the first skirmish of a pitched battle somewhere on the frontier. The shines tricked a half dozen Irish lads into 134th Street one night, I remember, and two of them never came out. Cops﻿—there weren’t any black cops then﻿—always went in threes at least. And I recall one day when twelve mounted policemen came galloping up 134th Street after one little West Indian iceman﻿—and galloped back without him. It was really comical.”

The others gave Bruce attention, watching him as he spoke. He too was fair, but less so than Merrit, and his skin was uniformly pallid. His face was lean, his features prominent and severe to the point of austerity, the nose large and narrow, the chin advancing, the mouth wide, straight, and thin-lipped. As if to offset the ascetic in this countenance, his eyes were deep-set and black, and in them some curious passion gleamed constantly like a flame. As he spoke these eyes engaged everything that might hold a drop of interest, comprehended it, drained it, left it; swiftly flashed from this to that, paused, penetrated, abandoned; sought further, halted, penetrated again, departed﻿—a pair of black wasps.

“Those were the happy days,” he went on. “People kept kettles of hot lye on the stoves and carried them to their doors whenever the bell rang. And you could go upon the roof of your house and not see a chimney within four blocks: they’d all been knocked down and the bricks stacked at front room windows for ammunition. And say﻿—one night a bunch of bad jigs﻿—like those over on Fifth Avenue now﻿—mistook me for a fay, and I had a devil of a time proving I was a Negro, too!”

===Chinese===
「呃，」布鲁斯打个圆场，「哈莱姆就是靠乱子起家的。我记得小时候，天一黑我就吓得不敢在外头待。那阵子真够呛——不是一帮白人小子逮住个黑人揍一顿，就是一帮黑人逮住个白人小子，教他领教领教上帝的威严。不管哪样，这都是边界线上某场大混战的前哨战。有一晚，黑人们把半打爱尔兰小子诓进了134街，我记得，里头俩再没出来。警察——那会儿还没有黑人警察——出门至少三人一伙。我还记得有一天，十二个骑警追着一个小小的西印度卖冰人一路冲上134街——又一路空着手续回来了。真是滑稽。」

众人都静听布鲁斯，看他说话。他也是浅肤色，却不及梅里特白，皮色是一种匀净的苍白。脸瘦，五官突兀冷峻，近乎刻厉：鼻梁又高又窄，下巴前突，嘴阔而直，唇薄。仿佛要抵消这张脸上的苦修气，他双眼深陷而乌黑，里头有一种古怪的激情常驻，像一簇火苗。说话间，这双眼睛逮住每一样可能有一滴趣味的东西，看透它，吸干它，撇下它；从这件倏地闪到那件，停一停，扎进去，丢开；再找，再停，再扎，再走——一对黑胡蜂。

「那才叫好日子，」他接着说。「家家炉子上坐着滚开的碱水，门铃一响就拎到门口去。站上自家房顶，四条街里看不见一根烟囱：全推倒了，砖码在前屋窗台底下当弹药。还有——有天夜里，一帮愣头黑青——跟如今第五大道上那帮一个路数——把我错当成白人，我费了老鼻子劲才证明我也是黑人！」

===Original===
“I had the same experience,” said Merrit. “You should’ve seen me exhibiting my kinky head.”

“It was probably straight for a while,” grinned Langdon.

“It’s the old, old story,” said Bruce. “War﻿—conquest of territory. But our side of the thing isn’t all there is to it. The fays have a side too, you know.”

“I know,” Merrit protested, like the lawyer he was, “but we aren’t supposed to see that.”

“Well, I don’t know. It’s easy to laugh now. But the fact is, it was tragedy. Black triumph is always white tragedy. We won﻿—we won territory. All the fays had to get out, make way, make room for us. What did they do? Resist, of course﻿—why the devil shouldn’t they? Clung to their district, tried to recover. And we broke their heads with chimney bricks and bathed their bodies in hot lye. How do you suppose they felt about it?”

===Chinese===
「我也有过一遭，」梅里特说。「你该看看我当场展览我这头卷毛。」

「那会儿兴许还是直的呢，」兰登咧嘴。

「老掉牙的老故事了，」布鲁斯说。「战争——夺地。可事情不光有咱们这一面。白人也有他们的一面，你们知道。」

「我知道，」梅里特像个律师似的抗辩，「可人家没打算叫咱们看见那一面。」

「唉，难说。如今笑一笑容易。可说实在的，那会儿是悲剧。黑人的凯旋从来是白人的悲剧。我们赢了——我们赢了地盘。白人只好全数搬走，让路，给我们腾地方。他们怎么着？当然抵抗——凭什么他妈不抵抗？死守他们的街区，想法子夺回来。咱们拿烟囱砖砸破他们的头，拿滚碱水给他们洗澡。你想想，他们心里是什么滋味？」

===Original===
“Best thing that ever happened to ’em,” grinned Fred.

“But tough on *them*, you’ll admit.”

“What of it?”

“Only this﻿—that when you move up there on Court Avenue, you’re opening up all those old scars. Just as Pott says, they’ll resist. They’ll warn you with threatening notes. They’ll try to buy you out. If these don’t work, they will probably dynamite you.”

“I’ve received one warning already.”

===Chinese===
「那是他们天字第一号的好事，」弗雷德咧嘴。

「可对他们*够狠的*，这你总得认。」

「狠又怎样？」

「只这一点——你一搬上法院大道，就是把这些老伤疤全数撕开。正像波特说的，他们会抵抗。先拿恐吓信警告你。再想法子把你买出去。这两样不灵，多半就要用炸药请你了。」

「恐吓信我已经收着一封了。」

===Original===
“You have?”

“You heard about Gamby, last month,” said someone. “They had a gang of toughs on hand and they wouldn’t even let the movers land his stuff on the sidewalk. Had to get the police.”

“Glad you mentioned that,” said Merrit. “I’ll send my worst stuff first, and I’ll get the toughest furniture-movers in Harlem.”

“Nowadays,” Bruce observed, “we grow by﻿—well﻿—a sort of passive conquest. The fays move out, and the jigs are so close no more fays will move in. So the landlord has to rent to jigs and the colony keeps extending. But if Fred wants to return to the older method, I don’t think it will do any great harm to the rest of us. He’s taking all the risk. And even though he claims a racial interest, he has admitted that the chief motive is personal after all. It’s his business.”

“There is absolutely no excuse for it,” was J. Pennington Potter’s final dictum.

“Who the hell asked for an excuse, Pott?” was all that Merrit answered.

===Chinese===
「当真？」

「上个月甘比（Gamby）那事儿你听说了，」有人说。「人家备好了一帮打手，连搬运工把东西卸到人行道上都不许。只好去请警察。」

「亏你提这个，」梅里特说。「我先送最不值钱的家什，再雇全哈莱姆最横的搬运工。」

「如今咱们靠的是——呃——一种不声不响的征服，」布鲁斯说。「白人搬走，黑人逼得近了，再没有白人肯搬进来。房东只好租给黑人，聚居地就这么一块块长。可弗雷德要想复古，走老法子，我看对咱们其余人也害不到哪儿去。风险他一人担。何况他自己也认了，嘴上说图的是种族，头一桩动机到底还是私人的。那是他自己的事。」

「这事无论如何说不过去，」这是J·彭宁顿·波特的终审判决。

「谁他妈要你说过去说不过去，波特？」梅里特回敬了这一句，再无二话。

### V / 五

===Original===
Court Avenue is a straight, thin spinster of a street which even in July is cold. There is about it an air of arched eyebrows, of skirts drawn aside and comments made with a sniff. It is adorned in sparse, lean, scrawny maples, all suffering from malnutrition, and these tend to stress rather than relieve the hardness of dry, level pavements.

The dwellings are all the same pale gray and are all essentially alike, four stories tall, thin to gauntness, droop-eyed with drawn shapes, standing shoulder to shoulder in long, inhospitable rows. Stone stoops, well withdrawn from the sidewalk, lend an air of inaccessibility, and the tiny front yards that might dispel this illusion by only a bit of grass or a flower are instead uniformly laid away beneath slabs of expressionless concrete.

Twice a day, when sunlight touches the windows of this side in the morning and again of that side at night, Court Avenue smiles a chilly, crystalline smile. It is the sort of smile that goes with the words, “My dear! Can you imagine such a thing?” and you might suppose that the street was returning even the sun’s genial greeting and warm farewell with a disapproving sneer.

In short, Court Avenue is a snob of a street. Yet it is somewhat to be pitied in its pretense at ignoring the punishment that is at hand: the terribly sure approach of the swiftly spreading Negro colony.

===Chinese===
法院大道（Court Avenue）是一条笔直、瘦窄、老姑娘似的街，哪怕七月里也是冷的。它周身一股挑着眉毛的气派，裙子往旁边一撇、鼻子里哼出一句评点的做派。街上稀稀落落点缀着几株干瘦嶙峋的枫树，棵棵营养不良，非但遮不住干燥平坦的路面那股硬，反倒衬得它更硬。

沿街的宅子一色的浅灰，样式也根本一个模样：四层楼高，瘦得形销骨立，眼皮耷拉、身架紧抻，肩挨肩站成长长两排，拒人千里。石阶门台离人行道远远缩回去，平添一层高不可攀；本可以凭一方草皮、一朵花散掉这股冷气的小小前院，却一律铺上没有表情的水泥板，封得严严实实。

一天有两回——早晨太阳晒着这边的窗户，傍晚又晒着那边——法院大道便露出一个冷冷的、结晶般的笑。那是配着这么一句话的笑：「天哪！亏你想得出来！」你简直可以设想，这条街连太阳亲热的问候、暖和的道别，都要撇着嘴哼回去。

一言以蔽之，法院大道是一条势利眼的街。不过它那副硬撑着无视大祸临头的做态，倒也有几分可怜：黑人的聚居地正风驰电掣地扑过来，跑不了了。

===Original===
---

Isaacs’ Transportation Company, which is to say old man Isadore Isaacs, would have trusted Joshua Jones with any moving job whatever. It was work that Shine loved because of the challenge it presented to his personal strength and skill. He took charge, accepted responsibility, helped execute the orders he gave. Whenever a staircase or hallway presented a difficult turn or an insufficient dimension, he was at hand to decide just how the problem should be solved. Whenever a valuable piece of ungainly size had to be dismembered, and afterwards reconstructed, his knowledge of the mysterious anatomy of furniture was wholly adequate. Whenever a piano was to be hoisted, he saw to each important item: himself selected and anchored the tackle and guided the instrument through the window that was chosen to admit it.

Pianos indeed, were his particular prey, his almost living archenemies. He personified them, and out of controlling them, handling them, directing them helpless through midair, he derived a satisfaction comparable to that of a tamer of beasts. There was a superstition that a piano would “get” a man one time or another. Jinx and Bubber had both suffered injuries from instruments that slipped from their grips. But Shine laughed at this superstition, not because it was a superstition, but because he knew that he simply couldn’t be “had.”

Even the four-ton van was to Shine a beloved companion. He called her Bess; and Bess was the only thing on earth that he coveted. She was padded within and especially designed for the moving of things fine and fragile; her engine was responsive and smooth, her treads pneumatic, single in front, double in the rear. She rode like a private ambulance and she could make forty on a level. It was Shine’s ambition one day to win her away from old man Isaacs.

The crew was usually made up only of Shine, Jinx, and Bubber, and these three in two years of cooperation had come to work together in a fashion beyond faultfinding, carefully, quickly, punctually, untrailed by patrons’ complaints.

===Chinese===
---

艾萨克斯运输公司（Isaacs' Transportation Company）——说白了就是伊萨多·艾萨克斯（Isadore Isaacs）老头儿——随便什么搬运活，只管交给约书亚·琼斯，放心。这活夏因爱干，因为它考较他一身的力气和手艺。他当家，担责任，自己下的指令自己也搭一手。楼梯或走廊每逢拐不过弯、尺寸不够，他一到便断定该怎么解；每逢一件贵重笨重的家什得拆散、之后再装回去，他对家具那套神秘解剖学也了然于胸；每逢要吊钢琴，桩桩要紧处他都亲自过目：亲自挑挂具、下锚点，亲自把琴领进选定的那扇窗。

钢琴尤其是他的独门猎物，他几乎算得上活的死对头。他赋予它们人格，而制服它们、摆弄它们、引领它们在半空里束手无策，从中得来的满足，不下于驯兽师。行里有个迷信：钢琴早晚会「收拾」一个人。金克斯和布伯都吃过从手里滑脱的琴的亏。夏因却笑话这迷信——不是因为它迷信，而是因为他清楚，自己压根儿不会被「收拾」。

连那辆四吨货车，在夏因眼里也是心爱的伙伴。他管她叫贝斯（Bess）；贝斯是他这世上唯一眼馋的东西。她内里衬了软垫，专为搬运精细易碎之物而特制；引擎听话而平顺，轮子是充气胎，前头单轮，后头双轮。她开起来像一辆私人救护车，平路上能跑四十迈。夏因的雄心，是有朝一日把她从老艾萨克斯手里赢过来。

班子通常就是夏因、金克斯、布伯三个人。这哥仨搭档两年，磨合得无可挑剔：仔细、麻利、守时，东家的投诉一回也没沾过身。

===Original===
---

This early summer morning Shine swung Bess, loaded with Merrit’s possessions, into the chilly Court Avenue atmosphere, and, with deliberate malice, sped up to a roar, then coasted, shifting his spark to make the motor spit.

“That’ll wake up somebody,” he grinned as Bess bang-banged like an automatic. “Come on you bomb-throwers﻿—do your stuff﻿—Let’s go!”

“Boy, lemme out this cab,” said Bubber. “This darkey done gone crazy.”

“Shuh!” complained Jinx. “Ain’t go’n’ be no rough stuff in this neighborhood. Deader’n Strivers’ Row.”

===Chinese===
---

这个初夏的早晨，夏因驾驶着满载梅里特家当的贝斯，一头扎进法院大道冷冰冰的空气里，存心使坏地轰了一脚油门，任它咆哮，随后溜车，又拨弄火花塞，叫发动机噼噼啪啪直吐唾沫。

「这声儿该吵醒哪位了，」他咧嘴，贝斯像机关枪似的砰砰作响。「来吧你们这帮扔炸弹的——露一手啊——走起！」

「哥，让我下去，」布伯说。「这黑人怕是疯了。」

「嘁！」金克斯抱怨。「这条街不会动手动脚的。比奋进者排屋还死气。」

===Original===
They drew up and backed against the curb before number three-thirteen. The door opened and Merrit himself came out to meet them. He wore his usual air of nonchalance and his usual cherubic grin.

“Hello, fellows,” he greeted. “Get it all on one load? That’s clever.”

All three stared. Such cordiality in a dickty was nothing short of astonishing, and it put the suspicious workers immediately on guard.

While Jinx and Bubber unfastened van-doors, Merrit went up to Shine and leaned carelessly against a tree. “What do you think of this?” he said, producing a letter.

Shine accepted the proffered note without enthusiasm. It was without heading or salutation, typewritten, and lacking a signature:

===Chinese===
车子在313号门前靠边，倒着贴上路牙。门开了，梅里特亲自出来迎。还是那副满不在乎的神气，还是那副娃娃脸的笑。

「你们好啊，伙计们，」他招呼。「一趟全拉来了？漂亮。」

三个人直勾勾瞪着他。一个体面人这么和气，简直是一奇，登时叫这三位起疑的工人戒备起来。

金克斯和布伯去开车门，梅里特踱到夏因跟前，懒懒往一棵树上一靠。「你看看这个，」他说着，掏出一封信。

夏因接过递来的信，爱答不理。信没有抬头，没有称呼，打字机打的，也没有落款：

===Original===
> You are not wanted in this neighborhood.

> If you move in, we’ll move you out.

“Where’d you get it?” Shine asked Merrit.

“Found it in the vestibule when I came up to look around yesterday.”

“Humph,” said Shine. “Jes’ let ’em start sump’m while we’re here, that’s all.” And because he disliked dickties and wanted no talk with any one of them, he changed the subject rudely. “Where you want us to put this stuff?”

“Anywhere. Spread it all over the first floor. My housekeeper’ll come in from the country-place and have it arranged later.”

===Chinese===
> 这个街坊不欢迎你。

> 你要敢搬进来，我们就把你搬出去。

「哪儿来的？」夏因问梅里特。

「昨天我过来看房子，在门厅里捡的。」

「哼，」夏因说。「趁咱们在，让他们动动手试试，就这么回事。」他厌恶体面人，懒得跟任何一个多费唇舌，便粗鲁地岔开：「这些东西你要我们摆哪儿？」

「随便。满一楼摊开就成。我家管家过几天从郊区别墅过来，回头自己归置。」

===Original===
“Hear that?” Jinx said to Bubber, out of sight at the rear of the van. “Thass what I say ’bout a spade. Spade can’t git a little sump’n without stretchin’ it. His housekeeper. His country-place. Humph﻿—what’s a use lyin’ like that﻿—?”

“He ain’ lyin’, fool. That jasper’s got mo’ bucks ’n you got freckles. Got a swell place on the upstate Pike, not far out o’ town. Throws big parties and raises hell jes’ like d’ fays. Folks up there didn’ know he was a jig till he had a party﻿—and they offered him a million dollars fo’ the place jes’ to git him out. He wouldn’ leave, though.”

“Million dollars?”

“Uh-huh.”

“And he wouldn’ leave?”

“Uh-huh.”

===Chinese===
「听见没？」金克斯躲在车后头，跟布伯咬耳朵。「我早说黑人的德性。黑人稍微得着点什么，就非得吹大三分。他家管家。他的郊区别墅。哼——这么撒谎有个什么劲——？」

「他没撒谎，傻子。那主儿口袋里的票子，比你脸上的雀斑还多。城北公路上有一处气派的宅子，出城没多远。办大派对、可劲儿折腾，跟白人一个样。那边的街坊起先不知道他是黑人——后来办了场派对才知道——人家出一百万买他那宅子，就为了请他挪窝。他倒偏不挪。」

「一百万？」

「嗯哼。」

「他还不挪？」

「嗯哼。」

===Original===
“Huh! You lie wuss’n he do.”

Merrit’s words came to them repeating, “Mrs. Fuller will take care of everything later.”

“Thare now,” commented Jinx, “y’see?”

“See what?”

“Soon as a old crow gits up in d’ world, he got to grab hisse’f some other guy’s wife.”

Bubber regarded him with pity. “How you figger dat out?”

===Chinese===
「哼！你撒谎比他还厉害。」

梅里特的话音又飘过来一遍：「富勒太太（Mrs. Fuller）回头会把一切都料理好。」

「这不结了，」金克斯评论，「瞧见没？」

「瞧见什么？」

「老乌鸦一抖起来，就得抢人家的老婆。」

布伯怜悯地看着他。「你这话怎么推出来的？」

===Original===
“*His* name ain’t Fuller, is it?”

“No. And yo’ name ain’t Sherlock. Don’t you know what a housekeper is? And ain’t you never heard of sech a thing as a widow?”

“Aw man, what you talkin’ ’bout?”

“You ought to be a policeman, brother.”

“How come?”

“ ’Cause you very suspicious and very, very dumb.”

===Chinese===
「*他*又不姓富勒，对吧？」

「对。你也不姓福尔摩斯。管家是什么，你知道不知道？寡妇这种东西，你听没听说过？」

「嗐老兄，你说什么呢？」

「你该去当警察，兄弟。」

「怎么讲？」

「因为你疑心重，又笨，非常非常笨。」

===Original===
These two had been unwrapping carefully covered hindmost pieces of furniture. Shine came around to lend a hand, and Merrit moved along the curb to a position such that he could observe them. Now he indulged in another astonishing speech.

“Don’t be too damn careful about these things. Flat didn’t have anything but junk in it, anyway. Good stuff’s in the country﻿—won’t move it in till fall. Just chuck this stuff in and let it lay.”

What manner of dickty was this? He greeted you like an equal, casually shared his troubles with you, and did not seem to care in the least what the devil you did with his furniture.

Jinx said sullenly to Bubber. “All he wants is for us to scratch up sump’m, so he kin call the five bucks off.”

Bubber said to Jinx, “That ain’t it. He’s jes’ makin’ sure o’ friends in case d’ fays start sump’m.”

Shine said to himself, “If this bird wasn’t a dickty he’d be OK. But they never was a dickty worth a damn.”

===Chinese===
这两人正拆着裹得严严实实的几大件压轴家什。夏因绕过来搭手，梅里特也顺着路牙挪到一个能把他们尽收眼底的位置。这时他又发了一通惊人的议论。

「这些东西他妈的别太当心。公寓里除了破烂什么也没有。好东西都在乡下——入秋才往这儿搬。这些东西随便一撂就得了。」

这是哪一国的体面人？跟你平起平坐地打招呼，随随便便跟你倒苦水，他的家什你爱怎么糟践好像都半点不心疼。

金克斯没好气地对布伯说：「他就巴不得咱们磕了碰了什么，好赖掉那五块钱。」

布伯对金克斯说：「不是那么回事。他是提前买好交情，怕万一白人闹起来好有人搭把手。」

夏因心里想：「这鸟人要不是个体面人，倒真不赖。可惜体面人里就没一个顶用的。」

===Original===
---

The job was finished and they were throwing byproducts into the emptied van﻿—burlap and canvas wrappers, quilting, hemp rope, leather straps. Merrit had just turned the key in his door and was facing about for departure, while Shine was on the point of climbing into the cab. At this juncture, simultaneously, everybody made an observation. It was the only observation that they all would have been likely to make at one time, and it held Merrit at attention on the stoop, rendered Shine motionless with one foot upon the step of his cab, and halted Bubber in the act of throwing a gunnysack over Jinx’s head. Along the hitherto empty street a girl was briskly approaching.

You could see that she knew they were staring, so completely did she ignore them, and the ease with which she did so, the queenly unconcern with which she passed, indicated that she was accustomed to being stared at and did not mind it at all.

There was quite obviously no reason why she should have minded it. Certainly her attire invited no criticism﻿—a brief frock of cool black satin, sheer gunmetal hose, and trim patent-leather pumps. Nor did she herself. She was tall and her face was pretty, and her body slenderly invited, though her legs perversely eluded, the persistent caress of the sedulous soft black satin.

Even if this had been all﻿—a pretty girl on that gaunt empty street at this solitary hour, the staring would have been pardonable. But there was in addition an especially extenuating circumstance: the girl was not white.

===Chinese===
---

活干完了，众人正把边角零碎往卸空的车厢里扔——麻布片、帆布罩、棉被衬、麻绳、皮绑带。梅里特刚在门上拧完钥匙，正要回身道别，夏因也一脚踩上了驾驶室的踏板。就在这节骨眼上，所有人同时有了一个发现。这也差不多是这群人唯一可能同时有过的发现：它叫梅里特钉在门台上侧耳细听，叫夏因一脚悬在车门口一动不动，叫布伯把一条麻袋罩上金克斯脑袋的动作僵在半空。先前空荡荡的街上，一个姑娘正步履轻快地走来。

看得出她知道众人在盯她——她把众人无视得那样彻底；而那份从容，那副目不斜视、女王似的冷淡，又表明她被盯惯了，压根儿不往心里去。

她显然也确实犯不着往心里去。衣着一丝毛病挑不出——一袭凉快的黑缎短裙，透亮的铁灰色丝袜，利落的漆皮浅口鞋。人也一样挑不出。她个子高，脸盘漂亮，身子纤秾合度地招引着，唯独两条腿别别扭扭地躲闪着那片殷勤的黑缎绵绵不绝的爱抚。

哪怕光是这样——这条枯瘦空街上、这个冷清时辰里，站着个漂亮姑娘——众人的瞪视也算情有可原。何况还另有一层天大的情由：这姑娘不是白人。

===Original===
Before she quite passed beyond earshot Jinx and Bubber were indulging in low enthusiasms:

“Boy, do you see what I see?”

“Law‑a‑aw‑dy!”

“Mus’ be havin’ a recess in heaven!”

“No lie. Umh-umh-umh﻿—” Grunts to signify admiration far beyond words.

===Chinese===
她还没走出听力所及，金克斯和布伯已经压着嗓子热烈起来了：

「哥，你瞅见我瞅见的没？」

「我的老天爷哎！」

「天堂准是放课间休息了！」

「不掺假。嗯——嗯——嗯——」几声哼哼，表示倾慕远非言语所能形容。

===Original===
“Lady, you kin have all my week’s pay﻿—ev’y bit of it.” Bubber dived elaborately into his empty pockets, while Jinx vowed:

“I’m go’n’ get religion and die so I kin go to heaven and meet that angel﻿—yassuh!”

Suddenly comment ceased. Only two doors beyond Merrit’s house the girl turned in, traversed the short cement walk, mounted the stoop, unlocked and entered the front door.

Merrit raised his brows in a characteristic little expression of surprise. Shine saw him do so and had a swift interpretation for that expression:

“Figgerin’ on a jive already﻿—the doggone dickty hound. Why the hell dickties can’t stick to their own women ’thout messin’ around honest workin’ girls﻿—”

===Chinese===
「小姐，我这礼拜的工钱全归你——一个子儿不留。」布伯煞有介事地往自己空口袋里掏，金克斯则起誓：

「我这就去信教，好死了上天堂见这位天使——不骗你！」

议论戛然而止。离梅里特家两户开外，姑娘一拐弯进去了，走过短短的水泥甬道，登上门台，开锁，进了前门。

梅里特挑起眉毛，做了一个他特有的、小小的惊讶表情。夏因瞧在眼里，闪电般替这表情作了解读：

「这就盘算上吊膀子了——体面狗东西。这帮体面人他妈的守着自家的女人不过，偏要来招惹正经做工的姑娘——」

===Original===
Bubber was rapturizing without restraint, “Man﻿—oh﻿—man! A honey with high yaller laigs! And did you see that walk? That gal walks on ball-bearin’s, she do﻿—ev’rything moves at once.” He illustrated his idea with head-wobblings, shoulder-rollings, and loose backward protrusions and retractions of buttocks. “See what I mean? Tail-conscious, man, tail-conscious﻿—”

“You jes’ a damn liar,” came unexpectedly from Shine. “She walks like what she is, a lady﻿—and you talk like what you is, a rat. Come on, it’s gettin’ late﻿—let’s go from here.”

Whereupon Jinx looked at Bubber and Bubber looked at Jinx. Here was indeed something new, Shine championing a woman.

“Well, kiss my assorted peanuts!” ejaculated Bubber.

“Guess that’s the dynamite,” was Jinx’s dyspeptic surmise.

===Chinese===
布伯正忘乎所以地赞叹：「我的——个——哥！蜜糖人儿浅黄腿！瞧见她那走道儿没有？那丫头浑身装的是滚珠轴承——一走全跟着动。」他晃脑袋、滚肩膀、屁股一撅一收，亲身示范自己的见解。「懂我意思没？尾巴有数，老兄，尾巴有数——」

「你就是个屁话精，」夏因冷不丁来了一句。「她走路，像个淑女——她本来就是个淑女；你说话，像个穷哥儿——你本来就是个穷哥儿。走了，天不早了——撤。」

金克斯瞧瞧布伯，布伯瞧瞧金克斯。这可真是新鲜事：夏因替一个女人出头。

「哎呀我的个乖乖花生米！」布伯失声。

「我看那才是炸药，」金克斯酸溜溜地猜。

## Uplift. / 提升

### VI / 六

===Original===
Miss Agatha Cramp had, among other things, a sufficiently large store of wealth and a sufficiently small store of imagination to want to devote her entire life to Service; in fact, to Social Service on a large scale. And because Miss Cramp took very personal interest in her successive servants, it came about that this Social Service was directed towards definite racial groups. When her maid had been French, Miss Cramp had organized a club to assist rebuilding demolished French villages; when her maid had been Polish, she had taken up with a Society for the Aid of Starving Poland; and shortly after hiring a Russian girl, she became a member of the Russian Relief Committee.

Thus Miss Cramp had devoted the more recent years of her life to Service, and now, with a colored maid on hand, she had no outlet for her urge. For two weeks she had been idle, and idleness drove her to distraction. She felt worse and worse day by day, until at last her doctor said what she paid him to say: that she was on the verge of a nervous breakdown and would simply have to go to bed and rest.

She rested three days; whereupon an ironic Court Avenue sun revealed to her something of which she had hitherto been unaware: her colored maid, bringing in her breakfast, looked somehow amazingly pretty. And although Miss Cramp had no very generous eye for beauty, she was so struck by the discovery of what hitherto had mysteriously escaped her that she was moved to exclaim:

===Chinese===
阿加莎·克兰普小姐（Miss Agatha Cramp）家资阔得可以，想象力却少得可怜，于是便想把她这一辈子整个儿献给「服务」——而且是大场面的社会服务。克兰普小姐对历任女佣都体己得很，这份社会服务于是也就总朝着明确的种族群体使劲。女佣是法国人那阵，克兰普小姐办了个俱乐部，资助重建被战火犁平的法国村庄；女佣换成波兰人，她又热心起「援救饥馑波兰协会」；雇下俄国姑娘没几天，她成了俄国赈济委员会的委员。

就这样，克兰普小姐把近年的光阴全数献给了服务；如今家里换了位黑人女佣，她这一腔热忱竟没了去处。她闲了两个礼拜，闲得心浮气躁，一日坏似一日，直到她的医生终于说出她付钱买他说的话：她眼看要神经衰弱，只好卧床静养。

她歇了三天；于是，法院大道上一个爱挖苦人的太阳，让她瞧见了此前竟不曾留意的一件事：她的黑人女佣端早饭进来，不知怎的漂亮得惊人。克兰普小姐对美的眼力本不宽裕，却叫这个一直神秘地从她眼皮底下溜走的发现震住了，不由失声道：

===Original===
“Why, Linda, what’ve you done to yourself? You look so nice this morning.”

Linda stood stiff in astonishment, eventually managing what might have been construed as a reply:

“You﻿—feeling better, Miss Cramp?” The twinkle in the maid’s eye escaped her mistress.

“I believe I am, Linda. I really believe I am.” Miss Cramp stared at the girl a while, then turned her attention to the tray just placed on her lap; inspected it, looked through it absently.

“Something else, Miss Cramp?” asked Linda.

“No. This is very nice, Linda. Very nice. But don’t go. I want to talk to you. Something has just occurred to me.”

===Chinese===
「哎呀，琳达（Linda），你把自己拾掇什么了？今儿早上真好看。」

琳达直挺挺站着，又惊又讶，半晌才挤出一声勉强算作回答的话：

「您——好些了吗，克兰普小姐？」女佣眼里那点闪亮，女主人没瞧见。

「我想是好些了，琳达。我真觉得好些了。」克兰普小姐盯着姑娘看了一阵，又把心思转到刚搁上膝头的托盘上；端详端详，心不在焉地看穿了过去。

「还有什么吩咐，克兰普小姐？」琳达问。

「没了。很好，琳达。很好。不过先别走。我想跟你谈谈。我刚想到一件事。」

===Original===
It had indeed. For fifteen years Miss Cramp had been devoting her life to the service of mankind. Not until now had the startling possibility occurred to her that Negroes might be mankind, too.

The bare statement is extravagant; the fact is not. The only Negroes Miss Cramp had ever spoken to were porters, waiters, and house-servants of acquaintances. These were the only ones of whose existence she had been even remotely aware. Negroes to her had been rather ugly but serviceable fixtures, devices that happened to be alive, dull instruments of drudgery, so observed, so accepted, so used, and so forgotten. Had all the dark-skinned folk in the country been blotted out by some specific selective destruction, Miss Cramp would not have missed them in the least, would not have been glad nor sorry, would have gone serenely on unaware, tchk-tchk-ing perhaps over the newspaper account, but remaining wholly untouched in her sympathies.

Not so with remoter disasters: Over the slaughter of Armenians by Turks she had once sobbed bitterly and even over the devastation of the Japanese by earthquake she had mourned a little; because, though she had never known Armenian or Japanese, she had thought somewhat about them; though they had never approached her person, they had penetrated her intellect a little. But Negroes she had always accepted with horses, mules, and motors, and though they had brushed her shoulder, they had never actually entered her head.

But now something had occurred to her.

===Chinese===
可不正是想到了一件事。十五年来，克兰普小姐一直把生命奉献给人类的服务。直到此刻，她才赫然想到一种可能：黑人，兴许也算人类。

这话听着耸人听闻，事实却一点不假。克兰普小姐这辈子搭过话的黑人，只有搬运工、侍者和熟人家的帮佣。她也只有对这几号人的存在有过一丝一毫的知晓。黑人于她，向来是些不好看却好使的摆设，碰巧活着的物件，苦役的钝家伙——这么看惯了，这么认下了，这么用着了，也就这么忘了。全国的黑皮肤人倘若叫什么精确的选择性天灾一扫而光，克兰普小姐绝不会想他们，不喜也不悲，照旧心安理得地过日子，至多对着报纸上的消息咂咂舌头，那点同情心纹丝不动。

隔着老远的灾祸就不是这样了：土耳其人屠戮亚美尼亚人，她曾放声痛哭；日本人遭了地震，她也哀悼过一阵。因为，亚美尼亚人也好日本人也好，她虽一个也不认得，却多少想过他们；他们虽从不曾走近她身旁，却多少钻进过她的头脑。可黑人，她是连同马匹、骡子和汽车一并接收下来的；他们虽与她的肩膀擦过，却从不曾真正进过她的脑子。

可这会儿，她想到了一件事。

===Original===
“Linda you’re quite different from most﻿—er﻿—colored people, aren’t you?”

To Linda, who had no idea what “most colored people” might mean, this was a baffling question.

“I don’t know, Miss Cramp,” she said.

“I mean﻿—you know﻿—you’re﻿—I hadn’t noticed before. You’re really quite pretty.” She was experiencing the difficulties familiar to all who itch with curiosity but prefer not to be seen scratching. “You’re so light, you know.”

Linda’s lips twitched. “Why I’m not so awfully light, Miss Cramp. And plenty folks lighter than I am are far from being pretty.”

“Yes﻿—of course,” Miss Cramp considered. “Even white people. To be sure. But of course you meant﻿—er﻿—colored. But your hair now﻿—it isn’t kinky.” At once an assertion and a question.

===Chinese===
「琳达，你跟大多数——呃——有色人很不一样，是不是？」

对琳达说来，「大多数有色人」指什么，她毫无概念，这个问题也就无从捉摸。

「我说不好，克兰普小姐，」她答。

「我是说——你知道——你——我以前没留意过。你其实相当漂亮呢。」她正体验着一切好奇心发痒、又怕人瞧见自己挠痒的人所熟悉的那种为难。「你知道，你这么白。」

琳达的嘴角抽了抽。「哪里，我也没多白，克兰普小姐。再说比我白的人多着呢，也远远算不上漂亮。」

「是啊——当然，」克兰普小姐斟酌着。「白人里也有不漂亮的。可不。不过你当然是指——呃——有色人。可你这头发——不卷嘛。」一半是断言，一半是发问。

===Original===
The only answer was, “No, Miss Cramp.”

“And how can you afford to wear such nice looking things on eighteen dollars a week?”

“Well,” Linda said, “ ’course I could do better on twenty.”

Miss Cramp did not hear this, but observed, “Patent-leather pumps and a black satin dress﻿—”

“They’re cheap shoes,” Linda explained. “Just look nice ’cause they’re new. The dress I got down on Eighth Avenue for seven dollars.”

“But your skin, my dear. You might pass for a Sicilian or an Armenian.”

===Chinese===
唯一可能的回答是：「不卷，克兰普小姐。」

「还有，一礼拜十八块钱，你怎么穿得起这么体面的东西？」

「唉，」琳达说，「给二十块自然更能体面些。」

克兰普小姐没听见这句，只顾端详：「漆皮浅口鞋，黑缎子连衣裙——」

「鞋是便宜货，」琳达解释。「就是新，才显得好。裙子是第八大道买的，七块钱。」

「可你这皮肤，亲爱的。你扮西西里人、亚美尼亚人都过得去。」

===Original===
Linda was not sure about these. “I was a gypsy once in a concert,” she admitted.

“A concert?”

“At church.”

“You go to church?”

“I like to go very much.”

“Now that’s just what I wanted to ask you about. Your people are very religious creatures aren’t they?”

===Chinese===
琳达对这两个民族没有把握。「我有回在音乐会里扮过吉卜赛人，」她招认。

「音乐会？」

「教堂里的。」

「你上教堂？」

「顶爱去了。」

「这就正巧，我本来就要问你这个。你们这族人，都是顶虔诚的生灵，对吧？」

===Original===
“Well, some are and some not.”

“I thought﻿—er﻿—slavery, you know, would have made you very religious.”

“Maybe it did,” said Linda. “I wouldn’t know ’bout that.”

“But don’t you have your own hymns? Spirituals, I believe they are called.”

“Not in my church,” Linda said.

“What church is that, Linda?”

===Chinese===
「这个，有是的，有不是的。」

「我原想——呃——你知道——奴隶制，总该叫你们变得顶虔诚吧。」

「兴许是吧，」琳达说。「那我就不知道了。」

「可你们总有自家的圣歌吧？灵歌，好像是这么叫的。」

「我们教堂里不唱，」琳达说。

「那是哪座教堂，琳达？」

===Original===
“Saint Augustine’s,” said Linda. “It’s Episcopal.”

“Episcopal!” incredulously. “Why *I’m* an Episcopalian.” The tone indicated clearly that there must be some mistake.

A little devilishly, Linda smiled, but all she said was, “Is that so?”

“But you﻿—” began Miss Cramp, then reconsidered. “But you must sing spirituals. All Negroes sing spirituals, don’t they?”

Doubtfully Linda ruminated. “Why﻿—I remember some jubilee singers gave a concert of ’em once at the Parish. And I’ve been to Methodist revival meetings where they sang ’em just like jazz. We only went for fun, to see the folks get happy and shout. I’ve never heard them at my church in regular service, though.”

“Well,” said Miss Cramp. And again, “Well.” Then, “What I was getting at was﻿—do your churches make any effort to improve conditions, to render any real service to your people?”

===Chinese===
「圣奥古斯丁教堂，」琳达说。「圣公会的。」

「圣公会！」简直不能信。「怎么，*我*就是圣公会的。」这口气明明白白：这里头必有差错。

琳达笑了笑，笑得有点小促狭，嘴上却只说：「是吗？」

「可你——」克兰普小姐起了个头，又重新想过。「可你们一定唱灵歌的。黑人个个唱灵歌，不是吗？」

琳达将信将疑地寻思。「这个——我记得有一回，几个禧年歌手在教区演唱过一回。卫理公会奋兴会我也去过，他们唱灵歌唱得跟爵士乐一个样。我们去就是图个乐，看教友们欢喜得喊叫。正经礼拜的时候，我在自己教堂里倒是从没听人唱过。」

「哦，」克兰普小姐说。又说一遍，「哦。」然后道：「我本来想问的是——你们的教堂，做不做点改良境遇的事，给你们族人办点实实在在的服务？」

===Original===
“Oh, yes. We have an employment agency. They sent me to the one that sent me to you.”

“No, no, Linda.” So stupid a reply restored Miss Cramp’s self-assurance. “That is not what I mean, my dear. I mean the people that are mentally ill, the criminals, the dope-fiends, the fallen women. Do your churches try to help them?”

“I don’t think so﻿—not unless they’re members.”

“There must be some organization to do such work among your people,” Miss Cramp insisted.

“Well,” Linda suggested brightly, “maybe the same organization does it that does it among your people.”

“Of course﻿—of course. One would think so, wouldn’t one? But I haven’t come in contact with﻿—of course, I haven’t worked in colored communities﻿—”

===Chinese===
「办啊。我们有个职业介绍所。我就是它介绍到另一家、另一家又介绍到您这儿的。」

「不，不，琳达。」这么个蠢回答反倒叫克兰普小姐找回了底气。「我不是这个意思，亲爱的。我是说那些精神病的、犯罪的、抽大烟的、堕落的女人。你们的教堂救不救他们？」

「我看不救——除非他们是教友。」

「你们族人里头，总该有个什么团体专管这种事吧，」克兰普小姐不依不饶。

「这个，」琳达灵机一动，「兴许管你们族里这种事的那个团体，捎带着也管我们的。」

「当然——当然。照理该是这样，是不是？可我一直没接触过——自然，我没在有色人社区做过事——」

===Original===
After a vacuous pause, Linda said, “Maybe you mean the G.I.A.”

“G.I.A.? What’s that?”

“General Improvement Association.”

“What do they do?”

“Well, they collect a dollar a year from everybody that joins, and whenever there’s a lynching down south they take the dollar and send somebody to go look at it.”

“Whatever’s the good of that?”

===Chinese===
空落落停了半晌，琳达说：「您说的兴许是促进会吧。」

「促进会？那是什么？」

「大众促进会（General Improvement Association）。」

「他们是干什么的？」

「这个，入会的每人一年收一块钱，南边哪回有了私刑，他们就拿了这一块钱，派人去看一看。」

「这顶什么用呢？」

===Original===
“I don’t know, Miss Cramp. Seems like they just want to make sure it really happened.”

“Well. Then what do they do?”

“Well, by that time the year’s up and it’s time to collect another dollar. So they collect it.”

“Why don’t they turn their attentions to conditions here at home?” Miss Agatha wanted to know. “There must be much to be done here among you﻿—an alien, primitive people in a great, strange metropolis. Why don’t they do something here?”

“Well, nobody gets lynched here.”

The simplicity of this response did not satisfy Miss Cramp, who could never have suspected that her colored maid would dare make game of her ignorance or play upon her credulity.

===Chinese===
「我不知道，克兰普小姐。好像是，他们就想核实一下确有其事。」

「哦。那看完之后呢？」

「看完，一年也就到头了，又该收下一块了。他们就收下一块。」

「他们怎么不把心思用在自家门口的事上呢？」阿加莎小姐要问个明白。「你们这里头可做的事多着呢——一个陌生的、原始的民族，呆在一座伟大的、陌生的都市里。他们怎么不在本地做点事？」

「这个，本地没人搞私刑啊。」

这回答的干脆并不能叫克兰普小姐满意；她万万想不到，她的黑人女佣竟敢拿她的无知打趣、拿她的轻信耍弄。

===Original===
“Why I can’t understand﻿—I really can’t. Here is a situation that surely needs attention, and the people do nothing﻿—absolutely nothing﻿—about it. Lynchings﻿—of all things! When right here in New York City there are﻿—How many of you are there here, Linda?”

“Two hundred thousand, according to Father Bruce.”

“Oh, that’s an exaggeration, of course. But even if there are as many as ten thousand, a great work could be done among them. This organization you mention﻿—”

“The G.I.A.”

“Yes﻿—quite evidently needs someone to point the way. Their attention is entirely in the wrong quarter.”

“Whyn’t you help them out, Miss Cramp?”

===Chinese===
「唉我就是不明白——真不明白。眼前的局面明摆着需要人操心，这些人却什么都不做——什么、都、不做。私刑——亏他们惦记私刑！就在纽约本地，明明有——琳达，你们这里一共多少人？」

「二十万，布鲁斯神父说的。」

「哦，那自然夸大了。可哪怕真有一万，在他们中间也大有可为。你说的这个组织——」

「大众促进会。」

「是啊——显然需要有人指点指点门路。他们的心思全用岔了地方。」

「您何不拉他们一把呢，克兰普小姐？」

===Original===
“That’s just what occurred to me Linda. Exactly what occurred to me. When I saw you this morning and noticed for the first time how different you were from most colored people, I said to myself, ‘There now﻿—why can’t they all be like that?’ And I said, ‘Why they *can* be if they have the right sort of help. Some organization that could render real service, that’s just what they need!’ Then you mentioned this G.A.R.﻿—”

“G.I.A.”

“﻿—and told me of the mistake they were making, and I said, ‘There now, there is an instrument that can be turned to good use in the proper hands.’ Yes indeed, Linda, I think I *will* help them out. I really do think I will.”

“They’ll certainly appreciate it, Miss Cramp.”

“Of course﻿ ﻿… Well, that’s all, Linda. Thank you very much. Linda, bring me the phone book when you come back, won’t you? I presume they have a telephone?”

===Chinese===
「这正是我想到的，琳达。一丝不差，正是我想到的。今儿早上我一见你，头一回看出你跟大多数有色人多么两样，我就对自己说：『瞧瞧——他们怎么就不能个个都这样？』接着又说：『只要有对路的帮助，他们*能*个个都这样。得有个能办点真服务的组织，他们缺的正是这个！』后来你又提起这个G.A.R.——」

「G.I.A.。」

「——跟我说了他们走的岔路，我就说：『瞧啊，这是一件家伙，搁在对路的手里就能派上正用。』可不，琳达，我看我*是*要拉他们一把。我真觉得我要拉了。」

「他们准会感激不尽的，克兰普小姐。」

「那是自然……好了，就这些吧，琳达。多谢你了。琳达，回头把电话簿给我拿来，好不好？我估摸他们总有电话吧？」

===Original===
“Who, Miss Cramp?”

“This G.I.R. Society.”

“A telephone? I don’t know, Miss Cramp.” Linda was elaborately uncertain, eventually concluding, “They might have one. They might at that. I’ll bring the book, Miss Cramp.”

===Chinese===
「谁，克兰普小姐？」

「这个G.I.R.协会呀。」

「电话？我说不好，克兰普小姐。」琳达煞有介事地拿不准，末了拿定了主意：「兴许有。还真兴许有。我给您拿簿子去，克兰普小姐。」

### VII / 七

===Original===
There is at least one occasion a year when Manhattan Casino requires no decorations, the occasion of the General Improvement Association’s Annual Costume Ball. The guests themselves are all the decoration that is necessary.
