这时，从楼上一心盯着帕特莫尔和琳达周围那一小圈舞客的人，看见夏因赶在人堆里琳达夺路欲逃之际，硬挤到了她跟前；看见帕特莫尔抬眼一望，缩了缩，矮了半截，一时进退未决，仿佛既巴望溜走又不甘心；看见夏因和琳达双双收步，面对面站定——姑娘又急又惊，汉子绷着脸、浑身紧张——又看见她冲动地朝他扑过去，嘴里一句无声却分明是哀求的话；看见他一把接住她，把她拨到自己身后，霍然回身——帕特莫尔却已不见；又看见帕特莫尔早已脱出人堆，脚底抹油，直奔一道侧门而去。

随后只见围观的人散去，夏因同琳达并肩走开。一对对舞侣若无其事地接着跳，人流仿佛不曾受扰，重又顺流而下。

J·彭宁顿·波特包厢里诸公，齐齐长出一口大气。

“妙极了！”邓恩先生评点。

“妙极了！”邓恩太太应和。

===Original===
And after a moment, “Marvelous!” cried J. Pennington Potter, like one who at last sees the light.

---

Miss Cramp found that Nora Byle had dropped into the chair beside her, and that insistent questions in her own head clamored for utterance at this opportunity. She was however quite unprepared to make the most of this opportunity, because she had never considered that certain methods of approach might be useless. She thought she had only to ask, and it would be given.

Between members of opposed races, however, the subject of race is difficult, almost indeed delicate. Neither party quite wholly sacrifices his illusions about his own people nor admits his ignorances about the other. The conversation, therefore, becomes a series of unwitting affronts, mutual mistrusts, and suppressed indignations increasingly harder to bear, till at last it futilely breaks off with both parties ready to burst﻿—each inwardly smoldering at the other’s unforgivable ignorance and tactlessness. Here is the hedonistic paradox if anywhere, that one best learns the facts of a race by ignoring the fact of race. If Nordic and Negro wish truly to know each other, let them discuss not Negroes and Nordics; let them discuss Greek lyric poets of the fourth century, BC.

Wise observers sense this and avoid the crassly obvious. But Miss Cramp was too deeply sincere and too genuinely curious to exercise tact. She ventured the usual opening:

===Chinese===
片刻之后，“妙极了！”J·彭宁顿·波特也喊出声来，俨然一个终于开了窍的人。

---

克兰普小姐发觉诺拉·拜尔已经落座在她身旁的椅子里，而自己脑子里那些按捺不住的问题，正嚷着要趁这机会出口。然而她全不晓得怎么利用这机会，因为她从不曾想过，某些接近的法子可能全然无用。她以为只消开口问，对方必有回答。

然而在敌对种族的成员之间，种族这个话题委实难谈，简直可以说是微妙。双方谁也不肯完全牺牲对自己人的幻想，谁也不肯承认自己对对方的隔膜。于是谈话成了一连串无心冒犯、相互猜疑和越憋越难忍的怒气，直到最后徒劳地收场，双方都濒临炸开——各自在心里闷烧，怪对方那不可饶恕的无知和不识相。享乐悖论便是如此——在这里体现得再清楚不过：愈是直奔种族问题，愈是摸不着一个种族的实情。北欧人也好，黑人也好，真想彼此相知，就别谈黑人和北欧人；去谈公元前四世纪的希腊抒情诗人吧。

明眼的观客嗅得出这个，于是避开那露骨的显而易见。克兰普小姐却诚恳得太深，好奇得太真，顾不上讲究分寸。她拣了惯常的开场白，冒险一说：

===Original===
“Your people seem to enjoy themselves so.”

“They do seem to,” agreed Nora with slightly different emphasis.

“You mean they really don’t?”

“Well, some folks laugh to keep from crying, you know.”

Miss Cramp thought she saw in this a personal confession. This exquisite creature of blended blood must indeed be very unhappy. The personal implication surely invited intimacy. She said sympathetically:

===Chinese===
“你们的人好像玩得挺尽兴。”

“看着是像。”诺拉应道，重音稍稍挪了挪。

“您是说，其实不尽兴？”

“这个嘛，有些人笑，是为了不哭，您知道的。”

克兰普小姐以为从中听出了一段私人自白。这位血统交融的妙人儿，心里必定苦得很。这份推心置腹的暗示，想必正邀请着进一步的亲近。她满脸体恤地说：

===Original===
“I suppose you speak from experience, my dear. How much white blood have you?”

Nora suppressed a gasp; then said too, too gently, “I don’t quite know, Miss Cramp.” And added sweetly as if returning a greeting, “How much black have you?”

Miss Cramp did not suppress her gasp; she merely prolonged it into a sputtering little laugh and exclaimed, “What a sense of humor you have, Mrs. Byle!”

“Yes, haven’t I?”

“I was just saying to Mr. Merrit,” Miss Cramp persevered, “that so much can be done for your people. Not for you, of course. Or Mr. Potter. But the great majority. You have heard that remark of somebody’s, no doubt, that most Negroes are just three jumps ahead of the monkeys?”

===Chinese===
“我猜您是经验之谈吧，亲爱的。您身上有多少白人血统？”

诺拉把倒抽的那口气咽了回去；随即温存得过了头地说：“我可说不好，克兰普小姐。”又甜甜地补上一句，仿佛回敬问候：“您身上有多少黑人血统呢？”

克兰普小姐可没憋住那口气，只是把它拉长成一阵噗噗的小笑，惊呼道：“您可真幽默，拜尔太太！”

“可不是嘛。”

“我方才还跟梅里特先生说来着，”克兰普小姐不屈不挠，“你们的人大有可为之处。当然，不是说您。也不是波特先生。是那大多数。想必您听过哪位先生的名言：大多数黑人不过比猴子多蹦三步远？”

===Original===
“White monkeys?” smiled Nora.

“Oh, Mrs. Byle﻿—how amusing﻿—! But seriously. I think there is much to be done, don’t you?”

“Oh, yes indeed﻿—”

“I was telling Mr. Merrit about my maid, Linda. The girl we were watching down there just now﻿—I must scold her severely for that. But﻿—why, do you know, I had no idea what really marvelous servants they make. After having Linda I wouldn’t think of having any other kind of maid. I’ve had Irish and French and German, but none of them were nearly so good as Linda.”

“The best maid I ever had,” disagreed Mrs. Byle, “was a German girl.”

===Chinese===
“白猴子吗？”诺拉含笑反问。

“哎呀，拜尔太太——真逗——！不过说正经的。我认为要做的还很多，您说呢？”

“哦，正是——”

“我正跟梅里特先生讲我家女仆琳达。就是方才咱们在底下盯着看的那姑娘——回头我得狠狠训她一顿。可是——您猜怎么着，我真没想到她们做仆人能做得这么出色。有了琳达，我再不考虑雇别样的女仆了。爱尔兰的、法国的、德国的我都用过，哪一个也不及琳达一半好。”

“我用过最好的女仆，”拜尔太太唱反调道，“是个德国姑娘。”

===Original===
That Mrs. Byle should have had a maid at all seemed to come as a shock to Miss Cramp, a shock unrelieved by the casual reference to the maid’s Nordicity. “You had a﻿—a German maid?”

“Yes. A wonderful worker. But I had to let her go finally. I simply couldn’t endure her English.”

“Well﻿—” said Miss Cramp “﻿—well﻿—anyway I prefer colored girls to any of the others.”

“Perhaps because they’re American.”

“American? Oh﻿—well, yes, they are﻿—in a way.”

===Chinese===
拜尔太太竟然用过女仆，这本身就让克兰普小姐吃了一惊；而对方顺口提到那女仆还是个北欧人，更没让这惊吓减轻半分。“您用过——一个德国女仆？”

“用过。干活一把好手。可末了我还是只好辞了她。她那英语我实在受不了。”

“唔——”克兰普小姐说，“——唔——反正比起旁人来，我还是更喜欢有色姑娘。”

“兴许因为她们是美国人吧。”

“美国人？哦——这个——是啊，也算是——从某种意义上说。”

===Original===
Nora bit her lip.

“I’m so int’rested in the Negro problem, genuinely int’rested, you know,” Miss Cramp continued.

“How do you plan to solve it?”

“Well there is the labor aspect of it. As I said before they make excellent servants. Why not have more Negro servants?”

“Porters and scullions and chamber maids?”

===Chinese===
诺拉咬了咬嘴唇。

“我对黑人问题可太感兴趣了，发自肺腑地感兴趣，您知道的。”克兰普小姐接着说。

“您打算怎么解决它呢？”

“这个嘛，总有劳动这一面吧。就像我方才说的，她们做仆人出色极了。何不多用些黑人仆役呢？”

“多些搬行李的、洗碗的、收拾房间的？”

===Original===
“Exactly. It may be possible to increase the numbers of such workers.”

“I don’t see how increasing the numbers helps solve any problem.”

“Well﻿—”

“Why not try to change them over into governesses and secretaries?”

“Oh, my dear﻿—who would want a colored secretary?”

===Chinese===
“正是。兴许能叫这类工种多起来。”

“我不明白数目多了怎么就算解决问题。”

“这个——”

“怎么不想法子让她们改行去做家庭教师、当秘书呢？”

“哎呀，亲爱的——谁会要一个有色秘书呢？”

===Original===
There was an awkward silence between them which neither the beating of tom-toms nor the rain falling on banana leaves seemed to relieve. Eventually Miss Cramp said:

“You met Mr. Merrit, of course?”

“Met him?”

“Didn’t you, my dear? A fine type of American gentleman﻿—”

“Why, I’ve known Fred Merrit for years.” The familiarity in this remark struck Miss Cramp as unseemly.

===Chinese===
两人之间一阵尴尬的沉默，不管通通鼓的鼓点还是打在芭蕉叶上的雨声，似乎都解不开它。末了克兰普小姐说：

“您当然见过梅里特先生了？”

“见过他？”

“难道没有吗，亲爱的？美国绅士的楷模——”

“哪里，弗雷德·梅里特我认识好些年了。”这话里的熟稔劲儿，在克兰普小姐听来未免有失体统。

===Original===
“Yes,” remarked she. “He said he’d worked among Negroes all his life.”

Nora experienced first resentment at the implication of this supremely thoughtless comment, then, conflicting with it, amusement at the realization that Fred had evidently been masquerading at this lady’s expense.

“Is there any reason,” she said, “why he shouldn’t work all his life among his own people?”

The statement transfixed Miss Cramp like a lance, and the swift change of mien from complacency to unbelieving horror was so violent that Nora almost felt remorse at having occasioned it.

“What!” Miss Cramp managed a faint little squeal.

===Chinese===
“是嘛，”她说，“他说他在黑人中间干了一辈子。”

诺拉先是恼火于这句没心没肺的话里暗藏的意味，随即又觉出好笑，同恼火撞了个正着——弗雷德分明是在拿这位太太当冤大头，一路装神弄鬼来着。

“请问，”她说，“他一辈子在自己人中间做事，有什么不妥吗？”

这句话像一杆长矛把克兰普小姐钉在了那儿，她那神色从自得转为不敢相信的惊骇，变得又快又猛，弄得诺拉几乎后悔造出这场面。

“什么！”克兰普小姐挤出一声细弱的尖叫。

===Original===
“You weren’t under the impression that Mr. Merrit was *not* a Negro, were you?”

“Why﻿—I﻿—I didn’t know. I thought﻿—”

“I’m sure he wouldn’t have deceived you intentionally.”

“But Mrs. Byle﻿—his complexion﻿—his skin is so fair﻿—”

“Yes. He even has green eyes.”

===Chinese===
“您该不会一直以为，梅里特先生*不是*黑人吧？”

“这个——我——我不知道呀。我还以为——”

“我敢说他不是存心瞒您。”

“可是拜尔太太——他的脸色——他的皮肤那么白——”

“是啊。他连眼睛都是绿的。”

===Original===
“I should never have thought﻿—”

“You ought to have noticed his hair, ‘my dear.’ ”

“His hair?”

“It’s all that betrays him and you have to look close to see that it really is kinky.”

At this point the irate Buckram Byle made his presence felt. No one had been paying much attention to Mr. Byle. And so, as much to attract notice as to punish his wife, he now called loudly to her that he had long since indicated his intention to go home and had no idea of letting her ignore it. Nora, having topped off an excellent evening, raised no objection.

===Chinese===
“我怎么也想不到——”

“您本该留意他的头发才是，‘亲爱的’。”

“他的头发？”

“只有头发泄露他的底细，还得凑近了看，才看得出那头发真是卷的。”

这时节，怒气冲冲的巴克拉姆·拜尔偏要叫人想起他的存在。先前谁也没怎么理会拜尔先生。于是，一半为引人注目，一半为惩戒太太，他扬声唤她，说自己早就示意过要回家，断不容她装聋作哑。诺拉这个良宵已经圆满到顶，也就不持异议。

===Original===
“I must go,” she said to Miss Cramp. “It’s really so very nice knowing you﻿—er﻿—my dear﻿—”

Miss Cramp sat staring about with eyes that comprehended nothing, the turbulence in her own mind confusing every perception: eddies and currents of heads swirling about in the stream below her; constantly shifting, insane patterns of color, coming and going; wanton cries, prodigal jests, abandoned Negro laughter; and the orchestra, remotely dominant, sustaining it all with a ceaseless rhythm like the pulse of a pounding heart.

All this the mad accompaniment of a pitiless cycle of reflections:

“A Negro on Court Avenue and I asked him to call﻿—they’ll blame *me*﻿—A Negro on Court Avenue﻿—”

===Chinese===
“我得走了，”她对克兰普小姐说，“认识您实在叫人愉快——呃——亲爱的——”

克兰普小姐呆坐四顾，两眼一片茫然，心里的翻腾搅乱了全部视听：脚下溪流似的人潮里，人头旋成一个个涡漩；色彩的花样疯疯癫癫，变换不休，聚了又散；放纵的喊叫，大手笔的打趣，忘乎所以的黑人笑声；还有那支乐队，远远地主宰着一切，用永不停歇的节奏托住满场，如同一颗擂动的心房的脉搏。

这一切，疯疯癫癫地伴奏着那一轮毫不留情、循环往复的心念：

“法院大道上住进一个黑人，还是我请他来家的——人家要怪*我*——法院大道上一个黑人——”

## Jive. / 花腔

### XIII / 十三

===Original===
Despite the genial atmosphere of Pat’s pool room, the substantial good will of the table over which the varicolored ivory balls rolled, the cozy cheer of the green-shaded low-hung light, Jinx and Bubber could not discuss even the weather in agreement.

“Sho is hot,” Bubber had commented, missing a shot and wiping a glistening brow on his arm.

“Don’ blame d’ weather jes’ ’cause you can’t shoot pool,” returned Jinx. “I likes warm weather like this.”

“Can’t see what fo’.”

“Well﻿—we got to work outdoors, ain’t we?”

===Chinese===
帕特的台球厅里气氛再融洽，五彩象牙球滚过的台面上善意再足，绿灯罩低垂的光线再温馨，金克斯和布伯俩人连天气都谈不拢。

“这天是真热。”布伯错过一杆，拿胳膊抹了抹亮晶晶的脑门。

“别自个儿球打不准赖天气，”金克斯回敬，“俺就稀罕这样的暖和天。”

“看不出稀罕个啥。”

“这个嘛——咱们的活儿是在外头干的，不是？”

===Original===
“Yea﻿—in d’ heat.”

“Aw right. In warm weather you kin find some place outdoors to cool off, but when it’s cold, damn if you kin find any place outdoors to git warm.”

“Cold weather fo’ mine,” disagreed Bubber.

“Shuh!”

“Yas *suh*. We got to wear clo’es, ain’t we?”

===Chinese===
“是啊——顶着大日头。”

“这就对喽。天暖和，你在外头总能找个凉快地儿；天一冷，你他妈倒在外头找个暖和地儿给我瞧瞧。”

“俺还是投冷天一票。”布伯不服。

“切！”

“可不*是嘛*。咱总得穿衣裳，不是？”

===Original===
“Uh-huh.”

“Well, when it’s cold you kin put on enough to git warm, but when it’s hot, damn if you kin take off enough to git cool!”

Jinx pretended to ignore this unanswerable point by bending far and low over a long corner shot.

“Number eight,” he called, signifying his intention to pocket the black ball. “Sho loves to make this eight-ball﻿—jes’ like punchin’ you in d’ nose.” And he made it, cueing the ball with exaggerated vehemence.

Henry Patmore sauntered up. “Where’s yo’ boy?” he inquired.

===Chinese===
“嗯哼。”

“这就对了。天冷，你多穿点儿就暖和了；天热，你他妈脱个够，看能不能凉快！”

这一击无可辩驳，金克斯只装没听见，弯腰弓背地伏到一记长长的角袋球上。

“八号。”他叫了一声，示意要把黑球送进袋。“打这个八号球俺就爱——跟照你鼻子来一拳一样痛快。”一杆下去果然进了，出杆的狠劲儿夸张得很。

亨利·帕特莫尔踱了过来。“你那宝贝伙计呢？”他问。

===Original===
“What you mean﻿—Shine?”

“Don’ mean his brother.”

“Hell,” said Bubber. “Ain’ see ’at boogy a single night sence d’ dance.”

“Jivin’ a dickty gal now,” explained Jinx, regarding the table critically, with a sidewise twist of his head. “Bringin’ me mud.”

“Yea?” said Pat.

===Chinese===
“您是说——夏因？”

“又不是说他兄弟。”

“得了，”布伯说，“打舞会那晚起，那黑哥们我一夜也没见着影儿。”

“这会儿正跟一个装体面的姑娘花言巧语呢，”金克斯歪着头，挑剔地打量球台，“存心恶心俺。”

“是嘛。”帕特说。

===Original===
“Dickty?” scoffed Bubber. “What’s dickty ’bout ’er?”

“Ev’ything,” said Jinx preparing to try a difficult combination, “﻿—compared to him.”

“Mean the gal he picked up at the Casino th’ other night?” asked Pat.

“Don’ mean her sister,” assented Bubber. “She ain’t nobody’s dickty, though. Powerful easy to look at but jes’ ordinary K.M. right on.”

“She may be a K.M.,” conceded Jinx, “but if they’s anything ordinary ’bout her, I ain’ seen it.”

===Chinese===
“装体面？”布伯嗤笑，“她哪儿体面了？”

“哪儿哪儿都体面，”金克斯说着摆开架势，要打一记高难度的组合球，“——跟咱们那位比起来。”

“说的是他前几天晚上在游乐场搭上的那个姑娘？”帕特问。

“不是她妹还能是谁。”布伯附和。“不过她可不是谁的装体面。模样那叫一个中看，可说白了，就是顶普通一个帮佣丫头（K.M.）。”

“帮佣丫头就帮佣丫头，”金克斯让步，“可她身上要有一星半点儿普通，俺算没长眼。”

===Original===
“Got the big boy goin’, huh?” grinned Pat.

“Goin’ and comin’,” said Bubber; then to Jinx, “How long you go’n’ look at that ball, man? Go on﻿—shoot!”

“Who d’ hell’s makin’ this shot?”

“Ain’ nobody makin’ it, far as I kin see.”

Pat smiled metallically and moved off. Jinx called and shot, dispersing a cluster of balls, of which not one found its way into a pocket. Whereupon Bubber echoed their cackling laughter, revealing his stretch of bare upper gum between the two lateral stumps.

===Chinese===
“把那大块头迷住了，嗯？”帕特咧嘴一笑。

“迷得七荤八素，”布伯说；随即冲金克斯：“那球你还要瞅到啥时候，伙计？赶紧——出杆！”

“这一杆到底他妈谁打？”

“照俺看，压根儿没人打。”

帕特咧嘴一笑，金牙一闪，走开了。金克斯报球出杆，把一堆球轰得四散，愣是一个也没进袋。布伯当即跟着咯咯怪笑，笑得露出两截残牙之间那一溜光秃秃的牙床。

===Original===
“One of these times when you laff like that,” prophesied Jinx with great ill-humour, “I’m go’n’ bus’ you in d’ mouth so hard you’ll grow yo’self some teeth.”

Bubber’s scorn was superlative. “You might stick out a fis’,” he warned, “but you won’t draw nothin’ back but a nub.” He busily chalked his cue, surveying the pattern of balls with enormous gravity.

“Yo’ laigs is so bowed,” Jinx observed, “that you wear yo’ shoes out on d’ sides. Better stop laffin’ at me like that. One of these times I bet I’m go’n’ run you knock-kneed.”

“I wouldn’ run that fas’,” returned Bubber, squatting to squint over the table, “after nobody.”

“Ain’ talkin’ ’bout after﻿—talkin’ ’bout from.”

===Chinese===
“哪回你再这么笑，”金克斯恶狠狠地预言，“俺一拳把你嘴砸狠点儿，叫你自个儿长出几颗牙来。”

布伯的轻蔑登峰造极。“你只管伸出拳头来，”他警告说，“收回去的可就剩个肉墩儿了。”他忙着给球杆上粉，以泰山般的凝重审视满台球势。

“你那两条罗圈腿，”金克斯发表意见，“罗得鞋净磨外侧。趁早别那么冲俺笑。哪天俺保准撵得你满街跑，管叫你两条腿改内八字。”

“俺犯不上跑那么快，”布伯蹲下去眯眼瞄球，回敬道，“追谁也不值当。”

“俺没说追——俺说逃。”

===Original===
“From?” Bubber stood erect. “Me run from you?”

“You do have bright moments, dark as you is.”

“Brother, let me tell you sump’m. If it ever even looks like I’m runnin’ from you, they won’t be but one explanation fo’ it.” Bubber paused oratorically. “Be ’cause you done outrun me so fas’ you mos’ caught up wid me ag’in.” Wherewith he made his shot.

Jinx solemnly shook his head. “It sho’ would be awful hard,” he said.

“What?”

===Chinese===
“逃？”布伯直起身。“俺从你手下逃？”

“你黑归黑，脑筋偶尔也亮堂一回。”

“老兄，听俺说句掏心窝的。万一真有哪回，瞧那架势像是俺在从你手底下逃，那解释就只有一条。”布伯演讲似的顿了顿。“那就是你追俺追得实在太快，差一点儿又从后头赶上俺了。”说罢一杆出手。

金克斯郑重其事地摇头。“那可真是够呛，”他说。

“什么够呛？”

===Original===
“Awful hard on old man Isaacs.”

“What you talkin’ ’bout?”

“To lose two good men at once.”

“Boy, you done gone crazy?”

“No. I was jes’ thinkin’﻿—”

===Chinese===
“够呛的是老艾萨克斯。”

“你扯什么呢？”

“一下子折损两条好汉，他老人家受得了吗。”

“小子，你疯魔了？”

“没。俺就是寻思——”

===Original===
“Oh. Thass different.”

“﻿—I’m go’n’ have to kill you sooner or later﻿—only way to git along with y’. And that gal is jes’ ’bout ru’nt Shine﻿—he ain’ never go’n’ be no mo’ good.”

“Shuh!” scoffed the other. “She might scratch ’im a little, but ain’ no gal go’n’ put no deep dents in that jasper. He ain’t got no place soft enough.”

“The hell he ain’t. Know where I seen ’im goi’n’ tonight, dressed up like a monkey-back?”

“Where?”

===Chinese===
“哦。那另说。”

“——俺早晚得把你宰了——跟你处好的独一个法子。还有那姑娘，眼看就把夏因毁了——他这辈子算甭想再有出息。”

“切！”那一位嗤之以鼻。“她顶多给他挠挠痒，哪个姑娘也甭想在那块料上砸出深坑来。他身上就没一处软乎的地方。”

“他没软的地方？知道俺今晚瞧见他往哪儿去不？那通打扮，活脱一只穿燕尾服的猴儿。”

“哪儿？”

===Original===
“Seen ’im goin’ in ’at ’Piscopal church.”

Bubber stared a moment, then proceeded disgustedly with his sighting. “What d’hell you ’spect a man to believe?” he commented.

“Swear I did. Not d’ main door. You know that side door﻿—’nuther buildin’ it is, where they have dances and basketball and ev’ything else they scared to do in d’ church itself. Call it d’ immunity-house or sump’m like that.”

“Yea?” Bubber dropped his stick. So long as Shine hadn’t entered the main door of the church, the matter was credible enough to be startling.

“I sho did.”

===Chinese===
“瞧他进了那座圣公会教堂。”

布伯直勾勾盯了他一会儿，又没好气地接着瞄球。“这叫人他妈信哪个？”他咕哝。

“俺发誓真瞧见了。走的不是正门。你知道他们那个边门——另有一座楼，跳舞、打篮球，还有诸般他们不敢在教堂里干的名堂，全在那儿。管那楼叫什么‘免疫馆’来着，反正差不离的名儿。”

“真的？”布伯手里的杆都撂下了。只要夏因进的不是教堂正门，这事儿就可信得足以吓人一跳。

“千真万确。”

===Original===
Bubber slowly shook his head. “Bye-bye, blackbird.” Then, still somewhat suspicious, “Where was you when you saw ’im?”

“Followin’ ’im. Thought he might need some help if he was out sheikin’.”

“Well, kiss my Aunt Annie’s preserves!” Bubber pondered the imponderable a moment, slowly recovering his stick and most of his incredulity. “Aw, don’ be no fool. That jigaboo’s jes’ jivin’.”

“Maybe. But, same time, ain’ nuthin’ to hinder *her* from jivin’, too. And when two folks gits to jivin’ each other, first thing y’ know sump’m happens.”

“Sump’m go’n’ happen awright, but ’tain’ go’n’ happen to *him*.” Bubber resumed his survey of the balls scattered widely by Jinx’s miss. “Bet I’m go’n’ run off all the res’,” he wagered.

===Chinese===
布伯缓缓摇头。“完了完了，黑鸟儿。”随即仍有些将信将疑：“你瞅见他的时候，人搁哪儿呢？”

“跟在他后头呗。寻思他要是出去沾花惹草，兴许用得上俺帮把手。”

“乖乖，俺安妮姑妈的果酱罐子哟！”布伯对着这桩无从掂量的事掂量了片刻，慢慢捡回球杆，也捡回了大半的不信。“得了，别犯傻。那黑小子不过是耍耍嘴皮子。”

“兴许。可同时，也没啥拦着*她*不耍嘴皮子呀。俩人凑一块儿互相耍着耍着，冷不丁就出事儿。”

“事儿是笃定要出的，就是落不到*他*身上。”布伯重新检视被金克斯那一杆轰得满台都是的球。“赌不赌，剩下的俺全给收了。”

===Original===
Jinx, however, had become philosophical. “Jes’ goes to show y’, see? There’s a guy what’s so big and hard he can’t be had. Mos’ these gals ’round hyeh tries they damnedest to make him﻿—but he jes’ don’ fall. No mo’n he fell that time Spider Webb cut at him and missed and nearly got broke in two. So hard. So hard his spit bounces. Says to me﻿—say, ‘Jinx you speckled-hide awstrich you, women ain’ no different from men﻿—only worse. You gotta be tough and tight, boy. Once they see you slippin’, it’s yo’ hiney from then on﻿—they’ll put d’ locks on you and throw d’ key away. But if you be hard with ’em, they ain’ no trouble ’tall.’ Yea. And then this one come along. She’s diff’runt, see? Act all dickty ’n ev’ything. High-hats ’im. K.M. awright﻿—but not jes’ ordinary K.M.﻿—*Dickty* K.M., see? That jes’ ’bout gits ’im. He gives up without a struggle.”

“How do you know he’s give’ up?” Bubber’s doubt persisted.

“Went in d’ damn church after ’er, didn’ he?”

“That ain’t nothin’. I’ve seen women I’d go in worse places ’n that after.”

“Yea?”

===Chinese===
金克斯却又哲人起来。“这不明摆着给你上了一课，瞧见没？就有这么一号人，块头又大、骨头又硬，谁也拿他没辙。这一片的大姑娘小媳妇，哪个没使出吃奶的劲儿想拿下他——他就是不动窝。当年‘蜘蛛’韦伯（Spider Webb）照他砍一刀没砍着，反差点儿叫人家撅成两截，打那以后更甭提。多硬。硬到吐口唾沫都能蹦起来。他跟俺说——说：‘金克斯，你这块花皮鸵鸟，娘儿们跟爷们没两样——就是更邪乎。你可得又横又抠，小子。一旦叫她们瞧出你松了劲儿，打那起你的屁股就不是你的了——人家给你上了锁，钥匙一扔。可你要对她们横到底，屁事儿没有。’可不是。结果这一位来了。她不一样，明白不？一举一动全是装体面的做派。拿架子晾他。帮佣丫头是不假——可不是普普通通的帮佣丫头——是*装体面*的帮佣丫头，懂？这下他可就交代了。一枪没放就举了白旗。”

“你咋知道他交代了？”布伯还是疑。

“人都跟着她进那破教堂了，不是？”

“那算个啥。有的娘儿们，比那更邪门的地方俺都肯跟进去。”

“真的假的？”

===Original===
“No lie. And they wasn’t near as easy to gaze on as that sister, either. Dickty﻿—shuh﻿—that ain’t got nothin’ to do with it. It’s that ball-bearin’ movement, thass what.”

“Damn if it makes *him* run any smoother. One day he’s good natured as a puppy-dawg, ’nother he’s evil as a black cat. Never seen a man change so. She done put it on ’im all right.”

“Bet he go’n’ put sump’m on her, too.”

===Chinese===
“童叟无欺。而且论模样，跟那位妹子差着十万八千里。装体面——切——那顶个屁用。顶用的是她走路那副滚珠轴承似的做派。”

“可那做派愣是没让他*转*得顺当点儿。头天还跟小狗崽似的和气，转天就黑猫似的邪性。没见过一个男人变这样。她是真给他上套了。”

“赌他也得给她上一套。”

===Original===
“Damn ’f I believe it. She’ll have ’im goin’ in d’ *main* door nex’. This is serious.”

“So’s this,” said Bubber, who had meanwhile run off seven balls, unnoticed. Thereupon, mimicking perfectly, he duplicated the shot which Jinx had made earlier with such exaggerated vehemence. The ball was the last on the table, and it sped to an already full pocket eagerly, greeting its fellows with a cheerful *clack!*

Bubber looked at his victim with a grin. Jinx frowned unbelievingly at the clean green table top and, as Bubber broke into his customary guffaw, stood scowling malevolence at him, as if undecided whether to dispose of him at once or let him live a little time longer.

===Chinese===
“打死俺也不信。下回她就能牵着他走*正*门了。这事儿严重了。”

“这杆也严重。”说话间布伯不知不觉已连收七球。随即，他分毫不差地学样，把金克斯方才那记狠得夸张的球原样复刻了一遍。那是台面最后一颗球，它欢天喜地直奔早已装满的球袋，跟同伴们咔哒一声脆亮地打了个招呼。

布伯咧着嘴端详他的猎物。金克斯对着空空如也的绿台面直皱眉，难以置信；等布伯爆出他那招牌式的大笑，他便站在那儿恶狠狠地瞪他，像是拿不定主意：是当场把他料理了，还是让他再活一小会儿。

### XIV / 十四

===Original===
“Baby﻿—” began Shine.

“Don’t call me baby!” exploded Linda.

“ ’Smatter? Don’t you like children?”

“It sounds so﻿—common.”

“I couldn’ mean it that way﻿—you know that.”

===Chinese===
“宝贝儿——”夏因起了个头。

“不许叫我宝贝儿！”琳达炸了。

“咋啦？你不喜欢小孩儿？”

“这称呼听着——俗气。”

“俺可不是那个意思——这你知道。”

===Original===
“How do I know what you could mean?”

“Couldn’ ever say nothin’ common ’bout you. Couldn’ even think it. ‘Baby’s’ a nice name.”

“Think so? Well, save it for your sweetheart.”

“I did,” he grinned.

“Wrong number,” she said, but she smiled.

===Chinese===
“我怎么知道你是什么意思？”

“跟你能沾上俗气的字眼，俺一辈子说不出口。想都不敢想。‘宝贝儿’是个好名儿。”

“是嘛？那留着叫你心上人去吧。”

“已经叫上了。”他咧嘴一乐。

“打错电话喽。”她说，可自己也笑了。

===Original===
“That was my lucky day,” he mused. “What did Pat say to you that night? Why wouldn’t you ever tell me?”

Thus, while Bubber and Jinx discussed them over a pool table, Shine and Linda strolled slowly along the west walk of Riverside Drive. A few blocks east lay Harlem, black and sullen, too uncomfortable by far for dancing this hot August night, even the distant and circumspect dancing permitted in a parish hall. Nearer was Court Avenue, whither the present roundabout walk led.

Here on the Drive it was cool. Occasional meandering couples passed arm in arm, and on the long benches that rimmed the walk, facing the Hudson, still others made love, oblivious and unashamed.

“Huh?” insisted Shine.

“Nothing to tell,” murmured Linda.

===Chinese===
“那晚可是俺的福日，”他若有所思，“帕特那晚跟你说了啥？咋一直不肯告诉俺？”

布伯和金克斯在台球案边这样议论他们俩的时候，夏因和琳达正沿着里弗赛德大道（Riverside Drive）的西侧步道缓缓溜达。往东隔几个街区便是哈莱姆，黑黢黢、闷沉沉，这个八月热夜委实不宜跳舞——连教区礼堂里准许的那种若即若离、循规蹈矩的舞也不相宜。再近些便是法院大道，这一趟绕弯的散步，正是奔它去的。

大道上这边凉爽。不时有相依挽臂的伴侣弯弯绕绕走过；沿岸一线长椅面朝哈得孙河，还有一对对在那儿谈情，浑然忘我，不知羞耻为何物。

“嗯？”夏因追问。

“没什么好讲的。”琳达低声说。

===Original===
“Must be. Saw enough myself to know that.”

“What’d you see?”

“Well, I’m lookin’ for Pat myself, see? He’s jes’ pulled a crooked deal on me a minute before, and I’m askin’ for ’im. Well, you know the crowd﻿—only people you can find is them y’ ain’t lookin’ for. I’m standin’ at the foot o’ the stairs lookin’ for that gray suit. Finally I sees it ’way across the floor﻿—and damn if the sleeves ain’t ’round your waist.”

“Stop swearing﻿—”

“That sort o’ cramps my style, see? Don’ want to mix you up in anything. But I got to have some o’ Patmore. So I’m standin’ there wonderin’ what the top card is and lookin’ at you. Then I see you don’ look so good﻿—kinda like a kitten some rough kid won’t turn loose. Turnin’ y’ head this way and d’ other way and sorter pullin’ ’way from this bird even though y’ keep on dancin’. And I smoke *him* over, and he’s grinnin’ like a Chess-cat with a mouse﻿—a nice young tender mouse, see what I mean? Well, I’ve seen that grin before, and I know it like I know my landlady’s. Only, any time I see a guy grin like that before, I jes’ feel kinda sorry for ’im f’ bein’ such a sap. This time I ain’t sorry. That same grin turns me cold.”

===Chinese===
“肯定有。俺亲眼瞧见的那些，够俺明白的。”

“你瞧见什么了？”

“是这么回事，俺自个儿正找帕特呢，明白不？前脚他刚坑了俺一票，俺满场子寻他。你也知道那种场子——你找得着的，净是你不找的人。俺站在楼梯口，专瞅那身灰西装。末了在舞池那头瞅见了——他妈的，两只袖子敢情正圈在你腰上。”

“别骂人——”

“这档子事把俺的路数全搅了，懂不？不想把你卷进是非里。可帕特莫尔那笔账，俺非讨不可。俺就那么杵着，掂量他底牌是哪张，眼睛瞧着你。后来俺看你脸色不对——活像只叫野孩子薅住不肯撒手的小猫。脑袋这边躲那边躲，一边接着跳，一边一点点从那位爷手里往外挣。俺把他从头到脚盘了个遍，他咧着嘴，笑得跟叼住耗子的柴郡猫似的——一只又嫩又鲜的小耗子，懂俺意思不？这种笑俺见得多了，熟得跟俺房东太太的笑一个样。只是往常见谁这么笑，俺顶多可怜他是个冤大头。这回俺一点儿也不可怜。就那一笑，把俺笑凉了。”

===Original===
He paused so long that she urged him on. “You didn’t stay cold long.”

“No﻿—and why? Because the next thing I know you stop dancin’ right in the middle of a step and look at him like you didn’t know anybody’s breath could smell so bad﻿—”

“Oh!﻿—”

“But it don’ worry Mr. Patmore none. He jes’ pushes his face on out at y’, and makes another crack. That’s the one I want to know about, because that’s the time you jerks away from him like as if he burnt your fingers. Meantime the kacks is closin’ in and you can’t make a quick getaway. And when I come to, I’m down on the floor haulin’ it through the crowd.”

“There’s an empty bench under that tree,” discovered Linda. They sat down, deep in the shadow of foliage, and during a moment’s silence, looked out over the river. Directly opposite loomed the Palisades, like a wide and gloomy black fortress, clear-limned against a sky dimly pale with an adolescent moon. Below, the dark water glittered a smile that derided the callow moon’s wooing.

===Chinese===
他停了半天没言语，她催他往下讲。“你也没凉多久。”

“可不是——为啥？紧跟着，你一个舞步迈到半截，说停就停，看他那眼神，活像头一回知道人的口气能那么熏人——”

“哎呀！——”

“可这点儿事不碍帕特莫尔先生的。他还把脸往你跟前凑，又贫了一句。俺要知道的就是那一句，因为正是那回，你猛地从他手里挣出来，跟烫了手指头似的。这工夫那帮白佬围了上来，你想抽身也抽不成了。等俺回过神，俺已经下了舞池，在人堆里硬犁出一条道来。”

“那棵树底下有条空长椅。”琳达发现了新大陆。两人在浓荫深处坐下，静了片刻，一同望着河面。正对面耸着帕利塞兹崖，像一座宽阔阴森的黑色堡垒，衬在夜空上，轮廓分明——天上悬着一弯未圆的新月，把夜色映得朦朦发白。脚下的黑水粼粼地闪着一片笑，笑那乳臭未干的月亮的求爱。

===Original===
“Well, I don’ know jes’ what happens then,” Shine presently continued, “but when I reach for Pat, he’s breezed. Never see a man catch so much air so fas’. Then you looked like you was gonna cry and said you wanted to go home or some place﻿—so I took you.”

“I didn’t know what I was saying.”

“I did.”

“Seen ’im since?” she asked.

“No. That’s why I want the dope. When I crown ’im I want to tell ’im exactly what he’s king of.”

===Chinese===
“这个，再往后俺也说不真了，”夏因接着讲，“反正等俺伸手去抓帕特，人早没影儿了。从没见过谁吸风吸得那么快。你那会儿眼看要哭，说想回家还是找个哪儿——俺就送你了。”

“我自己都不知道说了什么。”

“俺知道。”

“后来见过他没有？”她问。

“没有。所以才要讨个实底儿。等俺往他脑壳上戴王冠的时候，得跟他说明白了，他这王当的是哪一国的王。”

===Original===
“You mustn’t bother ’im﻿—let ’im alone.”

“I got a picture o’ myself lettin’ any guy alone that gets fly with my girl.”

“Your *what*?”

“You ain’ blind.”

“Well of all the nerve!”

===Chinese===
“你别去招惹他——随他去吧。”

“‘有人对俺姑娘撒野，俺一笑而过’——俺倒真给自己画得出这么一幅画儿。”

“你的*什么*？”

“你眼没瞎。”

“天下就有脸皮这么厚的人！”

===Original===
“Hit me,” he invited contritely, exposing a rugged cheek.

“Your﻿—” She was overcome. “Well what do you know about that?”

He answered her literally. “Nothin’, but I’m willin’ to learn.”

She averted her face to hide her smile. “I couldn’t have been your﻿—anything﻿—anyway, then. Didn’t even know your name.”

“Well,” he said with elaborate innuendo, “maybe I *was* jes’ a little bit previous.”

===Chinese===
“打俺吧，”他悔罪似的递上一边糙脸，发出邀请。

“你的——”她招架不住了。“哎哟，这可真是新鲜事儿！”

他老老实实作答。“新鲜在哪儿俺不知道，可俺乐意学。”

她偏过脸去藏住笑。“再说那时候，我根本不可能是你的——什么——连你叫什么都不知道。”

“这个嘛，”他字斟句酌地暗示，“俺当时兴许*确实*超前了那么一小步。”

===Original===
“What do you mean!”

“Nothin’ lady﻿—nothin’. Don’t get so excited. I jes’ mean to say, you know my name now, thass all.”

“Well, you needn’t think﻿—”

“And now *that* storm is over, how ’bout the dope?”

“What dope?”

===Chinese===
“你什么意思！”

“没什么，女士——没什么。别激动。俺就是说，如今你知道俺叫啥了，就这个。”

“哼，你也别想——”

“眼下*那阵*风浪也过去了，实底儿呢？”

“什么实底儿？”

===Original===
“What ’d Pat say?”

She was silent a long time. The lights of a homeward bound excursion boat broke through the river’s moonlit smile, but when the ship had passed, the smile was still ironically there. Wraiths of music and laughter drifted shoreward.

“If you promise not to get in trouble over it﻿—”

“Promise anything. Spill it.”

“You know he had said there were prizes for the best costumes.”

===Chinese===
“帕特说了啥？”

她沉默了好久。一艘返程游船的灯火，划破河面上月光的笑靥；船过之后，那笑靥依旧挂在原处，带着讥诮。音乐与笑语如游丝，一缕缕飘向岸来。

“你要是答应不为这个惹祸——”

“啥都答应。快说。”

“你知道的，他说过要给最好的服装发奖。”

===Original===
“Yea﻿—and he was a judge.”

“Yes. Well, I believed it. When he came back for the second dance, he was lit. I’d asked some other folks about it﻿—”

“The Sunday School boys you was dancin’ with?”

“No! The girls I came with. I asked them about the prizes and nobody knew anything about ’em. But I wasn’t sure and I didn’t want to offend him if he was telling the truth. So instead of asking him right out, I said, ‘I thought you told me there were going to be prizes,’ just as if I’d already found out there wasn’t. And all he did was to grin with all those brass teeth of his. That made me mad, and I told him what I thought of anybody that would do anything like that﻿—and﻿—”

“Yea?”

===Chinese===
“是——他还是评委呢。”

“对。我呢，信了。他回来跳第二支舞的时候，已经喝高了。我问过旁人——”

“跟你跳的那些主日学校的男生？”

“不是！是一起来的那些姑娘。我问她们发奖的事，谁都说不知道有这回事。可我又没拿准，万一是真事，我不想得罪他。所以没当面直问，只说了句：‘我记得您跟我说过要发奖的。’装作已经打听清楚了压根儿没这回事的样子。结果他呢，就那么咧开满口铜牙冲我一笑。这下我可火了，把心里对干这种事的人的看法一古脑儿倒给他——然后——”

“然后？”

===Original===
“Well, finally when I saw he really had been lying, I stopped dancing and tried to walk off but he held me and people began to look. Then he said﻿—”

“Said what?”

“He said﻿—I needn’t act so disappointed over losin’ twenty-five dollars﻿—that he was a judge, all right﻿—and﻿—”

Her voice became low and hard. Unconsciously they drew closer together. “And what?” he said after a moment.

===Chinese===
“后来，等我看出他真是撒谎，我就不跳了，想走，他偏拽着我不放，人家都开始看了。他就说——”

“说什么？”

“他说——我犯不着为丢了二十五块钱装得那么丧气——他说他反正是评委——还说——”

她的声音低了、硬了。两人不知不觉靠得更近。“还说什么？”过了一会儿他问。

===Original===
“Well﻿—he offered me twenty-five dollars.”

Silence enfolded them, deeper than the shadow. It seemed an endless period before someone laughed in the darkness a distance away. Thereupon the leaves of the tree overhead heaved a gentle, prolonged sigh.

They sat for a long time wordless, looking across the sardonic Hudson.

===Chinese===
“这个——他掏出二十五块钱要给我。”

寂静把两人裹住，比树影更深。过了仿佛无穷无尽的工夫，远处黑暗里才有人笑了一声。头顶的树叶随之轻轻掀动，发出一声悠长的叹息。

两人久久无言，望着那条满含讥诮的哈得孙河。

### XV / 十五

===Original===
It happened the next morning that Linda ran out of sugar, discovering her predicament only a few minutes before Miss Cramp’s breakfast was to be served. There were, of course, no grocery stores within three blocks of exclusive Court Avenue, and while ordinarily Miss Cramp would have waited without complaint till the errand was run, today the situation was awkward: Miss Cramp had company, a lady from Baltimore, Maryland; a friend, to be sure, but a friend whose breakfast must not be delayed by the delinquencies of a colored maid.

Linda, therefore, following professional tradition, resolved to borrow sufficient from her next-door neighbor to tide over the temporary lack, and was already on the kitchen-porch going to the Irish girl next door, when she saw a Negro woman beating rugs in the back yard of the second house. She had her own curiosity about that particular house, because she had overheard Miss Cramp and the present guest discussing it, and she decided that this was her chance at an opening that would satisfy that curiosity. She would borrow the sugar from the colored woman.

It was thus that she made the acquaintance of Fred Merrit’s housekeeper, Mrs. Arabella Fuller.

“Drop in any time,” invited Mrs. Fuller, who was a genial, lonesome soul, not too insistent on the social distinctions between housekeepers and maids, and who would apparently have had more to say had Linda been less obviously pressed for time.

“Thanks,” smiled Linda. “This afternoon. My folks are going to a show.”

===Chinese===
第二天早上，琳达家的糖偏偏用完了，离克兰普小姐开早饭只有几分钟，她才发现这窘境。深严排外的法院大道三街区之内，自然连家杂货铺也没有；往常碰上这事，克兰普小姐尽可不慌不忙等她跑一趟腿，今天却难办：克兰普小姐有客，一位从马里兰州巴尔的摩来的太太；朋友归朋友，可这位朋友的早饭，断不能叫一个有色女仆的差池给耽搁了。

于是琳达遵照同行传统，打定主意到隔壁邻居家借一些，救这一时之急。她人已上了厨房外的廊子，正要去隔壁那爱尔兰姑娘家，一眼瞧见第二户后院里有个黑女人在拍打地毯。那所宅子她本就好奇，因为克兰普小姐同眼下这位客人议论过它的话，都叫她听了去；她认定，眼下正是个开口的好机会，好奇可以得个满足。糖就找这位黑女人借。

就这样，她结识了弗雷德·梅里特的管家，阿拉贝拉·富勒太太（Mrs. Arabella Fuller）。

“闲了随时过来坐啊，”富勒太太相邀。她是个热心的寂寞人儿，对管家与女仆之间的门第之别不太讲究；若是琳达不那么一看便知赶时间，她显然还有说不完的话。

“谢谢，”琳达笑道，“今儿下午就来。我们主人家要看戏去。”

===Original===
And so that afternoon found her and Mrs. Fuller conversing in the Merrit kitchen with all the ease and confidence of a much more extended friendship.

Without conscious effort Mrs. Arabella Fuller would have arrested any cartoonist’s attention. Her profile was a series of adjoining semicircles﻿—a large one for the bulbous forehead, then a succession of smaller, approximately equal ones, forming from above downward nose, upper lip, lower lip, first chin, and second chin. From above downward, moreover, this series slanted unanimously rearward, so that the forehead bulged and the chins receded, and the general attitude was that of one caught in the act of swallowing half a banana.

This profile only stated the motif on which Mrs. Fuller as a whole was composed. Every outline of every part was a perfect semicircle, and so on integration she naturally became a cluster of hemispheres. There were, to be sure, unanticipatedly sudden constrictions about her at points: between chins, for example, at wrists, at waist, and at ankles. But these repressions were futile, for on either side of each constriction the flesh triumphantly bulged. They simply heightened the lady’s agglomerate bulbosity.

Out of the midst of this there escaped on occasion, an asthmatic wheeze. This confab was such an occasion, and it revealed at once that the asthma in no wise discouraged Mrs. Fuller’s flow of language.

“Yes, indeed, chile. Any time you want anything like that jes’ come right on over and get it﻿—we always has plenty ev’ything on hand. Thass one thing about Mr. Merrit﻿—he sho believes in eatin’. Reckon thass why he so thin. And it makes him mad as a wet hen to run out’n anything and thass why I always has plenty ev’ything on hand. So anytime you run out, jes’ come on over and I’ll trust you to keep ’count o’ ev’ything you get.” She fanned her shining round brown face with a limp dishcloth and smiled as she paused for breath. The smile revealed a shining row of white teeth, each of them just half a circle.

===Chinese===
于是那天下午，梅里特家的厨房里，两人谈得投机，那份从容和知心，俨然是多年老交情了。

阿拉贝拉·富勒太太这张脸，随便哪个漫画家见了，都不费吹灰之力就来神。她的侧影是一串首尾相衔的半圆——秃起的前额占一个大号，往下再依次排一列小些的、大小相近的：鼻子、上唇、下唇、第一下巴、第二下巴。而且这一列自上而下齐心协力向后斜，于是前额前凸，下巴后缩，通身的姿态，活像一个吞了半根香蕉、当场给人拿住的倒霉蛋。

这道侧影不过是道出了富勒太太周身的主题。她每一部位的每一道轮廓线都是标准的半圆，积分起来，整个人自然而然成了一簇半球。诚然，她身上某些点位出现了出人意料的骤然收窄：比如两个下巴之间、手腕、腰、脚踝。可惜这些压制一概劳而无功，因为每道箍的两侧，肉照旧凯旋般地鼓凸而出。这只会把她老人家浑圆的球感衬托得愈加高耸。

从这一堆浑圆之中，间或逸出一声气喘似的哮鸣。这一场畅谈正是这样一个场合，而且它立刻证明：哮喘丝毫浇不灭富勒太太的谈兴。

“可不是嘛，孩子。往后缺个啥，只管过来拿——俺们家啥都存得足足的。梅里特先生有一样好——他可真信奉吃这门学问。俺琢磨这就是他那么瘦的缘故。家里短了哪样他都气得跟落汤鸡似的，所以俺才啥都备得足足的。往后你短了啥只管来，你拿了啥，俺信你能一笔一笔记着。”她拿一块蔫塌塌的洗碗布扇着油亮的圆脸盘，歇气儿的当口笑了一笑。这一笑，露出亮晶晶一排白牙，每颗都恰好是半个圆。

===Original===
“It’s nice here,” Linda observed, looking about.

“ ’Deed it is. And Mr. Merrit’s such a nice man to work fo’. ’Cose he have his big times and so on, and he like his toddy now’n then a little too good, and ev’y once in a while he gets tied up with some woman ’nother, but ’cose thass natural, him bein’ a bachelor and havin’ so much money. I jes’ shets my eyes and says nothin’, ’cause ’tain’t none my business, and he ain’ never said nothin’ out the way to *me*, y’ understand, so I jes’ do my work and go on. You know how ’tis﻿—you mus’ see and don’ see.” There was another reluctant pause.

“Indeed so,” agreed Linda, already somewhat apprehensive at the conflict in Mrs. Fuller’s speech. It appeared that while Mrs. Fuller’s labored respiration sought to shorten her sentences, her sentences had a will of their own and simply refused to be shortened. Linda already found herself drawing deep sympathetic, but wholly useless breaths.

“ ’Cose there’s a lot o’ folks what don’ like to work fo’ colored, I understand that, and I don’ know as I would myse’f if it had to be some these uppity colored women what ain’ never been used to nothin’ and jes’ now got sump’n and think they so much mo’ n’ eve’ybody else. Take fo’ ’n instant that Sarah Bell Long, what’s always ridin’ ’round in Cornelia Bond’s auto. I knowed her when she was a baby﻿—knowed her father and mother before ’er. Neither one of ’em wasn’t nothin’. Ole man Bell run a saloon in Augusta till he made enough to buy up half the black belt; then he retired, got religious, gave d’ church a lot o’ money and got hisse’f preached into d’ kingdom and his wife along with ’im. Then this Sarah gal married this young doctor﻿—least, he was then﻿—and set him up in business, and when they got tired livin’ down there ’cause some them women liked his treatment too well, why they up and comes to New York. And havin’ plenty money natchelly they starts right out at the top. But I always say the top ain’ but a little way from the bottom﻿—can’t be﻿—’tain’ been risin’ long enough. And ain’ none of us so much better’n the rest of us that we can afford to get uppity ’bout it﻿—And thass why I jes’ couldn’ stand workin’ ’round nobody that act that way. Ain’ no sense in it. But Mr. Fred ain’ like that. Ain’ nobody in Harlem got no better things ’n Mr. Fred is, and some them things up in the country he brought back with ’im all the way from Europe and France and them places ’way yonder ’cross the water. You’ll see ’em when they get hyeh﻿—he always have ’em sent in town fo’ the winter. An ain’ nobody in New York got nothin’ no better, but it don’t turn his head none. Look like he jes’ buy things to spend his money on and when he get ’em that ends it. All ’ceptin’ one thing﻿—a picture of his mother. Least, I think it mus’ be his mother, though he ain’ never tole me so, but he stands and looks at that picture fo’ hours at a time, seems like. I b’lieve ’twould near ’bout kill ’im to lose it. But he sho’ is a nice man to work fo’﻿—don’ never bother you ’bout nothin’.”

Linda decided it would be less exhausting to do some of the talking herself. She hastened to inject at this pause, “I should think it would be nice, working for a man, anyhow. Bet he isn’t fussy ’n’ everything like an old woman﻿—specially an old maid. Gee!”

===Chinese===
“这儿真不赖，”琳达环顾着四周说。

“可不真的嘛。梅里特先生又是顶好伺候的东家。他自然有他闹腾的时候，酒瘾上来也贪那么两口，隔三差五还跟这个那个的女人缠上一腿，可这都天性使然，谁叫他打光棍又有的是钱呢。俺只当没瞧见，啥也不说，反正不关俺的事，他也从没对*俺*说过一句出格的话，你懂吧，所以俺干完活儿该干啥干啥。这行里的规矩你懂——眼里瞧见，只当没瞧见。”说到这儿她又恋恋不舍地歇了口气。

“可不是嘛，”琳达应着，心里已对富勒太太话里那场拉锯暗暗叫苦。看情形，富勒太太费力的呼吸一心要把句子剪短，她的句子却自有主张，死活不肯就范。琳达发现自己已在跟着深吸气，满腔同情，却全然无济于事。

“这个嘛，不乐意伺候黑人的确实有——有那么一号人，俺明白，俺自个儿也未必肯，要是东家换成年鼻孔朝天、没见过世面的有色女人——穷得叮当响才几天，就当自个儿比谁都金贵。就说那个萨拉·贝尔·朗（Sarah Bell Long），成天坐科尼莉亚·邦德家的汽车兜风那位。她还在襁褓里俺就认得——她爹她娘俺认得比她还早。老两口哪有什么根基。老贝尔在奥古斯塔（Augusta）开酒馆，攒够了钱，半条黑土地带都买到手；然后收山归隐，信了教，往教堂里大把捐钱，硬是叫牧师把自己讲道讲进了天国，顺手把老婆也捎了进去。跟着这位萨拉姑娘嫁了个年轻大夫——那会儿还年轻——出钱给他开张。两口子在下头住腻了——因为那地方有些娘儿们太稀罕他的‘治疗’——就收拾收拾搬来了纽约。有的是钱，自然一落脚就直奔上流。可俺总说，顶上离底下没几步——不可能远——垫起来的年月还太浅。咱们谁也没比谁金贵到哪儿去，摆不起那个谱——俺就是这号脾气，受不得跟摆谱的人一处做事。没道理。可弗雷德先生不是那号人。全哈莱姆再没谁家的东西比弗雷德先生家的更讲究，有的还是他打欧洲啊法兰西啊那些老远水那头的地方带回来的，如今搁在乡下宅子里，天冷进城就运回来，你到时候就瞧见了。纽约城里也没谁家有更讲究的，可他一点儿不烧包。瞧那架势，他买东西就为把钱花出去，到手就算完了。就除一样——他娘的一张画像。至少俺想那准是他娘，他自己可从没说过；可他能对着那张像一站站上几个钟头。俺看真要丢了，能要了他半条命。不过他可真是位好东家——从不为着啥事难为你。”

琳达盘算着，与其干听她讲，自己分担点儿还省点力气。趁这个气口她赶紧插进话去：“要我说，伺候男东家总是舒坦的。保准不像老婆子那样事事挑剔——尤其老姑娘。啧啧！”

===Original===
“Yo’ madam ain’ never had a husband of her own?”

“Uh-uh.”

“How come she ain’t?”

“Guess she never knew whether a man wanted her or her money.”

“What diff’runce that make?”

===Chinese===
“你家太太就没嫁过人？”

“没。”

“咋会呢？”

“我猜她始终闹不清，男人图的是她这个人还是她的钱。”

“那有啥两样？”

===Original===
“Well, I guess she figured if he *did* want it, she didn’t want *him*, and if he *didn’t* want it, there must be something wrong with him. That just made the whole thing sorter hopeless.”

“She nice to work fo’?”

Linda saw that the way to prevent Mrs. Fuller from talking herself to death was to keep her asking questions. “Well,” she answered, “she could be worse. Nicest part is she lives all alone and that makes the work light. But she get sick over the least little thing and she spends a lot o’ time in bed. She just got over a three weeks’ spell yesterday﻿—only reason she got up was because this friend from Baltimore was coming last night. You can’t imagine what made her sick this time.”

“Is this visitor a gen’leman friend?”

“Nope.”

===Chinese===
“这个嘛，我琢磨她是这么想的：他要真图钱，那她就不要他；他要连钱都不图，那这人准有毛病。这么一来，整件事就有点儿没指望了。”

“她好伺候吗？”

琳达看出来了，要不想叫富勒太太把话说断了气，就得让她不停地问。“这个嘛，”她答，“再挑理也有限。最好的是她独门独院住着，活儿轻省。可她动不动就病，一大半工夫泡在床上。昨儿才熬过一场三个礼拜的大病——要不是巴尔的摩这位朋友昨晚要来，她还躺着呢。她这回病的由头，你猜都猜不着。”

“来的是位先生朋友？”

“不是。”

===Original===
“Then what?” Linda could sense that Mrs. Fuller was merely nosing for an opening through which she could break for a long unobstructed run of speech.

“She found out that your boss was a jig, and it put her in bed for three weeks. I didn’t know what the trouble was till last night and I heard her talking to this Baltimore woman. The way she’s carrying on you’d think the house had turned to a hospital for smallpox. ’Deed it wouldn’t surprise me to see it burnt down any time.”

“What you mean, chile?”

“Well, you know how much fays like to have jigs move in next door to ’em.”

“Deed I do. I remember years ago﻿—”

===Chinese===
“那病打哪儿来的？”琳达听得出来，富勒太太不过在嗅探突破口，好撒开蹄子来一场无遮无拦的长篇大论。

“她查明你家东家是个黑人，一病就是三个礼拜。病因我昨晚才弄清楚，听见她跟这位巴尔的摩太太念叨来着。瞧她那如临大敌的架势，还当这宅子改成天花了呢。要我说，这房子哪天一把火烧了，我都不带奇怪的。”

“这话咋讲，孩子？”

“你又不是不知道，隔壁搬进来个黑人，白人有多‘欢迎’。”

“可不是。俺记得多少年前——”

===Original===
“Specially if it’s a nice neighborhood. They’d do most anything to get ’em out. Look at what they did to that man in Staten Island last fall. Ku-Kluxed him. It was even in the fay papers, how they burnt the man’s house down while he was out. I believe Miss Cramp is wild enough to do the selfsame thing﻿—or have it done.”

“Have it done﻿—how you mean?”

“*Pay* somebody to do it.”

“No!”

“Bet she’d offer to pay *you* to do it.”

===Chinese===
“街坊体面，就更了不得。撵人的损招他们样样使得出来。瞧瞧去年秋天斯坦顿岛那位先生的遭遇。三K党整了他。白人的报纸都登了，趁他出门把人家房子点了。要我说，克兰普小姐那股疯劲儿，一模一样的事她自个儿也干得出——要不就花钱叫人干。”

“花钱叫人干——咋讲？”

“*雇*人干呗。”

“不会吧！”

“赌她连*你*都肯雇。”

===Original===
“And I bet I’d smack her from hyeh to yonder, too!”

“Well, there’s plenty of fay toughs around here﻿—not right on this street but near enough﻿—and I bet she could get somebody to get *them*. Then she wouldn’t be suspected. Everybody’d think it was like that house on 149th Street somebody put dynamite under.”

“What!”

“Didn’t you read about it?”

“No!”

===Chinese===
“那俺也赌，一巴掌把她扇到十万八千里外！”

“这一带白人的地痞流氓有的是——本街上没有，可也隔不了几步——我打赌她雇得着人去支使那帮家伙。这样她自个儿就摘干净了。人人都会当那是像149街那所宅子一样，叫人埋了炸药给掀的。”

“什么！”

“没看报吗？”

“没有！”

===Original===
“It was in *The Black Issue*﻿—oh, a long time ago now. Man bought a house on 149th Street and they dared him to move in. Sent letters and all. But he went on in. And less’n a week after he moved in, they blew him out﻿—bajooey!﻿—just like that.”

“Well I never in all my life!”

“Deed they did. And Miss Cramp is worried and mad and everything. You ought to ’ve heard her last night talking to this southern woman.”

Linda decidedly had the floor now and she did not intend to relinquish it.

“She’s from some little dump in Maryland, but she swears she lives in Baltimore﻿—as if even that was anything to brag about. She’s just like Miss Cramp, only more so. Well, you know one time Miss Cramp asked me a lot o’ dumb questions about shines and I gave her a lot o’ dumb answers and she went and joined the G.I.A. to find out for herself. And for doing it!”

===Chinese===
“登在《黑人问题》（The Black Issue）上——哦，都过去好久了。有位先生在149街买了房，他们放话不许他搬。恐吓信什么的都寄了。他偏搬了进去。进去不到一个礼拜，人家就把他连人带房崩上了天——轰隆！——就那么一下。”

“活了半辈子，头一回听说！”

“千真万确就是这么干的。克兰普小姐急的、气的，五味俱全。昨晚她跟这位南方太太那通念叨，你真该听听。”

这会儿话头确凿无疑攥在琳达手里，她也不打算撒手了。

“她是马里兰哪个小破地方出来的，偏咬定自己住巴尔的摩——好像巴尔的摩就多了不起似的。她跟克兰普小姐一个模子，还更上一层。这个嘛，你知道，有一回克兰普小姐就黑人这事问了我一堆傻话，我也就回了一堆傻话，结果她倒好，亲自跑去加入了大众促进会，要弄个水落石出。也为着这一趟，落下了病！”

===Original===
“Say what?”

“Last night she was telling this other one all about it, and I mean they just carried on. Miss Cramp says, ‘My dear, I’m in the most awful trouble﻿—you simply must tell me what to do.’

“This other one is the funniest thing﻿—talks like a jig fresh from down home. First time I ever heard a fay talk like a shine﻿—I was never so surprised﻿—She says, ‘Deed, honey, with all yo’ money Ah cain’t imagine what could worry you.’

“Then Miss Cramp says, ‘If something isn’t done pretty quick this whole neighborhood’s going black!’

“ ‘What?’ says this Mrs. Parmalee﻿—that’s the other one.

===Chinese===
“说啥？”

“昨晚她把这一路的底细全倒给这位太太了，那场面，真叫一个热闹。克兰普小姐说：‘亲爱的，我惹上天大的麻烦了——你可得给我拿个主意。’

“这位太太才叫一绝——一口刚打南边乡下来的黑人口音。白人说话像黑人的，我头一回领教——惊得我够呛——她说：‘可不是嘛，宝贝儿，守着这么大一份家当，俺想破脑袋也想不出还有啥能愁着你。’

“接着克兰普小姐说：‘再不想点办法，这一整条街都要黑掉了！’

“‘啥？’这位帕马利太太（Mrs. Parmalee）说——就是那另一位。”

===Original===
“ ‘And that isn’t the worst of it,’ Miss Cramp sniffles. ‘The worst of it is that *I’ll* get the *blame* for it.’

“ ‘You’ll get the blame fo’ it?’

“ ‘I’m not responsible, really. But I got int’rested in the welfare of Negroes and joined a mixed organization for the improvement of conditions among them, you know. Well, naturally, I had to go about among them﻿—’

“ ‘Ah’ve always tole yuh yo’ cha’ity’d get y’ in trouble.’

“ ‘Well it certainly has. I went, on a friend’s advice too, to see how they acted in their own surroundings and there were both white and colored people in the box with me﻿—’

===Chinese===
“‘这还不算最糟的，’克兰普小姐抽抽搭搭，‘最糟的是，这笔账要算到*我*头上。’

“‘算到你头上？’

“‘论理儿不赖我，真的。可我关心起了黑人的福祉，加入了个黑白混合的团体，为的是改善他们的处境，你知道的。这一来，自然免不了要在他们中间走动走动——’

“‘俺早跟你念叨过，你那善心早晚会惹祸。’

“‘可不是惹下了。我听了人劝，去瞧瞧他们在自家地界是什么做派，包厢里白人黑人都有——’

===Original===
“ ‘What!’

“ ‘And one of them was the man that has bought a house almost next *door* to me here on Court Avenue﻿—and Irene, he intends to *live* in it!’

“And Irene says, just like a jig for the world﻿—‘Well, bless mah soul!’

“ ‘But my dear,’ says Miss Cramp, ‘that isn’t the worst of it. You can’t imagine. My dear, I asked him to call!’

“ ‘You what!’

===Chinese===
“‘什么！’

“‘其中一位，就是在法院大道买了房子、几乎就在我*隔壁*的那个人——艾琳，他要*住*进去！’

“艾琳那句答话，活脱脱一个黑人腔——‘哎哟，我的老天爷！’

“‘可是亲爱的，’克兰普小姐说，‘这还不算最糟的。你想象不到。亲爱的，我还请他来家里做客了！’

“‘你说啥！’

===Original===
“ ‘I thought he was white. He looked like it. He’s blonder than I am.’

“ ‘How’d you find out he wasn’t?’ says Irene.

“ ‘Someone else told me after he’d gone.’

“ ‘Well, Agatha,’ says Irene, ‘if you didn’t have no better sense’n to invite a strange man to call﻿—’

“ ‘But he was so nice, Irene﻿—’

===Chinese===
“‘我当他是白人呢。瞧着就像。他比我还白净。’

“‘那你怎么发现他不是的？’艾琳说。”

“‘他走后旁人告诉我的。’

“‘我说，阿加莎，’艾琳道，‘连个素不相识的男人你都糊涂到请人家上门——’

“‘可他人多好啊，艾琳——’

===Original===
“ ‘Agatha!’ ”

“ ‘I mean﻿—you wouldn’t have suspected, yourself. And, Irene, he swore he was coming, too.’ ”

“ ‘You don’ mean you ackshally think he will?’ ”

“ ‘Why won’t he?’ ”

“ ‘A nigger ought to know better.’ ”

===Chinese===
“‘阿加莎！’

“‘我是说——换了你，你也没起疑。再说，艾琳，他还赌咒发誓说来着呢。’

“‘你不是真当他会来吧？’

“‘凭什么不来？’

“‘黑鬼就该识相点儿。’

===Original===
“ ‘Well, this is New York, you know.’ ”

“ ‘Ah don’ care what this is﻿—’ ”

“ ‘Anyway, suppose neighbors of mine see my name on the literature of this organization. As soon as this man moves in, I’ll be accused.’ ”

“Then Mrs. Parmalee looks real evil and says, ‘He wouldn’t move in down in Balt’mo’ City, I bet y’.’ ”

“ ‘He will here though,’ Miss Cramp says. ‘And if he does, I declare I’ll move out. I couldn’t bear the shame.’

===Chinese===
“‘可是，这里是纽约啊。’

“‘纽约不纽约的，俺不管——’

“‘再说，万一我的街坊在这团体的文件上瞧见了我的名字。等这人一搬进来，人家就该归罪我了。’

“这时帕马利太太脸色一狠，说：‘换了巴尔的摩城，量他不敢搬，俺跟你赌。’

“‘这儿他可是铁了心的，’克兰普小姐说，‘他真搬来，我发誓我就搬走。这脸我没处搁。’

===Original===
“ ‘Thought you so anxious t’ uplift ’em?’ Irene says, and I nearly split.

“ ‘Well,’ answers Agatha, ‘it’s one thing to help them and quite another to live beside them as equals. And to have everyone in the street blaming me﻿—I simply couldn’t bear it.’

“ ‘Mean to move?’

“ ‘What else﻿—?’

“ ‘Move all these hyeh beautiful old things you’ve accumulated and yo’ daddy befo’ yuh? Leave this house he left yuh, where you’ve lived all yo’ life? Mean to jes’ get up and walk out and do nothin’ else at all?’

===Chinese===
“‘不是急着要提升他们来着？’艾琳这一句，差点儿没把我笑岔气。”

“‘这个嘛，’阿加莎答，‘帮他们是一回事，跟他们平起平坐做街坊是另一回事。再说满街的人都要怪我——我实在受不了。’

“‘当真要搬？’

“‘不搬还能怎样——？’

“‘把这些攒了一辈子的好东西全挪走？连你爹传你的也挪？撇下他留给你的宅子，你住了一辈子的宅子？就这么拍拍屁股一走了之，啥也不干？’

===Original===
“ ‘But that’s why I’m telling you, Irene. What else *can* I do?’

“ ‘Ah’ll tell yuh what else you can do. You can﻿—’ Then she stops a minute and says in a lower voice, ‘That maid o’ yours likely to be eavesdroppin’﻿—?’

“So of course then I had to catch air. Certainly wish I knew what she told her to do.”

The oppression of Mrs. Fuller’s compulsory silence together with the emotions excited by what she had heard by this time had her in the throes of dyspnea. She panted and gasped while Linda paused to look on with curiosity and some alarm. The girl’s apprehension cost her the floor.

“Know what you ought to do?” Mrs. Fuller managed to get in; to which there was but one thing to say:

===Chinese===
“‘所以我才找你商量啊，艾琳。我*还能*怎么办？’

“‘俺告诉你你还能怎么办。你可以——’说到这儿她顿了顿，压低了嗓子，‘你家那女仆，该不会在偷听吧——？’

“我当然赶紧作势喘气去了。她出的到底什么主意，真想弄个明白。”

强忍了半天不许说话的憋闷，加上听来的这些话激起的情绪，这会儿一齐发作，把富勒太太逼进了喘不上气的苦境。她大喘气、倒抽气，琳达停下来，又好奇又有点发慌地望着她。姑娘这一分神，话头又丢了。

“知道你该干啥不？”富勒太太总算抢进一句；对此能答的只有一句：

===Original===
“What?”

“You ought to refuse to stay in that woman’s house another minute. You ought to up and leave.”

“And go where?”

“Ain’t you got﻿—” Mrs. Fuller stopped short, struck with a notion. The notion flowered into an idea. She grinned a half-moon grin, scalloped with tiny lesser half-moons, drew breath prodigiously, and delivered herself:

“Chile, I’m go’n’ need a maid right hyeh. I done told Mr. Merrit already, and he say soon’s he come in town it’d be all right. Y’see we been livin’ in a ’partment all along and ’twasn’t but six rooms and I could take care of everything with a little day help, but now with all this house it’s go’n’ be too much for one pair of hands to tend too much fo’ me and I don’ feel none too good nohow so Mr. Merrit say it’ll be fine and to get a good girl and make sho’ she ain’t too ugly ’cause he didn’ want his stomach turned, and bless my soul if I ain’t forgot all about it till this very minute. Now if you ain’ got no objection to workin’ fo’ y’own people, he’s a fine man to work fo’ and’ll never give you no trouble﻿—least, not about yo’ work. ’Cose you kinda pretty fo’ a maid, but I reckon you can take good care o’ yo’se’f, and anyhow he’s a gen’leman. So hyeh’s a job ready and waitin’ fo’ you if you want it.”

===Chinese===
“该干啥？”

“你一分钟也不该再待在那女人家。就该起身走人。”

“走去哪儿？”

“你莫非没有——”富勒太太猛地刹住，心里给一个念头撞了一下。那念头旋即开花，结成了主意。她咧开一弯新月似的笑——新月边上还镶着一圈小小的月牙——猛吸一大口气，郑重发表：

“孩子，俺这儿正缺个女仆呢。俺跟梅里特先生早提过了，他说等他一进城这就定下来。你晓得，俺们一直住公寓，统共六个房间，雇个零工帮衬着也就拾掇过来了；如今这么大一所宅子，一双手哪里顾得过来，俺这身子骨也一天不如一天，梅里特先生说这样最好，叫俺寻个好姑娘，还叮嘱模样可别太俊，免得他倒胃口——瞧俺这记性，不到这会儿全忘没影儿了。你要是不嫌弃伺候自家人，他可是位好东家，绝不给你气受——至少干活的事上不受。你这模样当女仆是俊了点儿，可俺量你自己有本事护得住自己，何况他到底是位体面人。这不，活儿现成在这儿等你，就看你接不接。”

===Original===
“How much?” said Linda. “I’m getting eighteen﻿—that’s pretty good, you know.”

“Shuh, chile, he’d give you twenty﻿—jes’ to be givin’ you mo’n you been gettin’. He pays me twenty-five﻿—and says it’s a heap cheaper’n marryin’, but I jes’ tells ’im he needn’ hint at me like that ’cause they’s some things he couldn’ pay me to do﻿—”

“Twenty dollars!”

“Sho, chile. All I got to do is tell ’im﻿—”

Linda jumped up. “You mean it?”

===Chinese===
“多少钱？”琳达说。“我如今拿十八块——这就算不低了，你晓得。”

“小意思，孩子，他给你二十——就为叫你比原先拿得多。他一月给我二十五呢——还说这可比娶媳妇便宜老鼻子了。俺就跟他说，这种话你少跟俺递点子，有些事儿，你出多大价钱俺也不干——”

“二十块！”

“真的，孩子。俺只需跟他言语一声——”

琳达蹦了起来。“当真？”

===Original===
“Mean ev’ry word of it and you’d have lots mo’ time to yo’se’f, too.”

“Honest? Do you think﻿—” An old ambition raised its head﻿—“Do you think maybe I could go to night school sometimes and learn to run a typewriter?”

This time Mrs. Fuller stopped breathing altogether. “Do which?”

“I don’t want to be a K.M. all my life.”

“Aimin’ to better yo’se’f, huh?”

===Chinese===
“一个字都是真的，你自个儿的工夫也多出一大把。”

“当真？那你说——”一个旧日的念想抬起了头——“你说，兴许我还能时不时上个夜校，学学摆弄打字机？”

这回富勒太太干脆彻底断了气儿似的。“摆弄个啥？”

“我可不想一辈子当个帮佣丫头。”

“打算往上奔，嗯？”

===Original===
Linda was afraid she had made the wrong move here, but it was too late to change. She nodded with exaggerated vigor.

“Glory be!” was Mrs. Fuller’s surprising comment. “Glad to see it, chile, glad to see it. Does me good to see one our young girls what wants to better herse’f. Our girls ain’t got no ambition, no ambition ’tall, ’ceptin’ to go on the stage or dance in a cabaret or some such thing as that.”

There followed a lengthy dissertation on the laziness of “our” girls, to which Linda listened, eagerly impatient. Finally Mrs. Fuller perorated with:

“Deed, chile, that’s fine and I’m glad to see it and I’ll help you all I can﻿—you can get off mos’ every night﻿—and I bet Mr. Fred’ll give you all the encouragement in the world﻿—and maybe one them typewritin’ things to boot. Well, want to try it? You can start soon’s he get back. How ’bout it?”

“How ’bout it?” Linda exulted. “How ’bout it?﻿—Oh boy!”

===Chinese===
琳达生怕这一步走岔了，可改口已经来不及。她加倍用力地点头。

“荣耀归主！”富勒太太的评语出人意料。“看着高兴，孩子，看着高兴。咱们年轻姑娘里头有想着往上奔的，俺瞧着就痛快。咱们姑娘们没个志气，一星半点儿都没有，一门心思不是登台就是进夜总会跳舞，反正就那一类营生。”

接着是一场关于“咱们”姑娘们有多懒的宏篇大论，琳达听着，急不可耐又满心盼着。末了富勒太太以华彩段落收梢：

“真的，孩子，这可真好，俺瞧着高兴，能帮的俺都帮——差不多天天晚上都能腾给你——弗雷德先生保准竭尽全力给你打气——搞不好连打字的家伙什都送你一台。咋样，想试不？他一回来就能上工。成不成？”

“成不成？”琳达心花怒放。“还成不成？——哎哟喂！”

### XVI / 十六

===Original===
While he couldn’t compare it with the Lafayette Theater of course, still Joshua Jones considered it a pretty good show. At least it would have been if the dumbbells hadn’t jumped up and down so often.

It began with music, a chorus singing far away behind the audience﻿—outside the church, it seemed. The singing came nearer and entered at the rear, and Shine obeyed the impulse to turn and look; but before he could determine what the trick in it was, Linda pinched his arm sharply and brought him about, puzzled and resentful, to see her shaking her bowed head with ill-concealed vigor. Thereupon he noticed that everyone else stood like Linda, motionless, with lowered head, as if it wasn’t proper to look; and he wondered what manner of performance this was, which one might attend, but on which one might not gaze.

Into his surreptitious sidewise vision first came two kids carrying enormous lighted candles. The kids wore black bordered white robes and seemed to have an awfully hard time waiting for the rest of the procession to catch up. Then came the leading man, distinguished by his sedate bearing and singular position, also in a flowing white robe; Shine saw the lean face with its sharp profile and pallid skin and concluded that this guy didn’t much enjoy his job.

There passed, following the leading man, a countless succession of increasingly taller couples, all in robes, all singing lustily without ever once consulting the books they carried before them: not much of a chorus, since the costumes made it almost impossible to distinguish the chorines from the chorats. Good singing though, funny, slow, no pep, but something about it﻿—

Eventually they all found their places up front. There followed fifteen minutes of many and mysterious diversions: The two kids playing a game with the candles﻿—lighting a lot more candles arched over the stage, seeing who could light the most. That ended in a draw. The leading man singing a solo with the whole chorus coming to his rescue every time he paused for breath or seemed to falter. The leading man was all right, but he sure couldn’t sing. More singing﻿—this was better﻿—with the audience joining tardily in. Much jumping up and down on the part of everybody. And now the taking up of admission﻿—Shine exhibited a quarter to Linda questioningly. She nodded and presently he dropped the coin into the proffered box, murmuring﻿—“Well, y’can’t go wrong for a quarter.”

===Chinese===
当然，这戏没法跟拉法耶特剧院（Lafayette Theater）比，不过约书亚·琼斯觉得，还算不赖。至少，要不是那帮傻瓜动不动就起立坐下，本来还算得上一场好戏。

戏是从音乐开的场，一支唱诗班在观众身后老远的地方唱起来——听着像是在教堂外头。歌声渐渐近了，从后堂进来，夏因一时忍不住想回头瞧瞧；可他还没琢磨出这里头有什么门道，琳达就狠狠掐了他胳膊一把，把他拧回身来——他又纳闷又憋气——只见她低着头，掩也掩不住地使劲摇。他这才留意到，旁人个个都同琳达一样站着，一动不动，垂着脑袋，好像往那边看不合规矩；他好生纳闷：这是哪门子戏文，只许来看，不许来瞧？

头一个进入他贼溜溜的余光里的，是两个扛着老大的点着蜡烛的小孩。小孩们穿镶黑边的白袍，等队伍后头的人跟上来，等得那叫一个费劲。接着出场的是领衔主角，凭他那持重的仪态和独一无二的位置一望便知，同样一袭垂地白袍；夏因瞧见那张瘦脸，侧影刀削似的，皮色苍白，断定这家伙不大喜欢自个儿的营生。

跟着领衔主角鱼贯而过的，是一队接一对数不清的伴侣，一对比一对高，个个穿袍子，个个放声高唱，从没低头看过一眼捧在前头的歌本：算不得像样的唱诗班，因为这身行头，几乎分不出哪位是“队姐”、哪位是“队哥”。唱得倒是真好，逗趣，绵缓，不带劲儿，可那调调里有点什么——

末了众人在前排各归各位。接下来是十五分钟花样繁多的神秘节目：两个小孩拿蜡烛做游戏——把拱在台口上方的一大排蜡烛都点着，比谁点得多。比了个平手。领衔主角唱了一段独唱，每回他换气或是听着要走音，全体唱诗班便齐齐赶来救驾。领衔主角人是不赖，可唱歌是真不行。又是一通唱——这回强些——观众慢半拍地跟了上去。人人反复起立又坐下。跟着是收门票——夏因把一枚两毛五的硬币递给琳达瞧，眼里带着问号。她点头，他随即把硬币丢进递上来的募捐盒，嘴里咕哝着——“两毛五，总归亏不到哪儿去。”

===Original===
This marked the end of the first act. The leading man rose in his place at one side of the stage and began to talk. His deep-set black eyes seemed to fasten themselves on Shine, who soon found himself watching and listening intently. If ever as a youngster he had heard this tale at the Orphanage Sunday School, it had been in so different a guise that now it appeared brand new.

He told it to Jinx and Bubber later, and told it with great accuracy:

---

“It was all about a bird named Joshua﻿—a great battler some years back. A general, see? Led his own army, and how! This bunch could lick anything that marked time, see?

“Well, this Joshua thought he was the owl’s bowels, till one day he run up against a town named Jericho. Town﻿—This place was a flock o’ towns. It was the same thing to that part o’ the country that New York is to this. It was the works. Without it the rest of the outfit jes’ simply couldn’t go ’round.

===Chinese===
至此第一幕落定。领衔主角在舞台一侧的位子上站起来，开始讲话。他那双深陷的黑眼睛，像是径直钉在了夏因身上；不多时，夏因发觉自己看得入神、听得入神。这故事他小时候纵然在孤儿院主日学校听过，那会儿包装得也全然两样，如今听着竟像全新的一样。

后来他把这故事讲给金克斯和布伯听，讲得头头是道，分毫不差：

---

“说的是一位名叫约书亚的好汉——前些年的一位沙场大豪杰。将军，懂吧？自带兵马，还是亲率的！天底下凡会列队踏步的，没有他打不趴下的，明白不？

“这个嘛，这位约书亚自认为了不得，自认是猫头鹰的五脏六腑——顶了天了——直到有一天，撞上一座叫耶利哥的城。城？——那可是一窝城。在那一带地方，它就是这儿的老纽约。全套家当都攒在它身上。缺了它，其余那些凑数的压根儿转不动。

===Original===
“But try and get in. This burg has walls around it so thick that the gals could have their jazz-houses on top﻿—not a bad idea at all: if a tight Oscar held out on ’em, they could jes’ let him out on the wrong side o’ the wall. And here this red hot papa, Joshua, who’s never had his damper turned down yet﻿—here he is up against that much wall﻿—and the damn thing don’t budge.

“Now comes the castor oil﻿—the part that’s hard to swaller but that does you good if y’ do. Joshua asks the Lord what the hell to do about this wall. And the Lord says, ‘Josh, you’re my boy, see? You do jes’ what I tell you and them walls’ll fall so hard they’ll make a hole in the ground.’ ‘Spill it,’ agrees Josh, and the schemes is un-schum.

“Take it or leave it, this crack army o’ Joshua’s don’t do a damn thing but walk around that wall once a day for a week﻿—Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday too. Jes’ walk around, blowin’ horns. On Sunday they walk around seven times and on the last go ’round, the way they blow on them horns is too bad, Jim. Sounds like a flock of steamboats lost in a fog.

“Then every son-of-a-gun and his brother unhitches a hell of a whoop﻿—and﻿—take it or leave it﻿—that wall comes tumblin’ down same as if it was trained. Dynamite couldn’t a done it no better. The birds on the inside have been laffin’ at Joshua for a week﻿—damn fool tryin’ to blow a wall down, tootin’ a few horns. The brass-band army. Huh﻿—but now they ain’t even got time to pull up their pants, and what happens to their hinies is a sad, sad story, no lie.”

---

===Chinese===
“可你想进去，没门儿。这城的城墙厚到顶上都能开爵士乐窑子——这主意其实不赖：哪个月不谐的客官敢赖账，她们把他往墙外头一撂就得了。这边厢，红彤彤一条好汉约书亚，火力从没调小过——偏偏迎面撞上这么一大垛墙——那玩意儿愣是纹丝不动。

“接下来上蓖麻油——难咽归难咽，咽下去保你受益。约书亚问主，这堵墙他妈的到底怎么弄。主说：‘乔什，你是我的人，明白不？你一字不差照我说的办，那墙保准砸得狠到地面上砸出个窟窿。’‘您讲。’乔什应下，于是锦囊妙计倾囊而出。

“信不信由你，约书亚这支王牌军整整一个礼拜，一天绕那墙一圈，别的屁事不干——礼拜一、礼拜二、礼拜三、礼拜四、礼拜五、礼拜六，一天不落。光绕圈，吹号。到礼拜天，绕了七圈，末一圈那号吹得，吉姆啊，凶得没边儿。动静跟一整队轮船在大雾里迷了航似的。

“跟着，挨个的龟儿子携着他兄弟，放开嗓子吼出惊天动地的一声——信不信由你——那墙轰隆隆自己塌了，跟排演过似的。炸药来也办不了这么漂亮。墙里头那帮鸟人足足笑了一个礼拜——傻帽儿，想吹塌一堵墙，就凭吹几把号。铜管乐队大军。哼——可这会儿他们连提裤子的工夫都没了，他们屁股的遭遇那叫一个惨、惨、惨，童叟无欺。”

---

===Original===
But Shine did not repeat what Tod Bruce said from this point on. Enough to admit that you’d been in a church, without further confessing a genuine interest in the meaning of a sermon﻿—especially if the meaning was a little too deep for you anyhow.

---

Bruce spoke quietly, without show but with impassioned conviction; and though many of his hearers no more grasped his message than did Shine, there was none who felt the same when Bruce ended as when he began. His honesty and sincerity were contagious and the very defects in his imperfect analogy revealed a convincing absence of artifice, a contempt for trifling disparities, an impressive disregard of minor obstacles in conveying a major idea.

“Many a man laughs,” said he, his voice penetrating like his eyes, “at the preposterousness of this Hebrew fairytale. Some of you perhaps are laughing now. For your sake I am going to say something that a minister of the Gospel is not expected to say. I am going to say this: that I don’t care the least little bit whether this thing ever happened or not. To us it does not matter. Consider it a Jewish legend﻿—a parable of Paradise, if you will﻿—a myth, without any basis of factual truth. Even so, the spiritual value of the story looms and remains tremendous.

“You, my friend, are Joshua. You have advanced through a life of battle. Your enemies have fallen before you. On you march till a certain day that sooner or later comes to us all. And then you find yourself face to face with a solid blank wall﻿—a wall beyond which lies the only goal that matters﻿—the land of promise.

===Chinese===
不过从这儿往后，托德·布鲁斯（Tod Bruce）说了什么，夏因就没有复述。肯招认自己进过一回教堂，已经够意思了；再招认自己当真关心一篇布讲的含义——那就过了，何况那含义本来对你还深了那么一点儿。

---

布鲁斯讲得平静，不事张扬，却句句滚烫；听讲的人里，纵有许多同夏因一样没能听懂他的要旨，可从布鲁斯开口到收尾，没有一个人的心里还是原样。他的诚实与恳切感染全场，就连他那番类比里不够严整的破绽，也恰恰显出一种令人信服的无矫饰，一种对细枝错漏的蔑视，一种为传一个大意思而不恤小障碍的气派，教人动容。

“多少人讥笑，”他说道，嗓音同目光一样穿透人，“这个希伯来童话荒诞不经。诸位此刻说不定就有人在笑。为诸位着想，我要说一句福音牧师不该说的话。我要说：这件事究竟发生过没有，我一点儿、一丁点儿也不在乎。于我们，这不打紧。你们只管当它一段犹太传说——愿意的话，当它一则乐园寓言——一个神话，没有任何史实根据。即便如此，这个故事的精神价值依然巍然耸立，重逾千钧。

“你，我的朋友，就是约书亚。你从百战的人生里一路杀来。你的仇敌纷纷倒在你面前。你继续进军，直到那样一个日子——或早或晚，人人都有。于是你发现，自己面对面撞上一堵结结实实、光秃无门的墙——墙那头，躺着唯一要紧的归宿——应许之地。

===Original===
“Do you know what that goal is? It is the knowledge of man’s own self. Do you know what that blank wall is? It is the self-illusion which circumstance has thrown around a man’s own self. And so he thinks himself a giant when in reality he is a child, or considers himself a weakling when truly he is strong, or more often judges himself the one or the other when he is actually both. There are still subtler contrasts: he may consider himself irreligious when at heart he is devout. Atheists and agnostics﻿—this may be heresy, but it’s true﻿—are likely to be the most profoundly religious of all men, and clergymen, with whom it is all so routine, the least. A man may think he is black when he is white; boast that he is evil and merciless and hard when all this is but a crust, shielding and hiding a spirit that is kindly, compassionate, and gentle; may pledge himself to a religion when he is by nature a pagan, thus robbing himself and his generation of all that might come out of honest self-expression.

“There is no better advice, I think, than that of the ruffian on the street, whose motto is ‘Don’t kid yourself.’ But we can’t help kidding ourselves sometimes, and we almost always kid ourselves about our Self. And what is our Self, our knowledge of ourself, if not Jericho﻿—chief city of every man’s spiritual Canaan? And how can we strip off illusion and take possession of our own soul save by battle? No man knows himself till he comes to an impasse; to some strange set of conditions that reveals to him his ignorance of the workings of his spirit; to some disrupting impact that shatters the wall of self-illusion. This, I believe, is the greatest spiritual battle of a man’s life, the battle with his own idea of himself.

“Far more incredible than this tale of the Israelite warrior are the circumstances under which you and I engage in a similar battle today. It is easier to believe, I think, that the blast of rams’ horns and the shouting of a mob could cause a stone wall to crumble than that you and I should hope to find ourselves﻿—to take our Jericho﻿—by some brief event that shatters in a moment what self-deception has built up only over the course of years. But it is true. It is not only possible﻿—it must happen to all who would see things as they are. Self-revelation is the supreme experience, the chief victory, of a man’s life. In all the realm of the spirit, in all the Canaan of the soul, no conquest yields so miraculous a reward.

“I urge you therefore to besiege yourselves; to take honest counsel with the little fraction of God, of Truth, that dwells in us all. To follow the counsel of that Truth and beset the wall of self-deception. So will towering illusion tumble. So will you straightway enter triumphant into the promised land.”

===Chinese===
“你知道那归宿是什么吗？是人认识他自己。你知道那堵光墙是什么吗？是境遇罩在人自身外面的自欺之墙。于是他自以为是巨人，实则是个孩童；或自认是弱者，实则强健有力；更多的时候，本是两者兼有，他却只认其一。还有更细微的错位：他自认不敬神，心底实则虔信。无神论者与不可知论者——这话许是异端，却是实情——倒往往是一切人中最深刻地信着神的；而牧师们，事事成了例行程式，反倒信得最浅。有人明明是白的，却把自己想成黑的；有人自夸狠毒、无情、心硬，其实这一切不过一层硬壳，护着、盖着底下那慈和、悲悯、温柔的心性；有人生来是个异教徒，偏把自己许给一种宗教，也就坑了自己、坑了他这一辈人，坑了诚实的自我表达所能生出的一切。

“依我看，天下最好的忠告，莫过于街角那混混儿的座右铭：‘别糊弄自己。’可我们有时免不了糊弄自己，而且十有八九，糊弄的正是我们的自我。而我们的自我、我们对自身的认识，不正是耶利哥——每个人灵性迦南的都城吗？不经过战斗，人如何剥去幻象、收复自己的灵魂？人到不了绝地，便认不得自己；不到某种奇诡的境地突然向他揭出，他对自家心性的运转何其无知；不到某种天崩地裂的一击，击碎那堵自欺的墙。我相信，这就是一个人一生最大的灵性之战——与他心目中的那个自己交战。

“比起这位以色列勇士的传说，你我今日投入同一场战斗的种种情由，才更加难以置信。要我信羊角号齐鸣、万众呐喊就能震塌一堵石墙，倒还容易些；要我信你我竟能指望凭某件短短一瞬的事，一举击碎自欺经年累月才垒起的一切，从而找到自我——攻下各自的耶利哥——这才难。然而这是真的。这不仅可能——而且凡愿看清事物本来面目的人，都必得经历它。自我启示是人一生的至高经历、首席凯旋。在灵性的全部疆域里，在灵魂的整个迦南，再没有哪一场征服，报偿如此神异。

“所以我劝诸位：围攻你们自己；同人人心中都住着的那一小点神性、那一小点真理，坦诚计议。听从那真理的计议，围困那堵自欺之墙。于是高塔般的幻象必将坍塌。于是你们必将即刻奏凯，长驱直入应许之地。”

### XVII / 十七

===Original===
A casual visitor to Seventh Avenue that bright Sunday noontime might have thought, on seeing the released congregations, that many had already entered triumphant into the promised land.
This weekly promenade is characterized not only by an extravagant and competitive elegance but also by an all pervading air of criticism. Hither come self-satisfied, varicolored flocks from every fold in Harlem, to mingle and browse, to inspect and sniff, to display and observe and censure.
It must be explained that of Manhattan’s two most famous streets, neither Broadway nor Fifth Avenue reaches Harlem in proper guise. Fifth Avenue reverts to a jungle trail, trod almost exclusively by primitive man; while Broadway, seeing its fellow’s fate, veers off to the west as it travels north, avoiding the dark kingdom from afar. A futile dodge, since the continued westward spread of the kingdom threatens to force the sidestepping Broadway any moment into the Hudson; but, for the present, successful escape.
And so Seventh Avenue, most versatile of thoroughfares, becomes Harlem’s Broadway during the week and its Fifth Avenue on Sunday; remains for six days a walk for deliberate shoppers, a lane for tumultuous traffic, the avenue of a thousand enterprises and the scene of a thousand hairbreadth escapes; remains for six nights a carnival, bright with the lights of theaters and night clubs, alive with darting cabs, with couples moving from house party to cabaret, with loiterers idling and ogling on the curb, with music wafted from mysterious sources, with gay talk and loud Afric laughter. Then comes Sunday, and for a few hours Seventh Avenue becomes the highway to heaven; reflects that air of quiet, satisfied self-righteousness peculiar to chronic churchgoers. Indeed, even Fifth Avenue on Easter never quite attains to this; practice makes perfect, and Harlem’s Seventh Avenue boasts fifty-two Easters a year.
Shine and Linda, released from church with the others, might have overheard much critical comment as they walked along Seventh Avenue:

===Chinese===
那个晴朗的星期日晌午，若有生人偶然逛到第七大道，一见散场涌出的人群，恐怕会以为他们中间已有许多人得胜进入了应许之地。
这条每周一度的巡游大道，特色不只在铺张斗艳的排场，更在弥漫四方的评头论足之气。哈莱姆各个羊圈里那些自我感觉良好的、五颜六色的羊群，都汇集到此，掺和着，游荡着，打量着，嗅探着，炫耀着，观望着，挑剔着。
这儿得交代一句：曼哈顿最有名的两条街，百老汇和第五大道，谁也没能体体面面地走进哈莱姆。第五大道一过界便退化成一条丛林小径，踩在上头的几乎清一色是原始人；百老汇眼见同伴的下场，一路往北走便赶紧往西拐，远远绕开这黑色王国。这一闪身躲得纯属徒劳，因为王国不断向西扩张，眼看随时会把这条侧身避让的百老汇挤进哈德孙河里去；不过就眼前而论，这一逃总算成功。
于是第七大道这条最多才多艺的大街，平日里当哈莱姆的百老汇，到星期天便当它的第五大道；一连六天，它是从容购物者的步道，是车马喧嚣的通衢，是千百桩买卖的大道，也是千百次死里逃生的现场；一连六夜，它是一场狂欢，剧院和夜总会灯火通明，出租汽车窜来窜去，一对对男女从家庭派对赶往卡巴莱，游手好闲之辈在路边懒洋洋地晃着、色眯眯地瞧着，音乐不知从什么犄角旮旯幽幽飘来，还有快活的谈笑和响亮的非洲式大笑。然后星期天一到，有那么几个钟头，第七大道变成了通天大道，泛起那种老牌教堂常客特有的安安静静、心满意足的自义之气。说真的，就连复活节的第五大道也远不及此；熟能生巧嘛，哈莱姆的第七大道一年少说也有五十二个复活节。
夏因和琳达随着大伙儿从教堂里放出来，一路沿着第七大道走，本可以听到不少这样挑剔的议论：

“Hot you, babu﻿—!”
“﻿—’co’se it’s a homemade dress﻿—can’t you see that crooked hem?”
“Wonder where the fire sale was?”
“Whut is these young folks comin’ to﻿—dat gal’s dress ain’ nuthin’ but a sash!”
“Now you know a man that black ain’t got no business in no white linen suit﻿—”

"如今的年轻人都是往哪儿去哟——那丫头的裙子跟条腰带差不离！"
"你还不明白吗，那么黑的男人压根儿没资格穿白亚麻西装——"

===Original===
But Shine and Linda had issues of their own to decide.
“How’d you like it?” she asked.
“He’s a smart guy, that dude,” Shine passed judgment. “After he got through tellin’ ’bout that bird, Joshua, I didn’t know what the﻿—what it was all about. Where’s he get that stuff ’bout knowin’ y’self? How’s a guy go’n’ help knowin’ hisself? What’s the grand secret?”
“It’s easy,” said Linda. “ ’Spose a girl thinks she likes a fellow. Likes him better than anyone else. Then s’pose somebody else comes along and she falls head over heels in love with *him*. Well, see? She didn’t know herself the first time.”
He grinned. “Who was the guy ahead o’ me?”
And she answered with merry eyes, “There wasn’t any. You’re the first one. I’m talking ’bout the one that’ll come next.”

===Chinese===
不过夏因和琳达自有正事要商量。
"你觉得讲得怎么样？"她问。
"那家伙机灵是机灵，"夏因下了判语，"可他把那个叫约书亚的家伙讲完之后，我都不知道那什么——不知道他到底讲的什么玩意儿。他哪儿弄来的什么"认识你自己"？一个人还能不认识自己不成？这里头有什么了不得的秘密？"
"这还不容易，"琳达说，"打个比方，一个姑娘自以为喜欢上了某个小伙子，喜欢得谁都比不上。再打个比方，又来了一个人，她却昏天黑地爱上了*他*。这不就明白了？头一回，她就不认识她自己。"
他咧嘴一笑。"排在我前头的那位是谁？"
她眉眼含笑地答道："压根儿没有。你是头一个。我说的是往后会来的那一位。"

“It’s all that ever done me any good.”
“*Did.*”
“I mean did.”
“Well, why don’t you say what you mean?”

"到头来对咱有过好处的，也就这一样。"

She thinks she’s the hardheartedest Hannah that ever poured water on a drownin’ man. But she ain’t. Naw. Say, she’s soft as a baby.”

"是吗？"

In astonishment he stopped to stare at her. The expression of mingled amusement, decision, and tenderness with which she returned his look gave him a sudden overwhelming happiness. It almost upset him.

===Chinese===
"我才不懂呢。就算懂，这差事我也照接。"
